The "Outline of Evidence" is primarily designed for use as a ready reference for bar review study. 

The material is arranged to follow the Evidence lectures in the BAR STUDY GROUP Course and the book is chiefly intended for use in conjunction with such lectures. 

Brooklyn, New York

April 1, 1940.

CHAPTER I. 

. 

I. Definitions: 

A. Evidence: The term includes all the means by which any alleged matter of fact under investigation is proved or disproved.

B. Proof: The result of evidence. Albany Co. Bank, 149 

N. Y. 171.

C. Direct Evidence: That which tends to prove the fact under investigation without the necessity of proving other facts (e.g. eyewitness testimony). 

1. Corpus Delicti: 

a. Under C. Cr. Proc. 395: There can be no conviction on defendant's confession alone. Independent proof of the commission of the crime charged is necessary. Peo. v. Roach, 215 N. Y. 592; Peo. v. Nentarz, 266 N. Y.

S. 757. Such independent proof need not be positive or direct but may be circumstantial. (In a felony murder prosecution, defendant's voluntary confession of the homicide is sufficient to sustain his conviction as long as there is corroborating evidence of the homicide. 

The independent felony need not be corroborated. Peo. v. 

Lytton, 257 N. Y. 310.) b. Under P. L. 1041: In criminal prosecutions for homicide, corpus delicti (i. e. the death of the victim) must be proved by direct evidence (production of deceased's body showing violent death through non-accidental means, or by direct evidence as to the act of violence accounting for the nonproduction of the deceased's body). Defendant's guilt may then be proved by circumstantial or indirect evidence. 

D. Circumstantial (Indirect) Evidence: Evidence proba tive of facts other than those under investigation but which are commonly known to have a close association with the facts sought to be proved and from which the latter may be inferred.

E. Competent Evidence: That which the very nature of the thing to be proved requires, such as the production of a writing whose contents are the subject of inquiry. 

F. Satisfactory or Sufficient Evidence: That amount of evidence sufficient to satisfy the unprejudiced mind and

conscience of a common man and so to convince him that he would venture to act upon that conviction in his own affairs. Questions as to competency and admissibility of evidence are preliminary questions for the court to decide; the question of the sufficiency of the evidence is invariably for the jury. (Is there sufficient evidence to send to the jury?—that is a preliminary question for the court to decide. Cf. C.P.A. 457a; C. Cr. 

Proc. 410.)

G. Relevant and Irrelevant Evidence: Evidence is relevant when it affects disputed issues of fact. It is irrelevant when it does not affect them and does not tend to create a belief as to their existence or non-existence (e.g. evidence of the character of parties to a civil action is generally inadmissible). Taylor, 150 A. D. 509.

H. Material and Immaterial Evidence: Material evidence is relevant evidence, i.e. logically persuasive as to a controverted issue. Immaterial evidence is irrelevant, unrelated evidence. An objection to evidence as being either "immaterial," or else, "irrelevant" is generally held a specific objection since both terms indicate the same thing, viz. the evidence objected to is not germane to the issues. Groh's Sons, 177 N. Y. 8. But an objection that evidence is "incompetent" is not specific since evidence (or the witness who offers it) may be incompetent for any one of numerous reasons. Atkins, 45 

N. Y. 753.

I. Real Evidence: That acquired by the court from observation and inspection of a thing or object put in evidence.

J. Personal Evidence: That evidence furnished to the court by personal testimony. 

K. Fact: "A thing done, reality, not supposition, action, deed." Lackey, 10 How. Pr. 155. It may be visible or invisible, tangible or intangible, physical or a mere fancy, or thought of the mind, or emotion. Thus, that 

X said something, heard, felt or saw something, or had a certain opinion, intention or emotion, are facts. 

II. A. Judicial Notice: Judicial notice or knowledge means

the notice or knowledge which a judge, or court, will officially take of a fact so as to dispense with the necessity of offering proof of it. Judicial knowledge must be the knowledge of the judge as a judge as distinguished from his personal or private knowledge. Purdy, 162 

N. Y. 42.

B. Matters Judicially Noticed: 

1. Generally; Test: The general consensus of the decisions is that judicial notice will be taken of all facts of universal notoriety (Brown v. Piper, 91 U. S. 37), or which are known by all persons of common intelligence. Wynehamer v. Peo., 13 N. Y. 378. 

2. Matters of Law: 

a. Common Law: Common law courts, when sitting at law, judicially know the rules and principles of equity, and, when sitting in equity, know the rules of civil and criminal law. State courts of the American Union also know the common law of England and that the common law was in force there at the time of the American revolution. Stokes, 62 Barb. 145. 

b. Statutory Law: Besides taking judicial notice of the common law of the forum, a state court will also take judicial notice of the public statutes of its own state. L. I. RR. Co., 199 N. Y. 288. 

1. Federal Statutes: All courts (federal and state) judi cially know the federal constitution, public statutes and the executive orders of the president. Ingersoll- 

Rand Co., 195 A. D. 838. 

2. Private Statutes: Such statutes of the state, or private acts of Congress, will not be judicially noticed (M. E. 

Union Church, 19 N. Y. 482) unless such statute or act contains some section which is deemed public or required by some statute or by the constitution to be so deemed. Bretz, 4 Abb. Pr. N. S. 

c. Sister State Laws: Unless so required by stat ute, courts of one state will not judicially notice the common or statutory law of a sister state. 

Galland, 172 N. Y. S. 775. d. Foreign Law: State courts will not take judicial notice of the laws of a foreign country. Douglass, 138 N. Y. S. 912. However, it will be judicially

noticed that the courts of Upper Canada proceed according to common law principles (Lazier, 26 

N. Y. 146), and that the unwritten law of France is not the same as the common law of England. 

Matter of Hall, 70 N. Y. S. 406. 

1. Proving the Foreign Law: A printed copy of a stat ute, or other written law of a foreign state or country or a printed copy of a proclamation, edict, decree or ordinance by the executive power thereof, contained in a book or publication purporting or proved to have been published by the authority thereof, or proved to be commonly admitted as evidence of the existing law in the judicial tribunals thereof, is admissible as presumptive evidence of such statute, law, proclamation, etc. The official books of reports of cases adjudged in the judicial tribunals thereof, are admissible as presumptive evidence of the unwritten law of that state or country. (A mere citation of the foreign decision is insufficient. De Maio, 68 A. D. 167.) The testimony of competent witnesses (usually members of the bar of the foreign jurisdiction) is also admissible to prove the foreign unwritten law. The foreign law is to be determined by the court, and in such determination neither the trial court nor the appellate court is limited to the evidence produced by the parties but may consult any of the written authorities beforementioned as if these had been admitted in evidence. 

C.P.A. 391. e. International Law: Courts judicially know the ratified rules of international law and also which foreign governments are sovereign. Sokaloff, 239 

N. Y. 158. 

1. Treaties: These are the supreme law of the land, all courts take judicial notice of their existence and provisions. Schultz Co., 164 

N. Y. S. 454. 

f. Municipal Ordinances: In New York, courts as a rule, will not take judicial notice of municipal ordinances, by-laws, resolutions or regulations of local boards or councils. Peo. v. Traina, 92 Misc. 

82. (See C.P.A. 388 as to method of proof.) 

g. Federal Courts: All federal courts take judicial notice of the federal constitution and of the public laws of each state and of the Dist. of Col.

Lane, 217 Fed. 237. But on an appeal from the highest court of a state to the U. S. Supreme 

Court, the latter court will judicially notice only the laws judicially known in the court from which the appeal was taken. Hanley, 116 U. S. 1. 

3. Matters of Fact: 

a. Qualities and Properties of Matter: Will be judi cially noticed, as, for example, that coal oil is inflammable (Bennett, 81 N. Y. 273); that natural gas is explosive (Jameson, 128 Ind. 555); that whiskey, gin and brandy are intoxicating (Blatz, 116 N. Y. 450); that it is alcohol that makes them so (Peo. v. Polluch, 224 N. Y. 648); and that lager beer is a malt liquor. Peo. v. O'Reilly, 114 

N. Y. S. 258. But whether beer is intoxicating is a question of fact for the jury. Blatz, supra. b. Course and Laws of Nature: The alternation of day and night, the seasonal changes, the changes in animal and vegetable life (Brown, 91 U. S. 37), and the time of the rising and setting of the sun or moon (Montenes, 77 A. D. 493) will be judicially noticed. (As to the last, any reputable almanac, is admissible to refresh recollection of court.) 

c. Arts and Sciences: 

1. Medicine: That typhoid is a contagious dis ease (Peo. v. Hamilton, 177 N. Y. S. 222); that vaccination is generally believed to be a preventative of smallpox (Viemeister, 179 

N. Y. 235); that sudden mental shock due to an accident may cause a person's hair to suddenly turn gray (Shaw, 257 N. Y. 193); that the period of gestation is 280 days (Wells, 221 

N. Y. S. 714); that the Mendelian Law underlies blood-grouping tests (Re Swahn, 158 Misc. 

17) but not that a pathometer (lie-detector) is or is not effective to determine truth. Peo. v. 

Forte, 279 N. Y. 204. 

2. Mechanics: The principle of the ice cream freezer (Wells, 47 N. H. 235); that even new automobiles get out of running order (Stude-

baker Corp., 199 N. Y. S. 190); the mechanics of the voting machine. Re Robinton, 128

Misc. 163. 

3. Photography: The principles controlling pho tography and the chemical processes used therein. Cowley v. Peo., 83 N. Y. 464. 

4. Literature: Facts within the knowledge of average, well read people as to matters of literature (St. Hubert Guild, 118 N. Y. S. 582); that Gautier's "Madamoiselle de Maupin" has been widely sold. Halsey, 234 N. Y. 1. d. Religion: The general character and distinctive features of religious denominations (Shaffer, 190 

N. Y. S. 841); but not the jurisdiction and powers of a particular denomination, or its provisions as to its internal affairs or matters of administration. 

Baxter, 155 N. Y. 83; Youngs, 31 Barb. 49. e. Mind and Moral Nature: The more obvious manifestations of the human mind, as, for example, that there is a strong human instinct of selfpreservation (Reynolds, 58 N. Y. 248), and a general repugnance to death (Rowland, 139 N. Y. 

93); that an illiterate person usually has a retentive memory (Matter of Cross, 32 N. Y. S. 933); and that a pile of steel beams is an attractive nuisance to children (Boylhart, 270 N. Y. 217); but not that operators of vehicles habitually disregard the law. Belton, 54 N. Y. 245. 

f. Geographical Facts: The national boundaries. 

(Strawberry Island Co., 140 N. Y. S. 333); location of the chief American cities (Parks, 27 N. Y. 

S. 289); the navigability of large rivers (Strawberry Island Co., supra); the political subdivisions of the state, such as counties, cities, and towns (Peo. v. Blaedal, 16 N. Y. S. 827); that streets in 

New York City are numbered east and west from 

Fifth Ave. (Conavan, 27 N. Y. S. 413); that New

York City parcels of land are designated by section, block and lot numbers on tax maps. Wallach 

Co., 164 N. Y. S. 616. 

g. Historical Facts: That in 1915, all great Euro-

pean nations were at war (Roy Realty Co., 184 

N. Y. S. 458); that at the same time, the United

States was at peace (Goldstein, 167 N. Y. S. 837); that slavery was abolished by the Civil War (Swinnerton, 37 N. Y. 174); the political history of the state (N. Y. v. Vanderveer, 86 N. Y. S. 

659); and that Karl Marx was a revolutionary

Socialist (Matter of Lithuanian Workers' Lit. 

Soc., 187 N. Y. S. 612); but not that the Communist party is revolutionary in nature. Re 

Fierstein, 41 Fed. (2nd) 53. h. Business and Customs: Courts will take judicial notice of values of circulating mediums, as, for example, that German securities have depreciated since the World War (Zimmerman, 240 N. Y. 

501); but not of the values of particular services (Davies, 112 N. Y. S. 233, cook's services), or of the comparative intrinsic values of gold and silver. 

Blanck, 153 N. Y. 551. 

1. Financial Customs: That bankers and brokers usually negotiate public loans (N. Y. v. Sands, 105 N. Y. 210), that customarily the mortgagor pays the expenses and disbursements of making a loan (Penn. Steel Co., 193 N. Y. 37); that commercial agencies are commonly used by business men in giving credit (Eaton Co., 93 N. Y. 31); that an assignment absolute in form is frequently given as collateral security. 

Silleck, 117 N. Y. S. 964. 

2. General Customs: That, in the United States, vehicular traffic keeps to the right (Jacobs, 156 N. Y. S. 766); that office holders are often controlled by politicians (Re Investigation 

City and County of Albany, 132 N. Y. S. 908); that during theatre performances people congregate about the entrances (Hamlin, 155 

N. Y. S. 963); but not of local customs or of those pertaining to a particular trade. Roland, 115 N. Y. S. 1042. 

i. Words, Phrases and Abbreviations: The usual abbreviations of proper names, as for example,

that "Geo." stands for George (Peo. v. Ferguson, 8 Cow. 102); the meaning of "F. O. B." and "C. O. D."; and of vernacular words and phrases (Simpson, 67 N. Y. S. 401); commonly used phrases, such as "sale of whiskey in bond" (Turner, 195 A. D. 706); but not the meaning of foreign words or phrases. Hassbach, 124 N. Y. S. 379. 

C. Weight of Judicial Notice: Judicial notice is superior to an agreed statement of facts and also to uncontroverted proof in the record. Hunter, 116 N. Y. 615 (where the appellate court in reversing a judgment for negligence took judicial notice that a man could not strike his head against an obstruction four feet and seven inches above the place on which he was sitting, although the uncontroverted proof below so showed). 

III. Presumptions: 

A. Definition and Classification: A presumption is "an inference as to the existence of a fact not known, arising from its connection with facts which are known." 

Jackson, 7 Wend. 62. Presumptions are classified as presumptions of law and presumptions of fact. A presumption of law is a definite rule of law and can be drawn only by the court; a presumption of fact is merely an inference which may be drawn by the jury from circumstantial evidence. Presumptions of law, may be subdivided as conclusive and rebuttable. Where there is a legal presumption the necessity of furnishing evidence is obviated. 

B. Conclusive Presumptions: Forbid inquiry into the real facts. Thus, an infant under seven years of age is conclusively presumed to be incapable of committing a crime. Pen. Law, sec. 816. Also, at common law, a sealed instrument was conclusively presumed to indicate consideration. In New York, until September 1, 1935, this was still true of an executed instrument under seal, but a seal on an executory instrument was only presumptive evidence of consideration. Stiebel, 202 N. Y. 

266 (holding a sealed receipt to conclusively presume the payment of money). Since September 1, 1935, by

virtue of an amendment to C.P.A. 342, a seal on an instrument executed after that date is neither presumptive nor conclusive of consideration. 

1. Rule as to Written Release: A release in writing, signed by the party against whom it is to be enforced, made on or after April 6, 1936, is conclusively presumed to have been given for a sufficient consideration. Pers. Prop. Law, Sec. 33 subd. 2; Real Prop. 

Law, Sec. 279.

C. Rebuttable Presumptions: Those which persist until overcome by contrary evidence. Thus, the defendant in a criminal prosecution is presumed to be innocent until proven guilty beyond a reasonable doubt. 

D. Conflicting Presumptions: Of two conflicting presump tions, the stronger will prevail. Palmer, 162 N. Y. 130.

Thus, the presumption of innocence of crime would outweigh the presumption of continuance of life (Fagin, 151 N. Y. S. 809), or of the regularity of a marriage (In re Eichelberger, 152 Misc. 834) where either of the latter presumptions would make a person guilty of bigamy.

E. Presumption upon Presumption: Generally speaking, there can be no such presumption, since no inference may be properly premised upon a fact the existence of which itself rests upon a prior inference. Lamb, 195 

N. Y. 260.

F. Specific Presumptions of Law: 

1. Presumption of Continuance: A given state of facts being shown to exist will be presumed to continue for a reasonable time. This is also called "presumption against change." MacRae, 130 N. Y. S. 339. a. Illustrations: Mental, moral and bodily habits, such as insanity, gambling, adulterous intercourse, or celibacy will be presumed to continue in the absence of evidence of a change. Wilkins, 44 N. Y. 172. The same is true of business relations between parties, as for example, partnership. Cooper, 22 Barb. 516. Human life once shown to exist, will, in the absence of evidence to the contrary, be presumed to continue for a reasonable time. 

Hall, 122 N. Y. S. 401. This presumption has been applied up to the age of one hundred years. Matter of

Bd. of Ed., 173 N. Y. 321. On the other hand, it is presumed that eighty years after the acknowledgment of a deed, the grantor is dead. Young, 165 N. Y. 385. A woman is rebuttably presumed capable of bearing children as long as she lives. Age alone will not rebut the presumption; proof of loss of reproductive organs will. U. S. v. Prov. Trust Co., 54 Sup. Ct. 389. 

1. Antecedent Relation: Presumption of continuance does not relate antecedently, and a state of facts shown now to exist is not presumed to have existed at any previous time. Schultze Grocer Co., 199 N. Y. S. 196 (condition of beans). But insolvency once shown may be deemed to have existed a reasonable time before. 

See Gaylord, 213 N. Y. S. 25, also, Hopfan, 282 N. Y. S. 219. 

2. Will Cases: Nor does this presumption extend to will cases. Thus, failure to find a will shown to have been made does not give rise to the presumption that decedent died testate, but rather that he destroyed the will animo revocandi. Waterman, 11 N. Y. 157; In re 

Staiger, 243 N. Y. 468. (See Surrog. Ct. Act, Sec. 143 as to method of proving accidentally lost or destroyed will.) 

2. Receipt of Letters and Telegrams: Based on the regularity of the mail service, a letter shown to have been properly addressed, stamped, and deposited in the mail will be presumed to have been received in due course. Diehl, 227 N. Y. 318. Less than positive proof of mailing is not sufficient, without other proof, to overcome evidence that the letter was not received. 

Gardam, 198 N. Y. 175. Thus where the only proof of mailing is that a letter was delivered to an office boy with about fifty other letters to be placed in envelopes and mailed, this is not sufficient, without swearing the office boy, to overcome evidence that the letter was not received. Elmore, 161 N. Y. S. 533.

On the other hand, a bald denial of receipt is insufficient to overcome the presumption of receipt. Austin, 69 N. Y. 571. Positive testimony of non-receipt, accompanied by evidence of insufficient address is sufficient for that purpose. Caglostro, 102 N. Y. S. 

918. The fact that a letter is stamped as having been received at post-office, or sub-station, on a particular date does not give rise to a presumption that it was

deposited in mail box on that date or within a few hours of collection as regulated by custom or rules of post-office fixing specific times for collection. 

National City Bank, 267 N. Y. S. 527. A properly addressed telegram delivered to a telegraph company for transmission is presumed to have reached its destination opportunely. Oregon S. S. Co., 100 N. Y. 

446. But this rule does not apply in an action for the cost of transmission. Com. Cable Co., 165 N. Y. S. 

399. There is no presumption of law or fact that sealed mail addressed to one member of a family and containing a libel, will be opened and read by another member thereby publishing the libel. Weidman, 278 N. Y. 129.

G. Presumptions Arising out of Business: These are manifold. Some important ones are: Persons engaged in a particular business are presumed to be conversant with the general customs and usages of said business. Cassin, 172 N. Y. S. 754. It will be presumed that credit is extended to a responsible principal rather than to an irresponsible agent. Ferris, 47 Barb. 411. In the absence of an agreement as to time of payment for goods sold, it is presumed that payment is to be made on delivery. 

Pers. Prop. L. sec. 123. Similarly, a draft or note in possession of the drawee or maker is presumed to be paid. N. I. L. sec. 200, subd. 5. A contract silent as to time of performance is presumed to be performable within a reasonable time. Wimpel Elec. Co., 162 N. Y. S. 969. 

1. Delivery of Note, Check or Draft: It is presumed, in absence of evidence to the contrary, that delivery of a check is in payment of a debt. Nay, 113 N. Y. 575, (holding that the presumption must be negatived before a prima facie case of a loan can be made out). 

Acceptance of a check, note or draft of a third person by a creditor from his debtor is presumed to be in payment of an indebtedness contracted at the time. and not of an antecedent debt. Noel, 13 N. Y. 167. 

2. Consideration: Every negotiable instrument is presumed to have been issued for a valuable considera-

tion, and every person whose name appears on it is presumed to have received value. This presumption is rebuttable as against all parties except a holder in due course. N. I. L. secs. 50, 54. A completed, negotiable instrument is presumed to have been validly delivered. This presumption is rebuttable as between the immediate parties, but is conclusive in favor of a bona fide purchaser for value. 

3. Solvency: Is presumed until the contrary is shown. 

O'Connor, 117 N. Y. 275.

H. Ownership from Possession: Is presumed (Manhattan 

Co., 192 N. Y. S. 239) but the presumption may be rebutted by any evidence showing the character of the possession. Rawley, 71 N. Y. 85. The presumption is a weak one.

I. Sanity: Every man is presumed to be sane until the contrary is proved. Re Langdon, 160 N. Y. S. 3. The fact that a man commits suicide does not necessarily show that he was insane. Weed, 70 N. Y. 561. 

1. Of Criminal: If defendant produces evidence of in sanity tending to overcome the legal presumption as to his sanity, upon which the State could ordinarily rely, then it becomes incumbent upon the State to prove his sanity as part of the burden cast upon it to prove him guilty beyond a reasonable doubt. Peo. v. Koerner, 154 N. Y. 355.

J. Accidental Death: In civil cases, where there is no evidence explaining the cause of death, there is a presumption that death was caused accidentally and not suicidally. Whitlock, 149 N. Y. 45. 

K. "Idem Sonans": In the absence of other proof on the question there is a presumption of identity of person arising from identity of name. Doberty, 166 N. Y. S. 838.

L. Regularity: 

1. Generally: Everything necessary to validity of judi cial acts or proceedings will be presumed to have existed. Peo. v. Johnson, 46 Hun 667. This presumption extends to the public acts of all public officers. Ramsay, 187 N. Y. 370.

2. Jurisdiction: Courts of record of general jurisdiction are presumed to have acted with jurisdiction of the subject matter and of the parties (Steinhard, 163 N. Y. 410) unless the record itself shows lack of regularity. Re N. Y., 190 N. Y. S. 234. 

3. Due Execution: Formal instruments are presumed to have been formally executed. Re Schweigert, 17 

Misc. 186. 

4. In Will Cases: Where a witness to a will has died, his signature is presumptive evidence of its due execution. Re Hesdra, 119 N. Y. 615. Cf. Surrog. Ct. 

Act, sec. 142. 

M. Knowledge of the Law: Is presumed of all. This presumption applies even to knowledge of the unconstitutionality of an act, although long afterwards so decided by a five to four decision. Holland, 205 N. Y. S. 397. 

Cf. White, 255 A. D. 580. 

N. Consequence of Conduct: A sane man is presumed to intend the reasonably proximate results of his acts, in the absence of evidence of mistake, accident, or duress. Ampersand, 115 N. Y. S. 480. 

1. Murder: At common law, the use of a deadly weapon conclusively presumed intent to murder. Under the

Penal Law, sec. 1044, there is no such presumption and the State must prove a wilful killing with "premeditation and deliberation," to make out first degree murder. If there is an intent to kill, but no deliberation, and death results, it is second degree murder. 

O. Legality of Marriage: Is presumed (Smith, 185 N. Y. 

558) but in a criminal case, (e.g. bigamy or adultery), this presumption may be offset by the stronger presumption of innocence of crime. Palmer, 162 N. Y. 130. (Common law marriages were abolished in New York as of April 29, 1933.) 

P. Legitimacy of Children: Every child is presumed legitimate. Hynes, 91 N. Y. 451. The presumption is rebuttable. The modern "rule of reason" laid down in Matter of Findlay, 253 N. Y. 1, may be summarized as follows: 

1. If the spouses are living together in marital relationship, legitimacy will be presumed even though

the wife is shown to have committed adultery. 

2. If the spouses are living apart, legitimacy may be presumed if there is a fair basis of belief that at times, near gestation, they may have come together. (In the Findlay case, since the spouses at such time were separated by the Atlantic Ocean, the wife having eloped with another man, the court found no such "fair basis of belief" and refused to invoke the presumption in favor of a child born to the wife more than a year after the elopement.) 

Q. Death: 

1. Generally: A legal presumption of death arises from a person's continuous absence from home, unheard from, for seven years, unless such absence is otherwise reasonably explained. "The presumption does not arise, however, when there exist circumstances or facts which reasonably account for his not being heard of, or his absence and abstention from communication are reasonably explained without assuming his death, or where diligent inquiry as to whether he is alive or dead has not been made." Butler, 225 

N. Y. 197. 

2. Time of Death: There is, however, no presumption as to the precise time of the absentee's death (Davenport, 75 N. Y. S. 934), except in the case of a catastrophe whereby the absentee was subjected to peril of his life, e.g. fire or drowning at sea, in which case, the inference may be drawn that the absentee died at the time of such peril. Connor, 166 N. Y. S. 985. 

3. Intestacy: Is presumed, once death is proved. Barson, 191 N. Y. 306. 

4. Survivorship: There is no presumption of survivorship in the case of persons dying in a common disaster. Newell, 75 N. Y. 38. An interested person must prove that one of the persons involved outlived the others. Dunn, 121 N. Y. S. 686. But any evidence of survivorship is sufficient, even for a second. St. 

John, 102 N. Y. S. 808. 

5. Under "Enoch Arden Law" (Dom. Rel. L. sec. 7a): 

A marriage may be dissolved for the continuous, un-

explained absence of a spouse for five successive years, on proof of diligent search and belief of death. 

Such an action may be maintained in either of the following cases: (a) where petitioner is a resident of 

New York and has been such for one year preceding the commencement of the action; (b) where the matrimonial domicile at the time of the disappearance of the absent spouse was in New York. 

6. Under C.P.A. 341: A person possessed of personal property in this state or upon whose life an estate in real property depends; who remains without the 

United States, or absents himself in the state, or elsewhere, for seven years together is presumed to be dead, in an action or special proceeding concerning such property or the administration of the estate of such person, unless it is affirmatively proved that he was alive within that time. C.P.A. 341. See also 

R. P. L. secs. 570-587.

R. Innocence: In criminal cases, there is a presumption that the person accused is innocent until proven guilty beyond a reasonable doubt. This presumption is not affirmative evidence in his favor but means only that the complaint or indictment shall not be considered incriminatory. Peo. v. Waldo, 146 N. Y. S. 581. There is no such presumption in civil cases, even in the case of an action for damages for arson, embezzlement, or any other crime. Kurz, 180 N. Y. 88. In both civil and criminal cases, there is no presumption (in New York) as to "good character". Peo. v. Lingley, 207 N. Y. 396.

S. Incapacity: An infant under seven years of age is conclusively presumed to be incapable of committing a crime. Penal Law sec. 816. If he is between the ages of seven and twelve, the presumption is rebuttable. Penal 

Law, sec. 817. A plea of guilty, alone, by a child between the latter ages is insufficient to rebut the presumption. Peo. v. Domenico, 92 N. Y. S. 390. 

1. Rape: In New York no conviction for rape can be had against one under the age of fourteen years unless his ability to accomplish penetration is proved as

an independent fact beyond a reasonable doubt. Penal 

Law, sec. 2012. 

2. Marriage: A male infant under fourteen and a female under twelve were conclusively presumed at common law to be incapable of consent to marriage. In New 

York the legal age of consent is eighteen years for male and female. Dom. Rel. L., sec. 7. 

T. Malice: Is presumed in libel and slander actions, if the other elements of the offense are proved. Lewis, 16 

N. Y. 369.

U. Guilt or Guilty Knowledge: 

1. Possession of Fruits of Crime: Recently, after its commission, is prima facie evidence of guilty possession, and if unexplained, is sufficient to convict. 

Knickerbocker, 43 N. Y. 177. 

2. Possessing Dangerous Weapons in Automobile: 

Penal Law, sec. 1898a (added in 1936) makes the presence of certain dangerous weapons in an automobile presumptive evidence of illegal possession by all persons in the automobile at the time. The statute has been held both constitutional and unconstitutional. Cf. Peo. ex rel Dixon v. Lewis, 249 A. D. 464. 

3. Spoliation and Fabrication of Evidence: Deliberate destruction of written evidence relevant to the issues gives rise to the inference that the matter destroyed was unfavorable to the spoliator. Re Eno, 187 N. Y. 

S. 756. Also, fabrication of oral testimony in a party's own favor, creates an inference that he has a weak case. The same is true of an attempt to bribe a witness to testify in favor of the bribe-giver. "It is for the consideration of the jury, after ample opportunity for explanation and denial, under proper instructions to prevent them from giving undue attention to the collateral matter to the detriment of the main issue." Nowack, 166 N. Y. 433. 

4. Withholding Evidence: 

a. Real Evidence: Failure to produce real evidence in a party's possession, determinative of a disputed issue, gives rise to a legal presumption that the fact is otherwise than he claims. Reehil, 129

A. D. 563. 

b. Personal Evidence: In the case of failure to produce material, oral evidence (i.e. calling friendly witnesses) where a party has the power to produce same, the triers of the facts may legitimately draw the inference that the withheld testimony is unfavorable and may on that account give less weight to the evidence which the party has produced. Mullen, 195 N. Y. 109; Perlman, 192 A. D. 

179. This adverse inference does not arise from failure to call a witness equally available to both sides, or one who by relationship and employment would prove hostile (Hayden, 233 N. Y. 34) or one whose testimony would be merely cumulative or corroborative. Neale, 146 N. Y. S. 263. 

c. Failure to Testify: 

1. In Criminal Cases: In New York, at the pres ent writing, no presumption of guilt arises from defendant's failure to take the stand in his own behalf, nor may the court or prosecutor comment thereon. Nor may the court charge that "you are warranted in taking the facts and circumstances established in the case, which if innocent he might have controverted or explained, most strongly against him." Peo. v. Forte, 277 N. Y. 440. (Cf. Peo. v. Trombino, 238 A. D. 61.) The protection to the defendant is complete and absolute. (See Peo. v. Minkowitz, 220 N. Y. 399; Peo. ex rel Woronoff, 222 

N. Y. 456; 31 Mich. L. Rev. 226.) 

2. In Civil Actions: Failure of a party to tes tify in his own behalf as to a material matter personally known to him, creates an adverse inference against him. Dowling, 211 N. Y. 199.

V. Res Ipsa Loquitur: ("The thing speaks for itself.") A maxim commonly applied in negligence actions, where the mere happening of the accident gives rise to an inference of negligence, e.g. building suddenly collapses into street (Mullen, 57 N. Y. 567), or passenger elevator suddenly falls down shaft. Griffen, 166 N. Y. 188. Two

elements must concur for the maxim to apply: first, the accident must be accountable for on no other reasonable theory than defendant's negligence; second, the control of the agencies producing the accident must be wholely and solely in one defendant. Robinson, 194 N. Y. 37. 

See also Lessig, 271 N. Y. 250. 

1. Illustrations: P is injured by a heavy bolt used to hold together an elevated structure of which it was part falling to street level. Res ipsa. Volkman, 134 N. Y. 418. P, a pedestrian, is injured by a brick falling from a building in the process of construction, upon which building many workmen under the control of several contractors are working. Not res ipsa. Wolf, 164 N. Y. 30. Cf. Hughes, 131 A. D. 185. P, pedestrian, is injured by iron door lock, ripped from one of the car doors on D's elevated railway train and hurled through a window of the train. When in place the lock had been thirty-three inches from the floor of the car and the window opening of the car, at its lowest point, was fifty-four inches above the floor. Not res ipsa, because accident is accountable on theory other than D's negligence. Sandler, 249 N. Y. 236.

P, while a passenger in a trolley car is injured in a collision with another vehicle. Res ipsa as against trolley car common carrier, but not as against other vehicle. Plum, 233 N. Y. 

285. Passenger automobile suddenly swerves off highway, for no definitely assignable reason and injures P. Not res ipsa, accident might have been caused by lapse of driver or latent defect in mechanism. Galbraith, 267 N. Y. 230. P is injured as a result of coal stove, which had been previously connected with a water tank, exploding after a fire had been started in it. 

Res ipsa. Tortora, 269 N. Y. 167. P, while a passenger in a train on D's subway system, and while sitting quietly, is injured as the result of the sudden shattering of a pane of glass. Res ipsa. Bressler, 277 N. Y. 199.

W. Law of Foreign States and Nations: 

1. Statutory Law: Of sister state or foreign country is not presumed to be the same as that of New York. 

Venner, 145 N. Y. S. 725, aff'd 217 N. Y. 615. 

2. Common Law: It is presumed that the common law exists in all states in which such law is the basis of their legal system, and that it is the same as the common law of New York. International Text Book 

Co., 206 N. Y. 188, Cherwein, 272 N. Y. 165. But where the evidence of the common law of a foreign sister state is conflicting there is no presumption that it is the same as the New York common law.

a. Case: D resided in Pennsylvania and his automobile was registered in his name in that state. On a visit to New York, he loaned his car to his adult son S, to take P, S' fiancee, to her uncle's home in New Jersey. S was a licensed driver and had never had a serious accident before. While in 

New Jersey, P was injured due to S' negligent driving. P sues D in New York, but did not plead or prove the New 

Jersey law. From a judgment for P, D appeals. Held, error.

Our V. & T. Law, Sec. 59 has no extraterritorial effect. If 

New Jersey (lex loci) has a similar statute it should have been pleaded and proved. In the absence of such proof, it will be assumed that the New Jersey common law governs and that it is the same as ours. Under our common law, D would not be liable, since one who gratuitously loans his car to an adult member of his family, not known to be a negligent driver, for the latter's business or pleasure is not liable for the latter's negligence. Cherwein, supra. 

3. Non-Common Law Jurisdictions: It is not presumed that the law of a foreign state or country, whose law is not based on common law, is the same as that of New York. Where the cause of action arose in such foreign jurisdiction, the foreign law must be proved as a fact. Riley, 245 N. Y. 152.

CHAPTER II. 

. 

I. Burden of Proof: 

A. Defined: The duty resting upon a party to establish the truth of his affirmative allegations. Usually, but not necessarily, this party is the plaintiff. Kay, 163 N. Y. 447.

B. "Burden of Proof" Distinguished from "Burden of Evidence": Burden of proof, in its proper sense, never shifts. It means, in a civil case, that plaintiff must prove his entire case by a preponderance of evidence. "The same phrase is used, however, to indicate the duty of proceeding to adduce the evidence, and that burden does shift to the opposing party each time a prima facie case is made by the other. The fact that the same words are used for two entirely distinct ideas often leads to much confusion of thought." Toube, 116 N. Y. S. 673. 

C. Where Proof Evenly Balanced: Where the proof is evenly balanced at the end of the trial, the jury must find against the party having the burden of proof. Kay, supra. Hence the necessity of correctly determining, at the outset, the party having the burden of proof. 

D. Pleadings Fix the Burden of Proof: Generally, the party having the affirmative of the issues, as determined by the pleadings, has the burden of proof. Heineman, 62 

N. Y. 448. Thus, where defendant denies any of the material allegations of the complaint, plaintiff has the burden of proving such allegations (Marcotte, 91 N. Y. 

S. 744), but where defendant sets up an affirmative defense only, not denying plaintiff's allegations, defendant would have the burden of proof. Coffin, 136 N. Y. 655; 

Stokes, 155 N. Y. 581; Murray, 85 N. Y. 236. 

1. Unliquidated Damages: Even though defendant puts in an affirmative defense only, if plaintiff seeks unliquidated damages not admitted by defendant, plaintiff has the burden of proof. Tallmadge, 131 N. Y. 565. 

2. Counterclaim or Set-off: Defendant has the burden of proving allegations of his counterclaim or set-off 20

denied by plaintiff. Connolly, 45 N. Y. S. 1042.

E. In Criminal Cases: Burden of proof is on State to prove beyond a reasonable doubt (i.e. “to a moral certainty") every essential element of the crime charged. Peo. v. 

Russell, 266 N. Y. 147. 

1. Insanity: Defendant raises the defense. State must negative it beyond reasonable doubt. Peo. v. Koerner, 154 N. Y. 355. 

2. Self-Defense: Same rule. Sawyer v. Peo., 91 N. Y. 

667. 

3. Alibi: Same rule. Peo. v. Allocca, 183 A. D. 571. 

C. Cr. Proc. sec. 295 compels an indicted defendant, on the State's demand to serve a bill of particulars in advance of trial giving names and addresses of his witnesses relied upon to prove a proposed defense of alibi. So far, the section has been held constitutional. Note, however, that where an accused resists extradition on the ground that he was not within the demanding state at the time of the crime, and hence is not a fugitive from justice, the burden of proof is on him to completely and satisfactorily prove his contention. Peo. ex rel. Steel v. Mulrooney, 248 N. Y. S. 520. 

4. Possession of License to Do Otherwise Criminal 

Act: In such case (e.g., license to carry weapon, sell liquor, etc.) since the facts of the defense are peculiarly within his knowledge, defendant has the burden of proving same. Peo. v. Maxwell, 83 Hun 

157, 31 N. Y. S. 564. 

5. Criminal Prosecution for Abortion: There is authority that the burden is on defendant causing same, to prove that the abortion was necessary to save life. 

Bradford v. Peo., 20 Hun 309. However this case was decided in 1880 one year before the enactment of C. Cr. Proc. sec. 389 which provides that in a criminal case defendant is presumed innocent until the contrary be proved. As a result of this provision "the burden of proof may never be shifted from the People to the defendant." Peo. v. Russell, supra..

F. Right to Open and Close: The party who has the burden

of proof, in the absence of statutory provisions to the contrary, has the important right to open and close to the jury. Heilbron, 165 N. Y. 98 (holding that in an action for purchase price of goods sold, defendant has burden of proving his defense that credit term has not yet expired).

G. Burden of Proof in Particular Cases: 

1. Contracts: 

a. Failure of Consideration: In an action on a written contract, where the defense is lack, or failure, of consideration, the burden of proof is on plaintiff to prove a valuable consideration. Irving, 35

N. Y. S. 744. In the case of a negotiable instrument, plaintiff would be assisted by the presumption. See Spear, 120 Misc. 518. 

b. Illegal Consideration: Illegality of a contract, 

(e.g., usury, gambling contract) unless it so appears on the face of the complaint, in which case the illegality could be shown by defendant under a general denial, is a defense to be specifically pleaded. C. P. A. 242; Brearton, 252 N. Y. 495.

But plaintiff has the burden of proving that the contract was valid and had a legal inception. 

Murray, 192 N. Y. 172. Cf. Bigelow, 70 N. Y. 202. c. Fraud, Duress, etc.: These defenses are in reality negative in that they deny the existence of a legal contract having a valid inception. They "pertain to the facts which took place at the time the contract was alleged to have been executed, and became the res gestae, upon which the validity of the contract depends. This class of defenses is distinguishable from those affirmative defenses. which are based upon facts occurring subsequently to the execution of a contract, in which it may be changed, altered, modified or settled. 

It therefore follows that the plaintiff in undertaking to prove the contract upon which his action is based, had cast upon him the burden of establishing, by a preponderance of evidence, that it was a good and valid contract having a legal

inception which was binding upon the defendant, and that burden of proof continued with him throughout the case." Haight, J. in Murray v. 

Narwood, 192 N. Y. 172. d. Forgery: Is a negative defense and may be shown by defendant under a general denial, since, plaintiff, in order to make out a prima facie case, must show the instrument in suit to be a valid, subsisting obligation and the act and deed of defendant. Farmers Loan Co., 144 N. Y. 354. But see C.P.A. 342.

H. Non-Payment: There are three rules: 

1. In an action on contract, for the payment of money only, where the complaint does not allege a balance due over and above all payments made, defendant has the burden of proving payment and must specifically plead same (e.g., complaint alleges that defendant owes plaintiff $100, no part of which has been paid). 

Conkling, 181 N. Y. 258. 

2. When the complaint sets forth a balance in excess of all payments, owing to the structure of the pleadings, it is necessary for plaintiff to prove the allegation as made and this leaves the amount of the payments open to defendant under a general denial (e.g., complaint alleges that defendant owes plaintiff a balance of $50). Both sides of the account are open under the general issue. Plaintiff must prove nonpayment and defendant could show payment under a general denial. Acharan, 128 N. Y. S. 943. Cf. 

C.P.A. 242. a. Where, however, only one side of the account is left open under the pleadings, defendant must specifically plead and prove payment (e.g., complaint alleges that defendant owes plaintiff $100, no part of which has been paid except two payments of $10 each). Conkling, supra. 

3. When the action is not upon contract for the payment of money but is upon an obligation created by operation of law, or is for the enforcement of a lien where non-payment of the amount secured is part of

24 the cause of action, it is necessary for plaintiff both to allege and prove the fact of non-payment. Conkling, supra.

I. Negligence: In New York, plaintiff must plead the ultimate facts showing defendant's negligence, and, in addition, must plead his own freedom from contributory negligence. The latter may be pleaded by a general statement to such effect. (Failure to plead freedom from contributory negligence is not fatal where the ultimate facts pleaded show that only defendant was negligent.) On the trial, in order to make out a prima facie case, plaintiff must prove both. Camardo, 247 N. Y. 111.

In two cases, however, by statute, the duty of pleading and proving contributory negligence is put upon defendant, viz.: in a wrongful death action (C.P.A. 265), and in an action brought by an employee or his representative for damages for negligence arising out of the employment. Emp. Liab. Act, sec. 5; Hall, 220 N. Y. 299. 

1. In Bailment Cases: The burden of proof to show de fendant's negligence never shifts from the bailor. 

Hermann, 128 N. Y. S. 380. Thus, plaintiff makes out a prima facie case by showing non-delivery of the bailed property after due demand, or delivery in a damaged condition. Goldstein, 220 N. Y. 549; Claflin, 75 N. Y. 260. But after defendant has met the burden of evidence imposed upon him to rebut this prima facie case, by explaining the loss or injury, (e.g., by showing an accidental destruction by fire, or burglary through no fault of defendant) it is still for the jury, whether on all the evidence, plaintiff has made out a case of negligence. Lamb, 46 N. Y. 271. Where, however, the injury or loss can be explained only on the theory of negligence, the burden is on defendant bailee to show such injury or loss to have been caused through no fault of his. Selesky, 95 N. Y. S. 130 (sound horse returned by defendant bailee in injured condition through abuse); Wimpfheimer, 78 Misc. 

171 (furs placed in cold storage returned by bailee in moth-eaten condition). See also Gen. Bus. L., sec. 

200-201 providing special rules for innkeepers.

25 

J. Unjustifiable Discharge from Employment: B, breaking a contract with A, discharges the latter from employment. A sues B for damages for the breach. A need not show lack of justification, but B must show justification. 

B cannot prove justification under a general denial. He must specifically plead it and has the burden of proving it. Linton, 124 N. Y. 533. Cf. Milbank, 127 N. Y. 370.

Under a general denial, defendant may controvert by evidence anything which plaintiff is bound or permitted to prove in the first instance to make out his cause of action. Molloy, 217 N. Y. 577. 

K. Infancy: Defendant, who sets up infancy as a defense has the burden of proving it. Garbarsky, 73 N. Y. S. 199.

L. Undue Influence and Testamentary Capacity: In a con tested will probate, proponents have the burden of proving due execution and testamentary capacity. Matter of Eno, 187 N. Y. S. 756. Contestants, however, must prove fraud or undue influence. Matter of Kindberg, 207 N. Y. 220. There is no presumption of undue influence where testator leaves a large legacy to the attorney who prepared the will merely because of the relationship. Contra, where the gift is inter vivos. 

M. Law of Forum Governs Burden of Proof: This is so because "burden of proof" is procedural (relating to the remedy). Clark, 264 N. Y. S. 873. 

1. Case: P was injured in Ontario due to the negligence of

D railroad. The Ontario laws put the burden of proving contributory negligence upon D and further provided that contributory negligence (if less than D's negligence), defeats P's recovery only to the extent of such contributory negligence as found and apportioned by the jury. P sues D in 

New York. Should the New York courts apply the New

York law as to burden of proof? Held: No. The Ontario law governs. It is more than procedural. It goes to the cause of action itself. It gives P the right to recover even though contributorily negligent. In New York, if the accident arose here, P would have no such right. Fitzpatrick, 252 N. Y. 127.

II. Province of Court and Jury: Generally, questions of fact. 

are for the jury and those of law for the court. In the case of criminal prosecution for libel both the law and the facts are for the jury. N. Y. Const. Art. 1, sec. 8. Peo. v. Yui

Kui Chu, 273 N. Y. 191.

A. Credibility of Witnesses: "Weight" of evidence is deter mined by the credibility of witnesses and not by their number. Kurz, 180 N. Y. 88. Credibility, in turn, is determined by the jury from listening to and watching the witnesses. Maxwell, 219 N. Y. 597. “When a disinterested witness, who is not discredited, testifies to a fact, which is not of itself improbable, the witness is to be believed and the fact is to be taken as legally established so that it cannot be disregarded by court or jury.” 

Kavanagh, 70 N. Y. 177; Hull, 182 N. Y. 569. 

1. On Appeal from Death Sentence: In such case the credibility of witnesses may be examined by the appellate court. Peo. v. Landers, 264 N. Y. 119; Peo. v. Arata, 255 N. Y. 374.

B. Preliminary Questions of Fact for Court: 

1. Competency of Witness: Is the child witness, in the civil action, intelligent enough to be sworn? Is the witness incompetent to testify because of the privileged character of the testimony? Has the witness been properly qualified as an expert? Is the witness incompetent under C.P.A. 347? All these questions are for the court. 

2. Admissibility of Evidence: Was the dying declara tion made while the declarant was in extremis without hope of recovery? Has sufficient search been. made for a lost paper as a condition precedent to the right to offer secondary evidence? Do all the requirements for the admission of a pedigree declaration exist? These, too, are for the court and not the jury. 

3. Probable Cause in Malicious Prosecution: Where the facts are undisputed the existence of probable cause in a malicious prosecution action is a question of law for the court (Anderson, 116 N. Y. 336); where the facts are disputed the question is for the jury. Halstead, 234 N. Y. 1. 

4. Libel or Slander Per Se: Where words are capable of only one import, and that defamatory, the court should charge as a matter of law, that they are libelous or slanderous. Kimmerly, 262 N. Y. 99

(reference to plaintiff as "latest prominent assassin" held libelous per se). Where, however, the alleged defamatory words may have an innocent interpretation, the question whether they are actually libelous or slanderous is for the jury. Wash. Post Co., 250

5. Foreign Law: Is for the court. See same title under

Judicial Notice, Chap. I. 

6. Reasonable Care: Certain acts have been adjudicated to be negligent per se, e.g., approaching a blind railroad crossing in an automobile without slackening speed (Crouch, 260 N. Y. 227), crossing a railroad track without exercising the senses of sight and hearing (Stackus, 79 N. Y. 464), driving an automobile without brakes, sending a box of dynamite by express without disclosing the contents, "hitching" a ride by a twelve year old on the rear bumper of a moving automobile. Jennings, 255 N. Y. 626. 

7. Directed Verdict: In a criminal case the court may direct a verdict of acquittal only. C. Cr. Proc. 410. 

For a discussion of the right to direct a verdict in a civil case and the applicable test see same title Berk. 

Practice. 

C. Court May Not Usurp Jury's Function: During the trial or in its charge, the court may not make statements as to the weight to be given to the testimony (except where the law requires such instruction) nor may the court, in a criminal prosecution, by comment. tone, demeanor, gesture, or otherwise, impose upon the jury the court's own opinion as to defendant's guilt. Peo. v. Ohanian, 245 N. Y. 227. Where the trial court has been guilty of such "convicting comment," the appellate court will reverse the conviction and will not speculate on whether or not the jurors were improperly influenced by the comment. "Certainly a jury which must find a verdict of guilt, in obedience to instructions, is not a judge of the facts, despite all wordy assurances, to the contrary, voiced by the trial judge." Peo. v. Cohen, 251 

N. Y. 375. 

1. Case: Trial of indictment for attempted burglary. The

evidence was that D tried to gain admittance into a tenement house flat by rapping on a kitchen door several times. 

Receiving no response D walked down the hallway to a parlor door and knocked several times. He then passed up a hall stairway to the kitchen door, knocked again, and tried to open the door. D was arrested as he was leaving the building. Court charged "the inference is irresistible that D was there with the intent to steal"; that "no logical man could find any other reason except that of guilt of the person who tried those doors," and that the question of D's guilt or innocence was for the jury. D was convicted, and appeals. 

Held: In the absence of proof that D was pursuing, an innocent quest (he may have been an over zealous brush salesman) it was a fair inference that his purposes were criminal, but the inference was for the jury and not for the court. The court by its charge usurped the jury's function. Reversed. 

Peo. v. Cohen, supra.

CHAPTER III. 

 I. Real Evidence: 

A. Generally: Is that acquired by the court and jury from observation and inspection of physical objects brought into court, or which the jury is permitted to inspect out of court. Peo. v. Fernandez, 35 N. Y. 49. This is, of course, the most satisfactory form of evidence. Thus, juries may and should be permitted to inspect clothes identified as those worn by accused at the time of a homicide, or in a negligence action, a broken hook (King, 72 N. Y 609) or a defective matting (McGuiré, 10 N. Y. S. 386) alleged to have caused the accident. In fact, any object which is relevant to the issues and which has any probative force is admissible. Smith, 177 N. Y. 379.

B. Resemblance: Inspection of persons is not permitted to prove resemblance. Bilcovec, 141 N. Y. S. 179.

C. Age: A child may be exhibited to the court or jury in order to determine its age. C.P.A. 334. Penal L. sec. 817. 

D. Injuries: Plaintiff in a civil action for damages for personal injuries may exhibit his injuries or their effects to the jury where the nature and extent of the injuries are matters in issue. Looram, 11 N. Y. St. Rep. 652.

But where the only effect of such an exhibition is to prejudice the jury by an appeal to their sympathies, it should not be allowed. Rost, 41 N. Y. S. 1069 (child's amputated foot preserved in glass jar shown to jury held error).

E. Restrictions on Introduction: 

1. Indecency and Impropriety: Should be avoided as far as possible in introducing real evidence. Thus, a photograph of plaintiff's nude body should not be allowed in evidence to show injuries. Guhl, 109 Wis. 

69. But he may be allowed to bare his body to the waist to show fractured ribs. Perry, 74 N. Y. 1. 29

2. Privilege against Self-Incrimination; (N. Y. Const. 

Art. 1, Sec. 6): Does not prevent the seizing of in criminatory articles found on an accused as an incident of a legal arrest and placing them in evidence. 

Peo. v. Van Wormer, 175 N. Y. 188 (where defendant's shoes were seized for the purpose of comparing them with footprints). Nor does it prevent a search being made of defendant's body for marks and bruises, or the removal of a growth of beard or toupee for better identification. (Would defendant have a constitutional right to shave where he is deprived of such right in order to facilitate the growth of a beard so as to aid identification? See N. Y. L. J. 

Jan. 20, 1937.) Note, too, that defendant may be forcibly compelled on his trial to stand up in order to assist a witness in identification. Peo. v. Gardner, 144 N. Y. 119. 

a. "Testimonial Compulsion": The New York rule against self-incrimination is very liberal in favor of the prosecuting authorities. Virtually the only case in which defendant can claim his constitutional privilege against self-incrimination is in the case of "testimonial compulsion" i.e., compelling defendant by order or ruling of the court, before or on trial, to testify against himself or to produce articles, books, papers, records, etc. tending to incriminate him. Peo. v. Adams, 176 N. Y. 351; 

Peo. v. Defore, 242 N. Y. 13. 

1. Case: On the trial of a larceny indictment the D. A. 

was permitted over D's objection and exception to ask D's attorney, in the presence of the jury, to produce certain incriminating papers and then to call the attorney to the stand to testify about them and to declare his refusal to produce them. Would this procedure be legal as long as it had for its purpose the laying of a foundation for the introduction of secondary evidence? Held: No. The procedure was illegal for any purpose. In effect it was compelling D to either testify against himself or create an adverse inference in the jury's mind because of his refusal to do so. 

Peo. v. Minkowitz, 220 N. Y. 399. 

3. Illegal Search and Seizure; New York Rule: In New

York even though the objects, books or papers have

been illegally seized in violation of the U. S. Const. 

Fourth Amendment against unreasonable search and seizure (Civil Rights Law Sec. 8) their use in evidence will not be restrained, either on that ground or on the ground that such use would be a violation of the constitutional guarantee against self-incrimina tion (U. S. Const. Fifth Amend.; State Const. Art. 1, 

Sec. 6). a. Case: In Peo. v. Defore, 242 N. Y. 13 it was held that where a police officer illegally arrested D without a warrant for a misdemeanor not committed or attempted in his presence, a black-jack found in D's possession at the time of his arrest was competent evidence, nevertheless, on an indictment against D for the possession of a dangerous weapon. In that case, the court put much stress upon the fact that the weapon was per se contraband; i. e., one subject to destruction or forfeiture upon seizure. 

The court said "Things outlawed or contraband possessed without right and subject upon seizure to forfeiture and destruction, may be offered in evidence without trenching upon the privilege in respect of self incrimination whether seizure has been made with warrant or without" and, although the court refused to determine the application of the constitutional privilege to things lawfully possessed, since the question was not before it, every inference in the decision points to the conclusion that no exception will be made even in case of seizure of things lawfully possessed, should the question arise. 

4. Illegal Search and Seizure; Federal Rule: Evidence illegally seized by federal agencies may be excluded both under the Fourth and Fifth Amendments of the U. S. Const. Weeks, 232 U. S. 383; Gouled, 255 U. S. 

298. Evidence illegally seized by a private person and furnished to a federal agency is admissible. 

Burdeau, 256 U. S. 465. State officers, who are not acting solely on behalf of the federal agency, in illegally seizing the evidence, are deemed private persons. Gambino, 275 U. S. 310. a. Wire Tapping: Sec. 605 of the Fed. Communications Act (47 U. S. C. A. 605) provides that no person, not being authorized by the sender, shall intercept any communication and divulge or publish the existence or contents thereof. Under this section, tapping of interstate wires is illegal and

evidence thus obtained is inadmissible in the federal courts. Nardone v. U. S., 302 U. S. 379. 

It has recently been held that this prohibition extends as well to the tapping of intrastate wires (U. S. v. Weis, 60 Sup. Ct. 269) and also to evidence indirectly derived from the illegal tapping. (The New York state constitution now authorizes wire tapping on court warrant issued on a showing of probable cause and necessity.) Cf. 

Peo. v. McDonald, 177 A. D. 806; In re Davis, 299 N. Y. S. 591. 

b. When Warrant Necessary to Support Search: If defendant is lawfully arrested, as an incident to such arrest, his person, and the premises where the arrest is made, even if it is his home, may be lawfully searched without a warrant for the fruits or evidence of crime. This is the New York rule as well as the Federal rule. Peo. v. Chiagles, 237 

N. Y. 193; Carroll, 276 U. S. 132. The search cannot be a general, exploratory search (Lefkowitz, 285 U. S. 452) nor may the search (without warrant) be justified as an incident of a lawful arrest which is made after and as a result of the search. 

Taylor, 286 U. S. 1. But a search without warrant may be made when there is probable cause to believe that a crime is being committed in the presence of the arresting officers so that an arrest could be made forthwith without warrant. Lee, 274 U. S. 559. And if the evidence sought by the government be in a ship or vehicle, as distinguished from a dwelling, and if there be probable cause for believing that contraband is being transported, the search and seizure may be lawfully effected without a warrant. Carroll, 267

U. S. 132. 

1. Note: In New York, a peace officer or private person who arrests for a misdemeanor, without warrant, acts illegally, unless the crime. was committed or attempted in his presence. 

C. Cr. Proc. 177, 183.

c. Rights of Defendant where Evidence is Illegally 

Seized: He may: 

1. move before trial for its return. Weeks, 232

2. move to exclude it on the trial if until that time he had no knowledge of the illegality of the seizure. Gouled, 255 U. S. 298. See also, Agnello, 269 U. S. 20. 

3. move to exclude any evidence lawfully obtained as a result of information illegally seized. Silverthome Lumber Co., 251 U. S. 385.

F. Inspection of Scene of Crime: May be permitted in the court's discretion, at any time before verdict (the jury may be permitted to suspend deliberations to visit scene of the crime). Peo. v. Landers, 264 N. Y. 119. Such inspection, however, is no part of the trial or the taking of testimony. Its purpose is to enable the jurors to more accurately understand the personal testimony. 

Peo. v. Thorn, 156 N. Y. 286. Cf. Snyder v. Mass., 29 

U. S. 97. (Note that an unauthorized inspection by one or more jurors either in a civil or criminal action is improper and may result in a mistrial. Curry, 166 N. Y. 

S. 367. Note, too, that in New York a jury view in a civil action, other than waste, is not permissible. R. P. 

L. sec. 528; Buffalo Steel Co., 90 N. Y. S. 268.)

II. Circumstantial Evidence: 

A. Character: 

1. In Civil Actions: Evidence of the character of a party to a civil action is not admissible for the purpose of raising an inference as to his conduct. Taylor, 135 N. Y. S. 450. This rule has been applied in an action for slander (Houghtaling, 1 N. Y. 530); in an action on a fire insurance policy, where defendant made a fraudulent valuation of his loss (Fowler, 6

Cow. 673), and in an action for fraudulent representations as to solvency. Gough, 4 N. Y. 493. When character is in issue in civil actions, evidence thereof is admissible. Stafford, 142 N. Y. 598. But plaintiff may not offer evidence of good character unless

defendant first puts in evidence of plaintiff's bad character. McKane, 202 N. Y. 181 (where in an action for damages for breach of contract to marry, plaintiff's reputation for good character was held inadmissible to disprove specific act of misconduct proved by defendant by way of defense). a. Libel and Slander: In an action for libel or slander, defendant may show that plaintiff's general reputation is bad, since in that case he suffers less damage. Ellis, 177 N. Y. S. 730. However, such evidence must be confined to general bad reputation; evidence of rumors or of specific instances of bad conduct is inadmissible. Abell, 241 

N. Y. 327. But if plaintiff seeks damages for injury to the feelings, defendant may show specific acts of misconduct in mitigation of damages. 

Osterheld, 131 N. Y. S. 247. b. Malicious Prosecution: The same rule applies in an action for malicious prosecution. Such proof is relevant to show probable cause and absence of malice on defendant's part and also on the question of damages. Hart, 64 N. Y. S. 827. c. Breach of Marriage Promise: In such action, defendant may show plaintiff's unchastity both by proof of general reputation and by proof of specific instances of misconduct. McKane, supra. d. Seduction: The same rule applies in an action by a father for his daughter's seduction. Wandell, 25 Hun 498. Evidence of the parent's bad reputation for chastity is inadmissible, although it would seem, on principle, that, in mitigation of damages, defendant should be able to show that the suing parent is of such debased susceptibilities as not to have sustained any real injury to the feelings. e. Criminal Conversation: Defendant may show, in mitigation of damages, the wife's reputation for unchastity and her specific acts of misconduct, but plaintiff husband may not show the wife's good reputation before she met defendant, unless

336 defendant has first given evidence against the wife's general character other than proof of his own acts with the wife. Pratt, 4 N. Y. 493. 

f. Divorce: Evidence of defendant's reputation as a person of good character is inadmissible to disprove specific act of misconduct. Booth, 238 

N. Y. S. 193. g. New Law Abolishing "Heart-Balm" Suits: See same title Berk. Practice. 

2. In Criminal Actions: 

a. General Rule: The state may not introduce evidence of defendant's bad character, in order to raise an inference of guilt, unless and until defendant "opens the door" by putting in evidence of his good character. Peo. v. Hinksman, 192 

N. Y. 421; Peo. v. Zackowitz, 254 N. Y. 192. So strong is this rule that if defendant does not offer evidence of good character but testifies in his own behalf (to prove a defense) the state may not undermine his general character while impeaching his reputation for veracity as a witness. Peo. v. Hinksman, supra. But once defendant introduces evidence of good character, which he may always do (since good character evidence, if believed by the jury, may be sufficient to raise the reasonable doubt necessary to acquit, Peo. v. Trimarchi, 231 N. Y. 263) the state may counter with proof of both bad reputation and previous convictions of other crimes. 

C. Cr. Proc. sec. 393-c. (The exclusion of repu tation evidence offered by defendant is error even though the state concedes his good reputation. 

Peo. v. Holm, 243 A. D. 818.) 

1. Case: D is on trial for maintaining a bawdy house. 

K, a housekeeper in D's hotel testified on D's behalf that she had no knowledge that D's hotel was used for immoral purposes. On cross-examination, the prosecution was permitted to prove by K that she had previously worked for D in other hotels and that D had been convicted of running these hotels as disorderly houses and that K knew this. D did not take the stand and did not "open the door". Held,

error.

The cross-examination was improper, since indirectly, it showed previous convictions of D, the latter not having opened the door to such proof. 

Peo. v. Richardson, 222 N. Y. 103. 

b. Nature of Evidence: Reputation evidence must be closely scrutinized. Two rules apply: 

1.. the evidence must be confined to the specific moral trait involved, e.g., honesty in a larceny case, but not in a homicide case; 

2. the witness must testify from his knowledge of the general reputation in the community, and not from his own personal knowledge of the subject's conduct. Peo. v. Gaasbeck, 189 

N. Y. 408. The community must be one in which the subject resides (or has resided) and is known. Conkey v. Peo., 1 Abb. Dec. 418.

The witness must have resided in the same community for a considerable length of time, altho such residence need not be continuous. 

Graham, 37 How. Pr. 279. The "community" may be a college, army regiment, or even a prison. Thomas v. Peo., 67 N. Y. 218, Peo. v. 

Colantone, 243 N. Y. 134. 

(a) Negative Evidence: Good reputation may be shown by evidence that a witness so situated that he probably would have heard the subject's reputation discussed never heard any discussion of the matter. 

Peo. v. Van Gaasbeck, supra. But bad reputation may not so be shown. Carlson v. Peo., 147 N. Y. 652. 

3. "Good Reputation" Distinguished from "Good Character": "Character" is what a person is intrinsically. "Reputation" is what people believe him to be. On the necessity principle, since we cannot prove the first, we are forced to prove the second. Although reputation is founded largely on hearsay, and the common speech of the community, the law says that it exists like any other fact and is testified to by the "character" (or reputation) witness as a fact. 

4. Reputation of Victim of Homicide: For violence and

vindictiveness may be shown by defendant in selfdefense, if known to defendant at time of homicide. 

Peo. v. Rodawald, 177 N. Y. 408. Threats of the deceased made against defendant before the homicide are admissible to show that the deceased was the aggressor and that defendant killed in self-defense, even though the threats were never communicated to defendant. 

Peo. v. Taylor, 177 N. Y. 237. 

5. Character of Complainant in Rape and Seduction 

Cases: In a rape case, defendant may show previous acts of intercourse with complainant and also her reputation for unchastity. Woods, 55 N. Y. 515. But particular acts of unchastity with third persons have been held both admissible (Peo. v. Abbott, 19 

N. Y. 192) and inadmissible. Peo. v. Jackson, 3 Park.

Cr. Rep. 391. Where such acts amount to common prostitution they may be shown. Peo. v. Buccufirni, 158 A. D. 186. a. In Second Degree Rape, unchastity of complainant may not be shown since the element of consent is immaterial. Penal Law, sec. 2010. 

b. In Seduction Prosecutions: The woman must be previously chaste. Penal Law, sec. 2175. This means actual virtue and not merely good reputation for chastity. Peo. v. Nelson, 153 N. Y. 90.

Therefore, unchastity can be established only by proof of specific instances of immorality. Peo. v. 

Kenyon, 26 N. Y. 203. (May a widow be criminally seduced? Yes. See State v. Eddy, 40 S. D. 

390, 167 N. W. 392; Butts v. State, 12 Okla. Cr. 

391, 157 Pac. 704.)

B. Habits and Customs: 

1. Generally: Where the doing or not doing of a certain act is in question, proof of habit or custom may be relevant and admissible. Beakes, 126 N. Y. 293. 

2. Negligence Cases: 

a. Defendant's Negligence: May not be proved by evidence that he had previously committed similar acts or that he was generally negligent. 

Wooster, 25 N. Y. S. 378. Nor is it competent

to show that an employee of defendant, charged with negligence, had been negligent on previous occasions (Warner, 44 N. Y. 465), although such evidence is ordinarily competent on the question of exemplary damages, where defendant is charged with gross negligence in knowingly employing an incompetent servant. Cleghorn, 56 N. Y. S. 129.

Evidence of general usage in similar businesses is admissible for what it is worth where the negligence charged is the failure to provide adequate safety devices. Shannahan, 204 N. Y. 543. 

b. Freedom from Contributory Negligence: Proof of a person's habitual conduct in respect to using proper care raises no inference that, at the time of an injury, he was exercising proper care, and hence is inadmissible. Parsons, 205 N. Y. 226.

This is the rule where there are eye-witnesses of the accident (Zucker, 205 N. Y. 50), and also, it seems, where there are no such witnesses. 15 

A. L. R. 125, note. 

3. Criminal Cases: A person may not be shown to be guilty of a particular crime by proof that he had committed similar ones at about the same time. Peo. v. 

Dudenhausen, 195 N. Y. 554. This general rule is subject to exceptions to be noted later. 

C. Capacity: 

1. Generally: A party's capacity or lack of capacity to do an act is always admissible, especially in criminal cases. Thus accused may show that he was physically incapable of committing the crime charged. Peo. v. 

Galbo, 218 N. Y. 283 (legless cripple charged with brutally killing a strong man after a fierce fight). 

2. Means: Where a party possesses the means to do a certain act, the probabilities are stronger that he did such act than where he lacks such means, and vice versa. Thus lack of financial means, as to make a loan, may be shown. Pontius, 82 N. Y. 339. Ordinarily, possession of financial means does not give rise to an inference of payment. But where plaintiff seeks to defeat a defense of payment by proving

defendant's inability to pay, defendant may rebut by proof that at the time in question he was financially able to pay. Dick, 188 N. Y. 426. Possession of peculiar means adapted to commission of a crime, as for instance, the very poison from which the victim of a homicide died, may give rise to an inference that the possessor committed the crime. Peo. v. Molineux, 168 N. Y. 264. But mere possession of ordinary tools suitable to commission of a certain crime may not be shown to raise such an inference, in absence of other evidence connecting the possessor with the crime. 

Sorenson, 168 Fed. 785.

D. Conduct: 

1. As Indicative of Pre-Arranged Plan: When a pre conceived or pre-arranged plan is evidenced by conduct, the latter is admissible to show the existence of the plan. Thus, on a trial for murder committed with a revolver, it is competent, as tending to show preparation, to prove that five days before, accused had possessed himself of a revolver. Peo. v. Scott, 153 N. Y. 40; Peo. v. Tice, 131 N. Y. 651 (evidence held admissible that on the day before a homicide defendant was seen sharpening a knife, similar to the one used in the killing). Threats and threatening conduct are admissible to show a pre-conceived plan and also to aid in determining the aggressor in a homicide or assault case. Peo. v. Taylor, 177 N. Y. 

237. Such threats need not be communicated to defendant. 

2. As Indicative of Motive: 

a. Generally: While proof of motive (as distinguished from criminal intent) is not necessary in a criminal prosecution (Peo. v. Sanducci, 195 

N. Y. 371), and may properly be excluded where the commission of the crime is undisputed (Peo. v. Farmer, 194 N. Y. 272), nevertheless, proof of motive tends to weaken the presumption of innocence and to corroborate evidence of guilt (Peo. v. Kennedy, 39 N. Y. 245) and is always admissible where defendant disputes the commission of

the crime. Thus, on trial of defendant for murder of his wife, his relations with a paramour may be shown, as tending to show motive for getting rid of his wife. Peo. v. Scott, 153 N. Y. 40. But evidence of a mere temporary intimacy with a prostitute has been held inadmissible. Peo. v. Harris, 209 N. Y. 77. Where defendant denies commission of the crime, he may always show lack of motive. 

b. Other Crimes: Proof of commission of crimes other than the one alleged is admissible where it tends to prove motive. Peo. v. Molineux, supra. 

Thus evidence that defendant committed bigamy by running off with B's wife was held admissible as establishing motive on the trial of defendant for murder of B. Pierson v. Peo., 79 N. Y. 424. 

3. Conduct as Indicative of Guilt: Any conduct of an accused showing consciousness of guilt raises an inference of guilt, and may be considered by the jury for that purpose. Peo. v. Willett, 213 N. Y. 368.

Such guilty conduct may be evidenced by words indicating guilty knowledge; by falsehood or evasion (Peo. v. Conroy, 97 N. Y. 62); by re-action to threatened arrest, as by turning pale (Lindsay v. 

Peo., 63 N. Y. 143); flight, or by offer to make amends or restitution. Peo. v. Elston, 174 N. Y. S. 1 (offer by defendant to marry girl who accused him of rape held admissible). 

a. In Negligence Cases: Evidence of repairs made after an accident is not admissible, to show culpability (Framberg, 190 N. Y. S. 710), but is admissible to show ownership and control of the premises. Clapper, 131 N. Y. 382. 

4. Further Exceptions as to Other Crimes: In addition to showing motive, proof of the commission of other crimes, not charged in the indictment, is admissible to show: 

a. Criminal Intent (Guilty Knowledge): Particu larly in cases of larceny (Peo. v. Lovejoy, 55

N. Y. S. 543), receiving stolen property (Peo. v.

Marino, 271 N. Y. 317), forgery (Peo. v. Everhardt, 104 N. Y. 591), obtaining property by false pretenses (Peo. v. Levin, 194 N. Y. 554), passing counterfeit money, "repeating" at elections (Peo. v. Molineux, supra, Peo. v. Marrin, 205 N. Y. 275). In Peo. v. Marino, supra, it was held that where defendant is being tried for criminally receiving stolen automobiles, similar receipts from other thieves may be shown thus changing the rule in Peo. v. Doty, 175 N. Y. 164, restricting proof of similar receipts to the same thief. b. Absence of Mistake or Accident: As where accused pleads accidental poisoning. Peo. v. Molineux, supra. 

c. Common Plan or Scheme: As in a prosecution. 

for bribery (Peo. v. Duffy, 212 N. Y. 57) or subornation of perjury. Peo. v. Toledo, 135 N. Y. 

S. 49. But such other crimes must be so connected with the crime charged as to make them all parts of a single criminal scheme. Peo. v. Grutz, 212 

N. Y. 72 (test: "are there connecting unities of time, manner and circumstances"?). Cf. Peo. v. 

Sharp, 107 N. Y. 427. d. Identity: Of perpetrator of crime charged. Peo. v. Hill, 198 N. Y. 64. 

1. Case: Prosecution for murder. State offered evidence that D, a few days before the homicide, had burglarized a house and had stolen revolvers, with one of which he was apprehended. Held, admissible in order to show the perpetrator of the burglary and the homicide to be the same. Peo. v. Hill, supra. 

2. Case: Prosecution for assault, defense being alibi.

The State offered evidence that two weeks before the assault D had visited the complainant in his tailoring shop and had then maliciously destroyed goods belonging to the latter. Held, admissible. D's identity being involved, any proof tending to show that complainant was not mistaken in his identification was relevant and proper. Peo. v. Thau, 219 N. Y. 39. 

e. To Impeach Credibility of Witness whether De fendant or Not: See same title in chapter on

Examination of Witnesses.

f. Where Ingredient Element of Crime Charged: If proof of the prior conviction is necessary under the allegations of the indictment, it may be shown even though defendant has not "opened the door" and has not taken the stand as a witness.
