(Don't confuse this situation with the situation under the Baumes' law where the previous convictions are shown after conviction of the crime charged in the indictment for the purpose of imposition of punishment.) 

5. Conduct as Indicative of Mental Condition: A per son's conduct, as evidenced by specific acts, is competent on the question of his mental condition. Peo. v. Wood, 126 N. Y. 249. Thus a sudden mental shock may derange a person's mentality, and evidence of such a shock is properly admissible to show a psychological cause for sudden insanity, as in a prosecution of defendant for killing deceased on sudden information of adultery with defendant's wife. Peo. v. Wood, supra. Proof of the cause by sudden mental shock is collateral and should not be permitted to befog the main issue. Peo. v. Jung 

Hing, 212 N. Y. 407. a. Case: D, an illiterate laborer, was indicted for killing a physician, X, who had treated D's child. Defense-the homicide was unintentional and in the heat of passion brought on by D's belief that X had been negligent. In order to show premeditation, the state was permitted to prove by an expert that the treatment and dosage given by X to the child was correct. Held, error. The issue was D's belief as to the fact of X's negligence, not whether his belief was mistaken. Peo. v. Caruso, 246 

N. Y. 437. 

b. Case: Prosecution of D for murdering his wife. De fense-temporary insanity brought on by the wife's confession that she was pregnant by another man. State offered rebuttal evidence that at the time of such communication the wife could not have been pregnant. Held, such evidence was properly excluded, the fact in issue being whether the communication was made, not its truthfulness. Peo. v. Harris, 209 N. Y. 70. 

6. Condition or Qualities of Material Objects: 

a. Generally:

In order to prove the condition of a

given object at a given time, it is competent to show its condition, subsequently or previously, provided the conditions are substantially the same. Thus, in an action against a city for injury to a pedestrian caused by the fall of an awning, evidence that the awning had been inspected by a city inspector several years before and pronounced unsafe is admissible to show that the awning was out of repair and that the city had notice of it. Mansfield, 104 N. Y. S. 386. Likewise, in an action for injury to a miner, due to a fall of rock from the roof of the mine, it is error to exclude evidence that the mine was inherently dangerous, and that the owner should have known of such danger and taken precautions against the same. Arras, 105 N. Y. S. 440.

Again, evidence that water overflowed from a culvert upon a sidewalk for a long time prior to the accident is admissible in an action by one who fell upon the ice formed on the sidewalk in order to show the negligence of defendant city in maintaining a defective culvert. Woolsey, 155 N. Y. 573. b. Effect on Similar Occasions: Tendency or effect of objects may be proved by previous instances of manifestations under similar conditions. This rule is most frequently applied in cases of defective highways and sidewalks. Thus, in an action for injuries caused by a fall on a defective sidewalk, it is permissible to show that other persons had fallen on the same sidewalk on previous occasions, it being shown that the sidewalk was in substantially the same condition. Quinlan, 11 Hun 

217. Where, however, the time is too remote or the conditions too dissimilar, such evidence will not be admitted. Flansburg, 205 N. Y. 423, McKinney, 231 N. Y. 194. 

1. Case: P seeks damages because his wagon wheel

Iwas forced into a hole in the street at the side of a rail of D railroad, which hole was caused by D's neglect, in consequence of which the wagon was

tipped over and P injured. D defended on the ground that the accident was caused by a defect in P's wagon, causing an inclination thereof. D offered to show that the day after the accident, the wagon was almost upset together with a load thereon due to the defect in the wagon complained of by D. This evidence was excluded. Held: Error. Similar effect under similar circumstances, hence admissible. Hoyt, 118 N. Y. 399. 

2. Case: Proceeding by landlord to dispossess D, tenant. 

D counterclaims for failure of the landlord to heat' the premises. The landlord offered evidence that there was a central heater and that there was sufficient heat supplied to other tenants on the same floor who never complained. Held: Admissible. Similar effect under similar circumstances. Brendan Realty Co., 173 N. Y. 378. c. Negative Evidence: In New York, it is competent to show that no similar accident had occurred, although the object or place causing the injury had been in the same condition for a long time. In such case, negligence cannot be charged because the possibility of accident was not foreseen. Hubbell, 104 N. Y. 434. 

d. Fires Caused by Locomotives: Where a par ticular locomotive is identified and charged with setting the fire, evidence of spark emissions from that engine on previous occasions is admissible. 

If the evidence relates to subsequent occasions, to render it admissible, it must appear that the engine was in the same condition as at the time of the fire. Collins, 109 N. Y. 243. Where plaintiff is unable to identify the particular engine which caused the fire, the present rule is that, if the negligence charged is that the locomotive was out of repair rather than of improper construction, evidence that other engines of similar design emitted sparks can have no probative value and hence is inadmissible. Chandler, 124 N. Y. S. 1046.

CHAPTER IV. 

. 

I. Admissions: 

A. Definitions and Distinctions: An admission is the voluntary acknowledgement by a party, or his agent, of the existence or truth of certain facts. Roosevelt, 40 N. Y. S. 

381. Such acknowledgement may be by statement, oral or written, or by conduct. Reed, 160 N. Y. 330. An admission is properly receivable as affirmative evidence (Peo. v. Cascia, 181 N. Y. S. 855); and is competent on the question of corroboration in a rape prosecution. 

Note that the best evidence rule applies to admissions, (Sherman v. Peo., 13 Hun 575) but, generally speaking, does not apply to confessions except in the case of a written confession before a magistrate. 

1. Distinguished from Confession: A confession is a declaration by an accused that the charge against him is true. (Defendant confesses his guilt, but he admits facts from which guilt may be inferred.) 

2. Distinguished from Declarations against Interest: 

Chief points of difference are: a. Admission may be used only against a party or his privies in interest; declaration against interest may be used against anyone. (Cf. Kwiatkowski, 276 N. Y. 126.) b. Admission may be used even if the maker is alive and present at the trial; declaration, only if declarant is dead. c. Admission may be made at any time; declaration must be made at time when there was no motive to misrepresent (not necessarily ante litem motam). d. Admission need not be against maker's interest at time made if it is so at time of trial; declaration must be against declarant's pecuniary or proprietary interest at time made.

B. No Foundation Need Be Laid: An admission may be given in evidence without a prior warning to the party 45

even though self-contradictory. Mindlin, 189 N. Y. S. 265.

C. Weight for Jury: The weight and probative value of an admission is for the jury. Gangi, 227 N. Y. 452. 

1. Generally: Admissions may be judicial, i.e. made on the record or in connection with legal proceedings, or extra-judicial. 

2. Judicial Admissions: May be subdivided into formal and informal. a. Formal: Formal judicial admissions are more part of the trial procedure than connected with the introduction of evidence. They are conclusive of the facts admitted. Curtis, Evid., p. 153; 

Potter, 136 N. Y. 384, (e.g.: stipulation in writing, agreed statement of facts, confession of judgment, or facts admitted by the pleadings, or in open court by party or his attorney). b. Informal: One incidentally made by a party, during course of trial or proceeding, in affidavit, deposition, pleading, or while on witness stand. 

1. Pleadings in Another Case: Admission con tained in former pleadings is competent against the pleader or his personal representative on trial of another action (Hutchins, 140 N. Y. 

115; Miller, 15 Abb. N. C. 204), provided it was made by the party personally (Cook, 44 N. Y. 156), and stated as a fact and not merely on information and belief. Reno, 226 N. Y. 546. a. Stipulations in Another Case: Are admissible only if made personally by the party, or by his attorney under his immediate direction. Elting, 2 Johns. 157. 

b. Testimony in Another Case: Statement made by a person while a witness in one case may be used against him in a subsequent action to which he is a party (Whiton, 88 N. Y. 299), even if the other parties are different. Kaht, 144 N. Y. S. 644 (where wife in a suit against her husband testified that she bought goods on her own credit. Held, such admission is competent against her in a subsequent action against her to recover for the goods).

c. Admissions in Affidavits and Depositions: When made in a pending cause, are admissible on trial of that cause de bene esse. Meyer, 20 N. Y. S. 

705. If made in a previous cause, it must appear that they were made on declarant's own knowledge or information, and that he is a party to the subsequent cause. Phillips, 98 N. Y. S. 423. 

3. Extra-Judicial Admissions: Any and all admissions not made in course of judicial proceedings. 

a. Implied from Conduct: Inconsistent conduct is admissible against a party as an admission that a fact is not what he claims it to be. Thus, it may be shown that a party, instead of disputing a claim against him, tried to adjust it on favorable terms. Slingerland, 12 N. Y. S. 647. So, receipt of sick benefits impliedly admits that the recipient was sick. Seidenspinner, 175 N. Y. 95. But the fact that defendant discharged a motorman after an accident cannot be shown as an admission of negligence (Engel, 203 N. Y. 321) or that defendant made repairs after the accident. 

1. Flight: Or an attempt to flee is admissible to show consciousness of guilt. Peo. v. Sharp, 107 N. Y. 427. But failure to flee or attempt to do so is not evidence in accused's favor. Peo. v. Rathbun, 21 Wend. 509. b. Implied from Silence: Where there is an obligation to speak, or where the circumstances are such as to prompt a denial, if the charge is false, an adverse inference may be drawn from silence. 

Peo. v. McCue, 178 N. Y. 579; Gibney, 34 N. Y. 

301. It must appear that the person actually heard and understood the statement which he failed to deny (Peo. v. Koerner, 154 N. Y. 355) and that he had full opportunity to reply. Peo. v. Kennedy, 164 N. Y. 449. In New York, it has been held that the fact that the accused is at the time under arrest is no objection to admissibility of his admission (Peo. v. Cascia, 181 N. Y. S. 855) except where he keeps quiet on advice of counsel. 

However, more recently, in Peo. v. Rutigliano,

261 N. Y. 103, and in Peo. v. Dolce, 261 N. Y. 

108, our Court of Appeals adopted the Mass. rule (Comm. v. McDermott, 123 Mass. 440) that silence in the face of accusation does not give rise to an implied admission where the accused at the time is under arrest, the situation then being similar to a judicial proceeding. See Peo. v. Lewis, 238 N. Y. 1. 

c. Implied from Prevarication: Where a party or an accused makes false statements in regard to material matters, an adverse inference arises against him (Peo. v. Wilkinson, 14 N. Y. S. 827) as in other cases of fabrication or spoliation. 

d. Unanswered Letters and Bills: Failure to answer a letter does not give rise to an implied admission as to the truth of the statements contained therein, since there is no duty upon the addressee to reply. Learned, 97 N. Y. 1. However, semble, where good faith requires that the addressee state his position frankly so that the addressor be not misled, acquiescence may be inferred from non-denial. Thomas, 141 N. Y. 506. Failure to return or object to a bill or statement sent by the creditor to the debtor, within a reasonable time, is competent evidence (but rebuttable) that the account is correct. In such case of non-return or non-objection within a reasonable time, the creditor may sue on an account stated. Lockwood, 18 N. Y. 285; Bradley, 218 N. Y. 351. 

e. Not Implied from Offer to Settle or Compromise: 

No implied admission of liability results from an offer of settlement or compromise made during the course of negotiations for same. Smith, 130 

N. Y. 677. But an express, unconditional, nonhypothetical admission of an independent fact, although made during the course of such negotiations, is admissible. White, 102 N. Y. 661 (holding, "the rule is well established, in this country, that the admission of a distinct fact which in itself tends to establish a cause of action or defense, is

not rendered inadmissible from the circumstance that it was made during discussion relating to a compromise, unless it is expressly stated to be made without prejudice; but if the admission is of such a nature that the court can see it would not have been made except for the purpose of producing the objects of the negotiation, and under an agreement that could fairly be implied from the circumstances that it was not to be used ⚫ afterward to his prejudice, it is not error for the court to exclude the evidence. The rule referred to is founded upon policy, and with a view of encouraging and facilitating the settlement of legal controversies by compromise"). 

f. To Pay Damages in Accident Case: An offer to pay damages for personal or property injuries, in reply to a demand for same, is admissible as evidence of liability. Brice, 108 N. Y. 428; Faurie, 205 N. Y. 526. But the rule is otherwise, if offer to pay is voluntary. Grogan, 211 N. Y. 30 (employer's voluntary offer to pay injured employee's medical expenses held inadmissible). g. Offers to Compromise Under C.P.A.: See same title Berk. Practice. 

E. Against Whom Admissible; Privies: 

1. Agent and Principal; Employee and Employer: "The admissions or declarations of an agent subsequent to a transaction in controversy, or after his agency has terminated, are not binding upon, or evidence against, his principal. But when the admission or declaration is made at the time of the transaction, or during his employment, when it pertains to the matter in hand, as agent, which is within the scope of his employment, his admissions and declarations are competent though not conclusive upon his principal." Stecher, 175 N. Y. 124. The same rule applies to the employee. Keeler, 33 N .Y. 648; Eastern Dye Works, 234 N. Y. 441. a. Case: After an accident D's motorman stated (but not so as to constitute part of the res gestae) that he couldn't stop the car because the brakes were bad. Held, inadmis-

50 

sible. The declaration is narrative merely, seeking to throw the blame from the employee to the employer and is no part of the employee's act for which P seeks to hold the employer liable. Luby, 17 N. Y. 131. Cf. Vadney, 177 N. Y. S. 114; Nowack, 166 N. Y. 433, Molino, 186

N. Y. S. 742 (running away of operator after accident held inadmissible). b. Case: X, a nurse, employed by D, states, two days after the occurrence, that she had negligently administered the wrong medicine to P, a patient. Held, inadmissible. The declaration is narrative, not within the scope of the employment and not within the employee's authority to make. Archibald, 201 N. Y. S. 86. c. Case: X, an employee of D, charged with the duty of making such reports, makes and sends to the Industrial Commission, an employer's report of injury sustained by P, an injured co-employee. The report contains admissions of liability. Held, admissible. Anthus, 185 N. Y. S. 

314, aff'd 231 N. Y. 557. See also Craciola, 253 N. Y. S. 752. d. Case: P, prospective customer fell and was injured on the front steps leading to D's restaurant. P offered testimony that after the accident D's manager said to a bus boy, "I thought I told you to take care of those steps." Held, inadmissible, since the manager had no power to bind D by his statement. Golden, 278 N. Y. S. 385. e. Case: P slipped and fell in one of D's chain stores. She said, "Clean it up before someone else falls." The manager replied, "I told some one to clean it up." Held, conversation inadmissible to prove D's negligence. Cook, 268 

N. Y. 599. f. Proof of Agency: Declarations of an alleged agent, made out of court, are inadmissible to prove the agency, since that would merely "beg the question." Shesler, 100 N. Y. 

S. 286. But where an agent's authority is undisputed, his declarations may be received to prove the fact. First Nat. 

Bank, 104 N. Y. S. 1040; aff'd 194 N. Y. 580. 

2. Husband and Wife; Parent and Child: Since no agency exists between husband and wife, or parent and child, merely by virtue of the relationship (Lelong, 187 N. Y. S. 150), admissions of one spouse against the other, or admissions of parent against child, or of child against parent are inadmissible, unless actual agency is established. Bonton, 170 N. Y. 

554; Lormore, 60 Barb. 62. 

3. Stockholder: The admission of a stockholder is in-

admissible as evidence against the corporation, unless made in a transaction in which the stockholder acted as an authorized agent of the corporation. 

Wallace, 198 N. Y. S. 60. Cf. Tompkins, 188 N. Y. 261. 

4. Partners: Admissions of one partner as to matters within the scope of the partnership business are binding on his co-partners on principles of agency, but statements of one alleged partner made out of court, are inadmissible to establish the partnership. 

Franklin, 130 N. Y. S. 47. (Each partner is the general agent of the partnership for partnership purposes. Part. L., sec. 20, subd. 1.) 

5. Principal and Surety: Admissions made by the principal in relation to the res gestae of the suretyship are competent against the surety. Griffin, 131 N. Y. 

S. 450. a. Case: D became surety on X's stock margin account with 

P, broker, and executed a mortgage to P to secure payment of any balance found owing from X to P. P now seeks to foreclose same and the only question involved is the amount of the balance. P offered in evidence a letter from P to X, written after the closing of the account, stating the balance due to P, which letter X had endorsed as being correct. Held, inadmissible. X's admission was made after all the transactions for which D had agreed to become surety had ceased, hence, not being part of the res gestae, it was not binding on D. Hatch, 65 N. Y. 489. 

6. Joint Debtors: Admission by one joint debtor does not bind the others, unless authorized or ratified by them, since the joint liability does not, ipso facto, create an agency. Wallis, 81 N. Y. 164. Thus a payment by one joint debtor does not revive, as to the other joint debtors, a claim barred by the statute of limitations, unless there was previous authorization or subsequent ratification by the others. McMullen, 89 N. Y. 456. 

7. Joint Tort-feasors and Co-Conspirators: There is no such joint interest between joint tort-feasors as to permit admissions or declarations of one to be used against the others, unless made as part of the res gestae. Sparf, 156 U. S. 51. But where a conspiracy

has been proved, admissions of each co-conspirator, in furtherance of the common object, are admissible against the others. Peo. v. Storrs, 207 N. Y. 147.

Peo. v. Ryan, 263 N. Y. 298 (admissions subsequently made by two co-conspirators that a killing had occurred during the commission of an independent felony, held not binding on other co-conspirator not present at the killing). 

8. Insured and Beneficiary: Technically, there is no privity between the insured, and his beneficiary under a life insurance policy, where the policy is not payable to the insured's estate. Hence an admission by the insured, during his lifetime, tending to defeat the beneficiary's rights under the policy, is not admissible as against the beneficiary. But if at the time of making the prejudicial statement (e.g., indicating self-destruction) the insured had a proprietary interest in the policy (the right to exercise a reserved privilege of changing the beneficiary is sufficient) the declaration would be admissible as one against interest. Martorella, 264 N. Y. S. 751. 

9. In Action for Wrongful Death: Statements made by the deceased before his death showing his own negligence or otherwise exonerating defendant, are admissible, against the administrator or executor in the wrongful death action, although the latter are not in privity with the deceased (i.e. they represent the statutory beneficiaries). Sec. 132 D.E.L., as amended in 1935, permits a recovery in a death action, for medical aid, nursing and attention incident to the injury causing death, paid by the decedent, or by a person entitled to a distributive share of the money recovered, or for which the decedent or person is personally responsible, and for funeral expenses. Thus, in every death case, damages may include sums personal to the decedent, in which instance, his admissions would be competent for the defense. Personal injury actions no longer abate (D.E.L. 119, 120, Laws of 1935) but may be continued or brought by the executor or administrator of the deceased. In such actions the deceased's

admissions and declarations also are admissible. Such an action may be consolidated with a death action on motion of either party. It would then be impossible to exclude the testimony in one action and permit it in the other all before the same jury. If this could be done, it might and probably would result in inconsistent verdicts. Kwiatkowski, 276 

N. Y. 126. 

10. Privies in Title: 

a. Former Owner of Personal Property: Admissions of a former owner of a chattel or chose in action are incompetent to affect the title of a subsequent owner (Merkle, 165 N. Y. 21; Paige, 7 Hill 361), unless the latter claims through him as executor, administrator, trustee in bankruptcy, etc. Staley, 177 N. Y. S. 112; Paige, supra. 

b. Former Owner of Real Property: A different rule applies in case of real property. Declarations of the former owner, made while still in title, are competent as admissions against those claiming under him. Spaulding, 35 N. Y. 204. The only restriction on this rule is that such admissions, if parol, are inadmissible to defeat a record title. by deed. Hutchins, 98 N. Y. 56. 

F. Against Whom Admissible; Parties to the Record: 

1. General Rule: Their admissions are always admissible against them. Mindlin, 189 N. Y. S. 265. 

2. Co-parties: If plaintiff sues B and C on a separate obligation, admissions of B affect only himself to the same extent as if he were sole party defendant. 

Whaples, 96 N. Y. S. 323. a. In Divorce Action: It has been held error to receive in evidence an extra-judicial admission. by defendant confessing adultery with the corespondent, since such admission, while competent against defendant, was incompetent to prejudice the interests of the co-respondent, although the offense was joint, and one could not be guilty and the other innocent. Scully, 166 N. Y. S. 464. b. Joint Owners: Admissions of a joint owner of

real or personal property are admissible against the other owners when all are parties to the record. Hayes, 234 N. Y. 230. Where, however, the co-owners are merely tenants in common, admissions of one affect only himself, although all are parties to the record. Naul, 78 N. Y. S. 101. c. Joint Obligors: Admissions of one jointly liable on a contract are admissible against his coobligors when all are sued on the common obligation (Shirk, 79 N. Y. S. 225), provided the admissions relate to the subject matter of the joint liability. Wallis, 81 N. Y. 164. d. Parties in Will Probate: Since a will cannot be probated as to certain beneficiaries and denied probate as to others, admissions of one beneficiary tending to show invalidity of the will are incompetent as against both him and the other beneficiaries, since it would be impossible to restrict the effect of the admissions to the beneficiary who made them. Kennedy, 167 N. Y. 163.

Of course, if the beneficiary making the admissions is the sole devisee or legatee, they are admissible against him. Campbell, 173 N. Y. S. 753. 

e. Nominal Parties: Statements by a nominal party in interest are not received as against the party beneficially interested. Eberhardt, 10 Ab. N. C. 

374. Thus admissions by a guardian ad litem are not competent against the infant plaintiff. Geraty, 44 N. Y. S. 659, aff'd 160 N. Y. 658. Admissions of executors or administrators, acting in their representative capacity are admissible against the estate (Simon, 213 N. Y. 589) if made by all the executors or administrators, or by the sole one. 

Spencer, 62 N. Y. S. 826.

F. Admissions May Be Founded on Hearsay: Unlike, the case of a confession, an admission may be based on hearsay. Reed, 160 N. Y. 330 (where defendant, in a negligence action against him, admitted that the accident was caused by failure to set a "dog" of a machine,

although he himself was not present at the accident. 

Held, by thus adopting the explanation of others as to the cause of the accident, without stating the sources of his information, defendant is bound by his statement). 

Rudolph, 251 N. Y. 208 (where plaintiff, in an action on a life insurance policy, filed as proof of death, a certificate of an attending doctor containing statements as to cause of death and previous illnesses. Held, such statements, although hearsay, are competent as admissions against plaintiff because he adopted them as his own). 

See also Davison, 278 N. Y. S. 167.

II. Confessions: 

A. Definition: A confession is an express acknowledgment of guilt of the crime charged or of some essential part thereof. Peo. v. Mondon, 38 Hun 197.

B. Requisites of Admissibility: 

1. Must be Express: The confession must expressly incriminate defendant as to the very crime charged. 

Peo. v. Mondon, supra. Confession of another crime would not be admissible. Peo. v. Bills, 114 N. Y. S. 587. a. Form: Is immaterial; the confession may be oral or written; it may be in form of a letter or a diary. Peo. v. Giro, 197 N. Y. 152; Willett, 27 

Hun 469, aff'd 103 N. Y. 587. (In Peo. v. 

Hayes, 71 (2nd) Pac. Rep. 321, a sound motion. picture of defendant making a confession to police officers was held admissible.) But if accused has signed a written confession on a preliminary hearing before a magistrate (C. Cr. Proc. secs. 

198-200), evidence of an oral confession is inadmissible. 

2. Must be Complete: If any part of a confession is received, the whole of it must be allowed, as, for instance, the entire conversation in which the confession occurred. Peo. v. Cosmides, 117 N. Y. S. 718, aff'd 198 N. Y. 566. 

3. Must be Voluntary: 

a. Generally: A confession of a defendant, whether in course of judicial proceedings or to a private

person, may be given in evidence against him, unless made under influence of fear produced by threats, or under a stipulation of the district attorney that he shall not be prosecuted therefor. 

Code Cr. Proc., sec. 395. Confession to a sheriff or police officer, or to the district attorney, is to a "private person" within the statute. Peo. v. 

Randazzio, 194 N. Y. 147; Peo. v. Rogers, 192 

N. Y. 347. 

(1) Results of Involuntary Confession: Evidence of facts ascertained as a result of an involuntary confession is admissible although the confession itself is not. Duffy v. Peo., 26 N. Y. 588. b. Fear Produced by Threats: A threat in and of itself does not invalidate a confession unless it appears that the nerve and judgment of the accused may have been so affected as to cause him to misrepresent the truth. Thomas, 3 Park. Cr. 268. (1) Illustrations: A threat to put accused in the "dark room" of the jail has been held sufficient to induce a confession through fear. Peo. v. 

Rankin, 2 Wheel. Cr. 467. So, fear of violence from mob or private person if a confession is not made is sufficient to invalidate the confession. Peo. v. Meyer, 162 N. Y. 357. But mere advice to tell the truth (Peo. v. Randazzio, 194 N. Y. 147), or a threat that unless confession is made, accused will be punished for his crime (Peo. v. McClellan, 103 N. Y. 587) is not sufficient to invalidate a confession. Nor will persistent questioning for the purpose of obtaining a confession invalidate a confession so obtained (Peo. v. Kennedy, 159 N. Y. 346), unless applied under "third degree" methods. 

Wan, 266 U. S. 1. The fact that police officers kept defendant, a police prisoner, an unreasonable length of time (thirty-six hours) before arraigning him, does not, of itself, invalidate a

57 confession obtained from defendant while such a police prisoner, but may be considered by the jury in weighing the voluntariness of the confession. Peo. v. Alex, 260 N. Y. 425. (2) Confession before Magistrate: Is inadmissible unless the magistrate first warns accused that he is under no obligation to make a statement, but that, if he does, anything he says may be used against him. C. Cr. Proc. secs. 196, 198200. (3) Confession to Officer: While under arrest, is not invalidated by failure so to warn the prisoner. Peo. v. Randazzio, supra. c. Promise of Immunity: In New York, a false promise of immunity is sufficient to render a confession inadmissible only if made by the district attorney. C. Cr. Proc. sec. 395; Peo. v. 

Chapman, 224 N. Y. 90. 

1. Case: A confession made to a detective, employed by the district attorney, upon a promise of immunity made by the detective, is admissible unless such promise was authorized or ratified by the district attorney. Peo. v. Kurtz, 42 Hun 335. 

2. Case: Where, however, a prison chaplain, in the district attorney's presence, told accused that a confession would not be used against him, the silence of the district attorney was held to have ratified the chaplain's promise, so as to render the confession inadmissible. Peo. v. Reilly, 224 N. Y. 90. d. Deception: Employment of trick or artifice to obtain a confession is not sufficient to exclude it. 

Peo. v. Scott, 195 N. Y. 224 (holding admissible a confession made to a private person, who promised to allow accused to escape, although the district attorney knew of the deception, but did not promise immunity).

C. Challenging Admissibility of Confessions: Where the prosecution offers a confession in evidence, defendant may object to it as having been improperly obtained, in which case, the court must allow him to show the specific facts before ruling on the admissibility of the confession. Peo. v. Kenney, 202 N. Y. 389; Peo. v. Weiner,

248 N. Y. 118. The evidence should be taken before the jury. If the facts are disputed, the voluntariness of the confession is a question of fact for the jury under proper instructions from the court; burden of proof being on the prosecution to show that it was voluntary. Peo. v. 

Cassidy, 14 N. Y. S. 349, aff'd 133 N. Y. 612. Even if the confession is admitted, the jury may refuse to give it weight, because of the circumstances under which it was obtained. Peo. v. White, 17 N. Y. Cr. 538. 

D. Impeaching Confession After Commission: Where a confession has been admitted, as voluntarily made, defendant may impeach it by showing: 

1. That he was under the influence of drugs or liquor at the time it was made. Peo. v. Kent, 83 N. Y. S. 948. 

2. That he was of low mentality, or weak mind, and did not appreciate the effect of what he said. Peo. v. 

Joyce, 233 N. Y. 61. 

3. That the facts stated in the confession are untrue. 

Peo. v. Fox, 3 N. Y. S. 359. 

4. That the veracity of the witness, to whom it was allegedly made, is doubtful. Peo. v. Wilcox, 245 

N. Y. 404.

E. Pleas of Guilty as Constituting Confessions: 

1. Before Magistrate: Rule of voluntariness applies to a confession made in course of examination before committing magistrate or coroner. Peo. v. Ferola, 215 N. Y. 285; Peo. v. Mondon, 103 N. Y. 211. But if accused is warned as to his rights (C. Cr. Proc. secs. 196, 198-200), a confession of guilt made before a magistrate is competent against him on his trial, but it is not conclusive and must be corroborated. 

Heim, 47 App. C. (D.C.) 485. 

2. To Indictment: Here a different rule applies. A plea of guilty, if accepted, recorded and not withdrawn, is conclusive, and the court must pass sentence thereon. C. Cr. Proc. secs. 471, 717. If such plea is withdrawn with the court's permission, it is admissible nevertheless, on the criminal trial as if it had been an extra-judicial confession. Peo. v. Steinmetz,

240 N. Y. 411. Moreover, the plea of guilty to the indictment is admissible as an admission in a subsequent civil action brought against defendant for the crime (e.g., larceny). Peo. v. Daiboch, 265 N. Y. 

125. Note that no conviction may be had on a plea of guilty only where death is the punishment. C. Cr. 

Proc. sec. 332.

F. Confession by Third Person: On trial of defendant on a criminal indictment, he cannot offer in evidence a confession by B, to the crime charged, B not being a party to the indictment. Such confession is hearsay and inadmissible, unless B is made a witness for the defense. 

Peo. v. Schooley, 149 N. Y. 99.

G. Evidentiary Weight of Confessions: 

1. Generally: A confession voluntarily made is said to be the highest form of evidence, but extreme care must be taken to see that it was properly made. 

Peo. v. Joyce, 233 N. Y. 61. 

2. Necessity of Corroboration: C. Cr. Proc. sec. 395 

provides that a confession of an accused "is not sufficient to warrant his conviction without additional proof that the crime charged has been committed," (i.e., proof of the corpus delicti). The confession itself need not be corroborated. The only additional proof required is that "the crime charged has been committed." This proof need not be such as would be sufficient to sustain a conviction, but it must tend to prove (at least circumstantially) the commission of a crime and to implicate accused. Peo. v. Warner, 152 Misc. 607. Once the corpus delicti has been established, the agency which committed it may be proved by the confession. Peo. v. Joyce, supra.

CHAPTER V. 

. 

I. Hearsay Evidence: 

A. Definition: Broadly speaking, hearsay evidence is "that kind of evidence which does not derive its value solely from the credit to be given to the witness himself, but rests also, in part, on the veracity and competency of some other person." Greenleaf on Ev. (16th Ed.) sec. 

99. Thus, where a witness seeks to testify that he heard onlookers talking about a flagman's negligence at a railroad crossing accident, this is hearsay and is properly excluded, since the truth of the statement depends, in part, upon the veracity of the persons who made it, as well as upon the veracity of the witness who repeats it. Felska, 152 N. Y. 339.

B. Reason for Rule: The underlying principle of our system of jurisprudence is that all testimonial assertions should be given under oath and subject to test by crossexamination for the purpose of ascertaining whether the witness is telling the truth. So, the statement of a third person, who is not testifying and is therefore neither sworn nor subject to cross-examination, but whose statement is merely repeated by a witness, is hearsay and not admissible. Greenleaf on Ev. (16th 

Ed.) secs. 98, 99.

C. Illustrations: The rule applies to both oral and written words and includes even sworn statements. Brookman, 105 N. Y. 621. Thus, testimony as to an alleged conversation with the captain of a ship as to cause of damage to the ship is inadmissible. Miss. Corp., 237

N. Y. 1. On the same principle, whether work has been properly done cannot be established by the unsworn statement of a third party. German-American Ins. Co., 93 N. Y. S. 46, aff'd 185 N. Y. 581. Again, testimony that a waitress stated to plaintiff that defendant, her employer, had ordered her not to serve colored people, is hearsay, and inadmissible in an action for damages 60

under the Civil Rights Law. Robinson, 227 A. D. 208. 

Similarly, on the question of the sanity of A's mother, it is error to permit A to testify that he had received a letter from his mother's physician in which the latter wrote that the mother should be put in an asylum. 

Boschen, 224 N. Y. 356. Likewise, X, a policeman, should not be permitted to testify that A identified D as one of the parties to the crime. State v. D'Adama, 86 

Atl. 414.

D. Apparent Exceptions to Rule: 

1. Statements in Issue: There is a marked distinction between a statement, the making of which is in dispute, and a statement which relates to the facts in dispute. Where the making of a statement is in dispute, the testimony of a witness that such statement was made is original evidence of a fact and is not hearsay. Thus in a slander action any person who heard the defamatory statement may testify to that fact. Likewise, when plaintiff seeks punitive damages, defendant may disprove actual malice by proving the source of his information, and for this purpose it is competent to prove statements of third persons to defendant. Bishop, 233 N. Y. 446. Cf. 

Morey, 123 N. Y. 207. 

2. Statements Which Are Circumstantial Evidence: 

a. Generally: Statements may be original, circumstantial evidence of a fact in issue and are then admissible not to prove the existence or nonexistence of such fact, but to show the state of mind of the person making them, from which state of mind the fact in issue may be inferred. 

b. Sanity and Testamentary Capacity: Statements made by a person whose sanity is in issue may be repeated by any witness who heard them. 

Waterman, 11 N. Y. 157. Similarly, where testamentary capacity of a testator is in issue, declarations made by such testator at the time of the execution of the will and before or after (if not too remote) are admissible. Smith, 257 N. Y. 140 (where testator's declarations were held admis-

sible on question of undue influence). Cf. Re 

Dryer, 257 N. Y. S. 257. 

c. Intent: Declarations may be original circum stantial evidence of the declarant's intent. Thus, in an action on a life insurance policy, where accidental death or suicide was in issue, a declaration by insured, when last seen alive, that he was going on a business trip to a place near where his body was found, is admissible although hearsay. Landon, 60 N. Y. S. 188, aff'd 167 N. Y. 577.

But declarations of a savings bank depositor are not admissible to negative an intention to create a joint tenancy where the deposit is made in the names of declarant and another. Re Porianda, 256 

N. Y. 423. 

(1) Case: Prosecution for murder. Should D be per mitted to call X to testify that three years prior to the homicide X had seen the deceased with a pistol in her hand and that deceased then stated that she intended to commit suicide? Held, Yes. This testimony, though quite remote, is admissible on the issue of fact involving the question where deceased took her own life or whether her death was caused by D's act (i.e., admissible to prove intent). Peo. v. 

Conklin, 175 N. Y. 332. 

(2) Case: Prosecution of D for murdering his paramour. 

Are letters written by the latter to D indicating that she was pregnant by him and expected his support for the child admissible? Held, Yes. The contents of the letters are hearsay and cannot be given in evidence to prove the truth of the statements made therein, but although hearsay, the letters are admissible to show a motive on D's part for the murder. 

Peo. v. Sutherland, 154 N. Y. 345. See also Peo. v. 

Wood, 126 N. Y. 249. 

d. Reputation: General reputation of a person as made up from what is said and thought in the community, may be testified to as a fact. Walker, 122 Mass. 501. Thus X, a character witness, may state from his own knowledge what B's reputation is, although his statement may be based upon what he has heard others say. Peo. v. Van Gaasbeck, 189 N. Y. 408. In point of law a man's character is shown by his reputation.

ま ​e. Knowledge: Whether a person knew or did not know a certain fact may be shown by evidence of what he said. Swift, 63 N. Y. 186. 

3. Market Value: 

a. Personal Property: Generally, sales and public offers to sell, in places where the commodity in question is ordinarily dealt in, may be shown as evidence of market value. Whitney, 117 Mass. 

523. But private offers are inadmissible. Jones, 120 N. Y. 589. Qualified experts may state the market value of a given article, based on their own knowledge of specific sales, offers, or quotations, even though their information may be largely derived from commercial reports or from others in the trade. Watts, 207 N. Y. S. 493; 

Smith, 256 N. Y. S. 622. 

(1) Commercial and Stock Market Reports: Ac credited price-current lists, market reports, and lists showing bid and asked prices and sales on a given day are admissible. Virginia, 238 U. S. 202. 

(2) Newspaper Quotations: Formerly, newspaper quotations of the price of commodities were inadmissible on the question of market value unless the sources of information were shown to be credible. Whelan, 60 N. Y. 469. But by statutory amendment (C. P. A. 375a, added in 1934) "whenever the market price or value of any article, regularly sold or dealt in any regularly organized stock or commodity market, shall be in issue, reports published in newspapers or periodicals of general circulation purporting to be the reports of such markets shall be admissible in evidence," the sources of information bearing only on the weight to be accorded the lists. b. Real Estate: Offers of purchase and sale and actual sales are inadmissible to prove the market value of a specific parcel of real estate. Re 

Thompson, 127 N. Y. 463. The present market

value of real estate may be shown by the testimony of expert witnesses, whose knowledge of values of land in the vicinity may be tested by cross-examination as to specific sales and the prices brought on such sales; on re-direct, the expert may be further questioned as to such specific instances. Robinson, 175 N. Y. 219.

II. Real Exceptions to Rule: 

A. Generally: In certain cases, the introduction of original evidence is difficult because of the nature of the fact to be proved. Due to the necessity of the case, and because of special circumstances of reliability and freedom from fabrication, declarations as to the fact made by persons who cannot be witnesses are, in some cases, admissible. Greenleaf on Ev. (16th Ed.) sec. 114a.

B. Declarations of Testator as to Contents of Will: Where a will was lost or destroyed accidentally, the rule used to be that if due execution and accidental loss or destruction thereof were proven, it was proper to prove the contents by evidence of declarations made by the testator. Sugden, 1 Prob. Div. 154. Today, however, the only method by which the contents of an accidentally lost or destroyed will may be proved is by two credible witnesses or by one witness and a correct copy. 

Sur. Ct. Act., sec. 143. Note that the presumption is in favor of a destruction by testator animo revocandi. 

1. Declarations of Testator to Show Making of Will: 

"The great weight of authority in this state is to the effect that the declarations of a deceased person are not competent evidence either to prove that he has made a will or to prove the continued existence of a written will, unless made in connection with some act so as to be part of the res gestae. Declarations of the deceased tending to show that a written will has been revoked are open to the same objection, unless they accompany some act which the statute prescribes as a requisite of revocation." Matter of 

Kennedy, 167 N. Y. 163.

C. Declaration as to Pedigree: 

1. Generally: In order that hearsay declarations may

be admitted to establish pedigree, it is necessary that the issue upon which the testimony is offered be one of genealogy. Bowen, 74 N. Y. S. 101. A case is not one of pedigree merely because it may involve questions of birth, parentage, age or relationship. Where these questions are incidental, the pedigree exception to the hearsay rule does not apply. Eisenlord, 126 N. Y. 552. Bowen, supra (where letter of deceased brother was held inadmissible to prove age of insured). a. Case: Intestate opened a savings bank account "in trust for son, Thomas" and "in trust for son, John." Is proof of declarations of intestate made to friends and neighbors, that she, intestate, had no family or children, admissible to show that the trust beneficiaries were fictitious persons? Held, Yes. Pedigree is in issue directly, and all the requisite conditions exist for the application of the pedigree exception rule. Washington, 171 N. Y. 166. 

2. Rule Stated: Declarations relating to pedigree are allowed in actions in which pedigree is in issue, where the declarations were made ante litem motam, by a deceased person who was related by blood or affinity to the person concerning whom they refer. 

Re Whelan, 261 N. Y. S. 761. a. Ante Litem Motam: The declarations must have been made ante litem motam for it must appear that the declarant was under no controlling motive to misrepresent. Aalholm, 211 N. Y. 406. 

b. Death of Declarant: The declarant must be dead. If he were alive, there would be no need to admit his declaration since he could testify directly. Re Findlay, 253 N. Y. 1. However, there is dicta to the effect that if declarant were alive, but incompetent, or beyond the jurisdiction of the court, his declarations would also be received. Young, 165 N. Y. 385. 

c. Relationship of Declarant: Declarant must be related by blood or affinity to the family of which he speaks because it must be shown that he possessed adequate knowledge of the subject matter of his declaration. Young, supra. Re Wendel, 262 N. Y. S. 41. Only slight proof of the relation-

ship is necessary, but such proof must be independent of the declarations themselves. Aalholm, supra; Re Mosley, 247 N. Y. S. 520. The declarations need not be made upon declarant's personal knowledge. They may be based upon statements made to declarant by other members of his family. Eisenlord, supra. The conditions of adequate knowledge and relationship refer only to the declarant and not to the witness who heard the declarations. Arents, 156 N. Y. 1. (1) Case: Ejectment. P claims as the son and sole heir at law of the deceased. P calls X to testify that deceased, before his death, had told X that he, deceased, was married to L. It is undisputed that deceased and L never cohabited as man and wife. 

Is the declaration admissible under the pedigree exception to the rule against hearsay? Held, Yes.

All the requirements are present. Pedigree. is a direct issue, the declarant is dead, the declaration was made ante litem motam; and the declarant is related to the person (i.e., himself) of whom he speaks. In such case no special proof outside the declaration is required to show the relationship. 

Eisenlord, supra. (2) Case: P seeks to recover the property of K, escheated to the State. P claims as the half-brother of K who was the son of J. K. P testified that his mother, long since dead, had often told him that she was the second wife of J. K. P also called his nieces and nephews to testify that their deceased mother, P's sister, had told them the same story which she had heard from her mother. Were the declarations properly admissible under the pedigree exception? 

Held, No. Although all the other requirements are present, there is no independent evidence (outside of the deceased mother's declarations, themselves) of the declarant's (deceased mother) relationship to 

J. K. Aalholm, supra. 

3. What May be Proved: By pedigree declarations it is possible to establish marriage, or celibacy, steps of family ancestry and relationship. Chamberlain, 71 N. Y. 423. Peo. v. Tuthill, 163 N. Y. S. 843 (where declarant's statement that he had no relatives was held admissible to prove an escheat). Birth, death, (Hunt, 19 N. Y. 279); legitimacy and illegitimacy

may also be proved. Glaser's Estate, 273 N. Y. S. 860; 

Canjolle, 26 Barb. 177, aff'd 23 N. Y. 90. 

4. Form of Declarations: Pedigree declarations may be either oral or written. They may be bible entries (Hunt, supra), church records (Greco, 154 N. Y. S. 

306), baptismal and marriage certificates (Layton, 98 N. Y. S. 72); or even inscriptions on tombstones, family portraits, engravings on rings, etc. Greenleaf on Ev. (16th Ed.) sec. 114 (d).

D. Dying Declarations: 

1. Generally: The rule as to dying declarations applies in criminal prosecutions for homicide. Waldele, 19 Hun 69. It has also been extended to abortion cases, so that the dying declarations of the woman whose death is produced by any of the means set forth in the Penal Law (secs. 80-82) are now admissible. C. Cr. Proc. sec. 398a. With regard to other unlawful acts, though death result, such statements are inadmissible. Peo. v. Schiavi, 89 N. Y. S. 

564. "This exception to the general rule excluding hearsay evidence is founded largely on what is regarded as a public necessity, and the rule yields to the exception in order to protect the innocent and punish the guilty. As the subject of the homicide cannot testify, his unsworn statement of what happened to him is considered the best evidence attainable, and, hence, is admitted as legal evidence in order to prevent injustice, after all reasonable precautions have been taken to secure a truthful statement." Vann J. in Peo. v. Falletto, 202 N. Y. 494. 

2. Constitutionality of Rule: The objection that defendant in a criminal case has a right to be confronted with the witnesses against him has been overruled on the ground that a deceased declarant is not a witness within the constitutional provision. 

Peo. v. Corey, 157 N. Y. 332. 

3. Rule Stated: On a trial for homicide or abortion, the statement of the victim covering the actual details of the crime is admissible, provided, declarant made such statement while under a sense of im-

pending death, without any hope of recovery, and provided, declarant, if living, would be a competent witness. 

a. The Declarant Must Be In Extremis: He must honestly believe that death is imminent and that he has no chance of recovery. Peo. v. Del Vermo, 192 N. Y. 470. In such case, the law assumes that he will not stoop to falsehood. It is the condition of mind of the declarant which determines the question. If death is postponed longer than anticipated, it does not effect the admissibility of the statement. Peo. v. Chase, 29 N. Y. S. 376, aff'd 143 N. Y. 669 (where the fact that declarant died three days after his declaration was held not determinative of its admissibility). Conversely, if declarant believes he may live, his statement is not admissible, even though physicians tell him recovery is impossible. Peo. v. Chase, supra; 

Peo. v. Sarzano, 212 N. Y. 231. 

(1) Evincing Consciousness of Imminent Death: 

Declarant's fixed feeling of approaching death may be inferred from statements or from conduct. The following statements have been held to indicate a fixed feeling of imminent death: "Oh! My poor children! I am going to die. My poor family! I am going to die!" 

Peo. v. Del Vermo, 192 N. Y. 470. "I am going to die. I would like to live to show you what a good girl I could be, but I won't have the chance." Peo. v. Chase, supra. "I cannot live and want to make dying declaration." Peo. v. 

Sarzano, 212 N. Y. 235. Cf. Peo. v. Smith, 172 

N. Y. 210. The following conduct has been held to evince apprehension of approaching death: making final provision for a child (Peo. v. Stacy, 192 N. Y. 577) or final arrangement of affairs (Peo. v. Wood, 2 Edm. Sel. Cas. 71); or asking for and receiving last rites of church. 

Carver v. U. S., 164 U. S. 694. 

b. Declarant Must Be Competent Witness if He

Lives: If the declarant would not have been allowed to testify as a living witness against the defendant, because of some rule of law, his dying declaration is incompetent for the same reason. Donnelly, 26 N. J. L. 463. Moreover, the dying declaration must contain a statement of fact and not merely suspicion or belief. Peo. v. 

Shaw, 63 N. Y. 36; and if reduced to writing, the best evidence rule applies. (Note that the dying declaration may exculpate as well as incriminate. 

Mattox, 146 U. S. 140.) 

c. The Death Which Is The Subject Of The Charge Must Be The Death of The Declarant. This is important. Thus where A, and B, are convicted of the murder of X, and A just before his electrocution for his share of the crime, declares that B is innocent, such declaration cannot be admitted as a dying declaration to exculpate B, since a dying declaration, to be admissible as such, must be made by the victim of the homicide (X) for which the accused is on trial. Peo. v. 

Becker, 215 N. Y. 126. See also Peo. v. Schiavi, 89 N. Y. S. 564; Peo. v. Davis, 56 N. Y. 95. 

4. Admissibility and Weight: The admissibility of dying declarations is a question of law for the court. 

Peo. v. Becker, 215 N. Y. 126. But once having been admitted, the weight and probative value to be given is for the jury under proper instructions from the court. Peo. v. Knapp, 1 Edm. Sel. Cas. 177. The value of each declaration must rest upon its own facts, but it is never entitled to the same weight as primary proof. Peo. v. Smith, 104 N. Y. 491; Peo. v. 

Ludkowitz, 266 N. Y. 233; Peo. v. Kraft, 148 N. Y. 

631. Yet, it is not "the most suspicious kind of testimony." 

5. Form of Declaration: A dying declaration may be oral or in writing or may consist of accusing gestures. Thus in Peo. v. Madas, 201 N. Y. 349, declarant could not speak on account of a tube which had been placed in his windpipe to enable his respiratory

organs to function, after an operation on him necessitated by the wound inflicted. It was held competent to admit testimony that the declarant had answered questions as to defendant's guilt by nodding his head and pointing his hand, he being conscious and intelligent, comprehending what was said to him and realizing that he had no chance of recovery. The use of a printed form of ante mortem statement does not, of itself, render a dying declaration inadmissible, provided, the questions put to the declarant as to his consciousness of approaching death are not casual and perfunctory or too suggestive. Peo. v. Sarzano, supra; Peo. v. Kane, 213 N. Y. 260. 

6. Impeaching the Declaration: The dying declaration may be discredited by any means which may be used to discredit a living witness. For example, defendant may show that the declarant's reputation for truth and veracity was bad, or that declarant had been convicted of crimes. 16 A. L. R. 411 note. That declarant was a disbeliever in the future state of man after death, may also be proved by defendant, in order to show that declarant would not necessarily tell the truth in the face of death. State v. Rozell, 225 S. W. (Mo.) 931. Moreover, it is competent to show that the declarant's mental condition was such, at the time of making the dying declaration, that his recognition and identification of persons would be uncertain. Peo. v. Wilcox, 245 N. Y. 404; Peo. v. Shaw, 63 N. Y. 36. On the same principle, it would seem that it is competent to discredit the dying declaration by showing that the declarant had made previous inconsistent statements, (Carver v. U. S., 164 U. S. 

694; Maine v. Peo., 9 Hun 113), tending to show that the declarant's perceptive faculties or powers of reasoning were below normal. The witness who testifies to having heard the dying declaration made, is, of course, subject to impeachment like any other witness. Peo. v. Wilcox, 245 N. Y. 404.

E. Declarations Against Interest: 

1. Generally: To render a declaration against interest

admissible, it must appear to the satisfaction of the court that the necessity of the case demands it. 

Brewster, 2 Hill. 537. Where such necessity exists, the declarations are admissible in all actions, civil or criminal, provided the other conditions for admissibility exists. If declarant is dead, and hence unavailable as a witness, necessity is conclusively established. Lyon, 141 N. Y. 225. 

2. Rule Stated: Declarations, oral or written, made by a deceased while he was in a position to know of the matters stated, are admissible, provided, they were against his pecuniary or proprietary interest when made. a. Declarant Must Be Dead: In order for the rule to be applied in New York the declarant must be dead. N. Y. Water Co., 187 N. Y. 516. In other jurisdictions, the unavailability of declarant because of disability or absence from the jurisdiction is considered sufficient to warrant admission of his statement. 

b. Against Pecuniary or Proprietary Interest: The declaration must be against the pecuniary or proprietary interest of the declarant at the time when made. Since persons do not usually make statements in derogation of their own pecuniary interest, if they do, it is because they believe them to be true. McDonald, 62 N. Y. S. 764; Re 

McCafferty, 264 N. Y. S. 38. (1) Illustrations: Thus, it is against the pecuniary interest of a declarant for him to admit. that he is legally indebted to some other person (Swan, 34 N. Y. S. 829); that nothing is due him from a certain other person (Sparling, 49 N. Y. S. 321); that he received money from a certain person on a given day. Livingston, 56 N. Y. 507. But if the acknowledgment of receipt of payment is made after the debt has been barred by the statute of limitations, it is not a declaration against the declarant's interest but rather pro interest. Mills, 113

N. Y. 243. On the same theory, whether a declaration by A that he is not B's partner is a declaration against A's interest would depend on the solvency of the partnership. It is against the proprietary interest of a declarant to admit, while in possession of real property, that he has alienated it, or holds it as trustee; (Swan, supra) or that someone else owns it. 

Lyon, supra; Peo. v. Storrs, 207 N. Y. 147. (2) Although statements contrary to declarant's interest, other than pecuniary or proprietary, may have the same cause to be reliable, they are inadmissible. Donnelly, 228 U. S. 243 (holding that where declarations alleged to have been made would subject declarant to criminal liability, they are not admissible as declarations against interest). 

c. The Interest Must Be Actual: It is fundamental that the declarant's interest in respect to the subject matter of the declaration, at the time of making such declaration, must be actual and not contingent or prospective. Clason, 9 N. Y. S. 

609. The insistence upon such present interest as a requisite is placed upon the ground that if it existed, the declarant's motive to misrepresent would be materially lessened. Accordingly, after he has parted with his interest in certain property, the declaration of a former owner with respect to it, is inadmissible as a declaration against interest, for the reason, that the declarant had no interest to be antagonized at the time of making the declaration. Hutchins, 98 N. Y. 56. Moreover, not only must the declarant possess such an actual and present interest, but he must be aware that he possesses it, since otherwise, the inference that the declaration is true because of being adverse to such interest, could hardly arise. White, 1 E. 

D. Smith 493. However, in spite of some authorities to the contrary, it is not necessary that the declaration against interest be made ante litem

1 motam in order to preclude the existence of a possible motive to misrepresent. Therefore, the fact that such declaration was made after action begun is not controlling. Neither is it material that the declarant whose declaration is sought to be introduced as one against interest, is in privity with the litigants or a stranger to them. Livingston, 56 N. Y. 507.

F. Book Entries in Course of Business: 

1. Generally: This exception to the rule against hearsay comprises two classes of cases. The first, referred to as the shop book rule, considers book entries made by the parties to the action; the second, where the entries are made by non-parties, is often referred to as "entries in the regular course of business" rule. While the fundamental basis for these two rules, as exceptions to the hearsay rule, is the same, (viz., the necessity principle, since at common law a party interested could not testify in his own. favor), the conditions affecting their applicability are different. In either case, however, it is not necessary (in New York) that the party whose books are offered be dead or otherwise unavailable. In fact, he usually is present in court to identify and authenticate his books. Mayor of City of N. Y., 102 N. Y. 572. 

2. Shop Book Rule: 

a. Generally: Before the law was changed by statutory enactment (C. P. A. sec. 346) an interested. party could not testify in his own behalf. It was therefore virtually impossible for a shopkeeper who kept no clerk to prove a sale of merchandise. To relieve against this hardship, the courts early conceived the shop book rule. Case, 8 Johns. 

211. Although the disqualification of a party to testify in his own favor has been removed, the rule still exists and a party still has the right to introduce his account books as primary evidence. 

Smith, 163 N. Y. 168. The shop book rule affects the books kept by a "farmer, mechanic, profes-

sional man, or merchant." Tomlinson, 30 Barb. 

42; Rexford, 3 N. Y. S. 876. Since "it was founded upon a supposed necessity and was intended for cases of small traders who kept no clerks" (Smith, 131 N. Y. 169) it does not apply to the books of a corporation. Congdon Co., 42 N. Y. S. 255. The shop book rule and the "regular course of business entries" rule should not be confused with the rule that account books may be offered as auxiliary, documentary evidence to refresh the recollection of a witness; or if they fail to refresh his recollection, to be read in evidence, if the proper foundation is laid. Peck, 94 N. Y. 569. b. Rule Stated: Entries made in account books, kept by a party to an action, "are not evidence in the case of a single charge, because there exists, in such case, no regular dealing between the parties. They ought not to be admitted where there are several charges, unless a foundation is first laid down for their admission by proving that the party had no clerk, that some of the articles charged have been delivered, that the books produced are the account books of the party and that he keeps fair and honest accounts, and this by those who have dealt and settled with him." Vosburgh, 12 Johns. 461. 

(1) Entries Must Relate To Sales Or Services: 

The entries made in the shop book must relate to sales or services. Smith, 131 N. Y. 169 (holding that a cash loan may not be proved under the shop book rule). 

(2) Regular Course of Dealing: There must be a course of regular dealing between the parties. Proof of sale or delivery of merchandise, which constituted the sole transaction between the parties, is inadmissible under the rule. 

Vosburgh, supra. 

(3) The Party Must Keep No Clerk: "The clerk intended is one who had something to do with and had knowledge generally of the busi-

1 ness of his employer in reference to goods sold or work done." McGoldrick, 88 N. Y. 334.

Thus, a foreman, superintending each delivery of goods and putting it down every day on a slate from which plaintiff took them off daily and entered them in the account book, is not a clerk within the meaning of the rule, and the same is true of a salesman, bookkeeper, or temporary employee. McGoldrick, supra. Under modern business methods, it has been held that account books are receivable under the shop book rule where the party had three clerks, each of whom had special knowledge of one department, but none of whom had "general" knowledge of the business, "provided the knowledge of the clerks as to a part of the transactions recorded or as to the correctness of some of the entries, if available, is first exhausted." Shmargon, 182 N. Y. S. 343. 

(4) Some of the Articles Charged in the Account Book Must Have Been Delivered: Proof of delivery of a single item would be sufficient, but such proof would have to be independent of the account books themselves. Swan, 2 

Hilt. 422. 

(5) Books Must Be Original Entry Books. The books must be the account books of the party and the books in which the entries. were first permanently made. Winne, 36 N.

Y. S. 683. Thus, where entries are first made. upon memoranda sheets with intention of later transcribing them into a suitable book, the latter is considered the account book of original entry. Leask, 205 N. Y. 171. The records of a cash register are not considered books of account within the rule. Cullinan, 85 N. Y. S. 745. It has been held that books "of original entry" may be kept in ledger form and are admissible under the rule (Failey, 4 N. Y. S. 353) but where the account

has been merely copied or "posted" into the ledger, from day books or other books "of original entry", the ledger is not admissible. 

Clark, 2 N. Y. S. 408; Griesheimer, 124 N. Y. 

650, rev'g 8 N. Y. S. 582. 

(6) Party Must Keep Honest Books: The party producing the books must prove that he keeps fair and honest accounts by the evidence of persons who have dealt and settled with him. 

One witness is sufficient, (Smith, 163 N. Y. 

168) and the fact that such witness is also an employee of the party merely affects the weight of his testimony. Hurley, 87 N. Y. S. 924. 

c. Effect of Death of Adverse Party: C. P. A. 347, affecting the competency of an interested witness in an action against the personal representatives of a deceased person, or incompetent, does not prevent a party from putting in evidence his account books under the shop book rule, since it is the witness, and not the books, who is excluded under the statute. Young, 21 N. Y. S. 225. Nor does it preclude such party from taking the stand to lay the foundation for admission of such books providing he does not testify concerning the decedent's account (i.e., the "personal transaction" under C. P. A. 347). Bellows, 149 N. Y. S. 548: 

Botti, 254 A. D. 889. 

3. Entries in Regular Course of Business: 

a. Generally: The common law rule as to entries made in the regular course of business concerns book entries made by third persons who may or may not be in the party's employ. Thus, the records of a deceased attorney of proceedings in a matter handled by him are admissible in an action between the client and a third person. 

Leland, 31 N. Y. 115. Similarly, the records of a notary public whose testimony cannot be procured because of death, disability or absence from the jurisdiction, are admissible to prove due pre-

sentment, demand and notice of protest. C. P. A. 

368 subd. 2. b. Rule Stated: The common law rule is that in order for an entry to be admissible it must have been made in the ordinary course of business by a person whose duty it was to make it, contemporaneously with, or reasonably near the time. when the recorded transaction or event occurred, so as to form part of the res gestae, and the entrant must have had personal knowledge of the facts recorded by him, or such facts must have been communicated to him by one who had such personal knowledge and who was charged with the duty of making such communication. Mayor of City of N. Y., 102 N. Y. 572. 

(1) The Entry Must Be Made in the Regular 

Course of Business: This essential requirement is not complied with where it appears that the entry was made in the course of a business transaction which was not a part of the regular employment of the entrant. 

Peck, 94 N. Y. 569 (where an employer suspecting an employee of larceny, directed a co-employee to make a duplicate list of sales; the entries made by the co-employee were held not to be made in the regular course of business). However, it is not necessary that the entry be made in a purely commercial transaction. An entry made by a priest or minister, etc., in the course of his professional duty, such as in registers of births or marriages, is admissible. Fisher, 67 N. Y. 73; 

Wightman, 217 N. Y. 473. (a) Case: In order to prove the number of days' work performed in the repair of a street, D offered in evidence a time book and memorandum book kept by a foreman. The entries were made in these books by the foreman upon the report to him, twice a day, of the facts by the gang foremen. The foreman identified the entries, and the gang foremen testified that they correctly reported the facts to the fore-

man. Are the entries admissible? Held, Yes.

The court itself distinguished this case from the Peck case, supra, by the fact that the latter was the case of a mere private memorandum, while in this case, the entries were made and the books kept in the regular course of business. 

Mayor of the City of N. Y., Supra. 

(2) Entrant Must Be Under Duty to Enter: 

The entrant must have made the entry in the course of his regular duty, as distinguished from a mere casual entry. Mayor of 

City of New York, supra. 

(3) Entry Must Be Made as Part of Res Gestae: 

The entry must be made contemporaneously with, or reasonably near, the time when the recorded transaction or event occurred, so as to form a part of the res gestae. "There is no fixed rule with respect to what length of time may elapse between the fact chronicled and the entry thereof. The nature of the business, the usual methods of carrying it on, and the circumstances under which the entry is made, all must be considered." 

McKelvey on Ev. (2d. Ed.) sec. 183. 

(4) Personal Knowledge By Entrant: The en trant must have personal knowledge of the facts recorded, or else the facts must be communicated to him by one having such personal knowledge and duty-bound to make such communication. Mayor of City of N. 

Y., supra. (a) If the entrant dies after making the entry, it may be received in evidence, nevertheless, upon proof of the deceased's handwriting, and of the fact that the entry was made in the regular course of the entrant's employment. If the deceased was without personal knowledge of the facts recorded by him, his informant must testify as to them. Collins, 94 N. Y. S. 317. (b) If the informant dies, the entry may

1 nevertheless be admitted upon the evidence of the entrant that he made the entry in the regular course of business, as transmitted to him by the deceased. 

American Surety Co., 170 U. S. 133. 

c. Effect of C. P. A. 374a on Rule: The common law rule as to entries made in the regular course of business has been radically changed by C. P. A. 

374a. It is no longer a requisite to admission of entries that the entrant or his informant had personal knowledge of the facts recorded or that the entrant be under a duty to enter, or the informant to inform. Lack of personal knowledge may be shown to affect the weight of the entry but not the admissibility. The section provides: "Any writing or record, whether in the form of an entry in a book or otherwise, made as a memorandum or record of any act, transaction, occurrence or event, shall be admissible in evidence in proof of said act, transaction, occurrence or event, if the trial judge shall find that it was made in the regular course of business, and that it was the regular course of such business to make such memorandum or record at the time of such act, transaction, occurrence or event, or within a reasonable time thereafter. All other circumstances of the making of such writing or record, including lack of personal knowledge by the entrant or maker, may be shown to affect its weight, but they shall not affect its admissibility. 

The term business shall include business, profession, occupation and calling of every kind." (1) Where Applicable: C.P.A. 374a has been held applicable in the following, typical cases: in an action for rent, entries made in a ledger and monthly statements, prepared by P's agent who managed the property, are admissible as made in the regular course of business (Funk, 260 N. Y. S. 844); in an action to impress a trust, the minute and cash books of a religious congregation are similarly admissible. Zinaman, 285 N. Y. S. 20. In 

Warner Quinlan Co., 257 N. Y. S. 722, P's bookkeeper identified P's ledger showing the account with D. She

testified to the system which obtained in making the entries in the ledger and said they were posted from delivery slips bearing invoice numbers after the driver made delivery. The ledger was received in evidence, and 

P rested. It was held that a prima facie case was properly made out since the entries were admissible as primary proof under C.P.A. 374a. However, the section has been held inapplicable to admit in evidence a police blotter report of an automobile accident, made by a policeman, not a witness, but who got his information from others who were witnesses and who "volunteered" the information. Johnson, 253 N. Y. 124. Similarly, hospital records containing a report of an accident by a policeman, who was not a witness, are inadmissible under the section. Wolf, 254 N. Y. 598. To same effect see 

Gerocami, 291 N. Y. S. 500 (holding inadmissible a hospital entry made thirty minutes after an accident in which plaintiff was injured showing him to have been drunk when admitted to the hospital). Also, Stone, 271 

N. Y. S. 500. In a proceeding to dismiss a policeman for accepting gratuities for recommending clients to a lawyer, entries made by the lawyer or his clerk on cards kept as office records and notations on check stubs are inadmissible. Roge, 280 N. Y. 268. (In a proceeding to disbar the attorney the entries would be admissible against him as admissions.)

G. Declarations Which Are Part of Res Gestae: 

1. Generally: Literally, "res gestae" means the transaction or thing done. Declarations made at the time of such transaction or thing done by the parties participating therein are admissible as part of the res gestae. The test is the instinctive spontaneity with which they are uttered, and their reliability as evidence is based upon the psychological fact that they are the ex tempore effusions of the human mind at such a time, and under such circumstances, as negative the possibility of previous planning of false or misleading statements. Steph. Dig. Ev., art. 3; 

Waldele, 95 N. Y. 274. 

a. Time of Making: Where the declaration is spon taneous and coincident with the event so as to constitute an integral part of the res gestae, there is no question as to its admissibility (e.g., plaintiff, in falling from a trolley car because of the negligence of the guard in failing to let down a

folding step, exclaims "Yes, let down the step after I fall." Hutcheis, 128 Iowa 279). But where the statement is made sometime after the occurrence, the question of spontaneity becomes important. Peo. v. Curtis, 225 N. Y. 519. The criterion of admissibility is not so much the element of time as the instinctiveness and naturalness of the utterance, as distinguished from mere narration. Thus, a statement by a person injured and unconscious for eight days, made immediately upon regaining consciousness, was held admissible, since declarant had no opportunity for reflection. Britton, 110 Pac. 20. But in

Waldele, supra, where a deaf mute, discovered by his brother on the scene of the accident, a half hour after it occurred, told the story to him by means of signs, it was held inadmissible, although that was the first opportunity the declarant had of expressing himself. Cf. Greener, 209 N. Y. 

135; Peo. v. Del Vermo, 192 N. Y. 470; Martin, 103 N. Y. 626. In Handel, 277 N. Y. 548, the following declaration uttered by declarant one half minute after falling from an elevated railroad station was held inadmissible as part of the res gestae: "Save me, help me, why did that conductor close the door on me!" 

2. Declarations of Pain and Suffering: The doctrine of res gestae declarations finds its most frequent application, in civil cases, in declarations of pain and suffering. In New York, such voluntary statements are inadmissible unless made to a physician for the purpose of obtaining treatment; or made coincidentally with the injury so as to be part of the gestae; or where declarant has since died. But involuntary expressions of pain are always admissible. Orlando, 95 N. Y. S. 898; Tromblee, 173 A. D. 174. a. Rule in other Jurisdictions: Most other jurisdictions follow the rule which at one time existed in New York, to wit, that declarations of present pain and suffering may be proved by any one who heard them. Cashin, 185 Mass.

543; Caldwell, 11 N. Y. 416. Also, the New York rule making screams, groans, moans and other "inarticulate exclamations" always admissible has been criticized as being impracticable, since, "if a person is determined to falsify, it is as natural and feasible for him to lie with screams, groans and cries, as with articulate assertions of pain." Wigmore on Ev. sec. 1719. 

3. Declarations in Criminal Cases: The declarations of the victim of a crime, inspired by anger, despair, desire for revenge, or other strong emotion, made immediately after or during the commission of the crime, are admissible under the res gestae rule. Peo. v. Leonardo, 199 N. Y. 432; Peo. v. Del Vermo, supra, (where victim's declaration, "Del Vermo stabbed me with a knife," in response to "what happened?" was held admissible as part of the res gestae in the broadest sense of the term). But in 

Greener, supra, which was brought to recover damages for wrongful death, the deceased's statement, "my feet is broke; the ladder bent over," made in response to "what happened?" was held inadmissible. as narrative (i.e., the declarant speaking about the facts, not the facts speaking through declarant). 

The Greener case has since been followed by Peo. v. 

Sprague, 217 N. Y. 373 and Peo. v. Chapman, 181 

N. Y. S. 750. a. In Rape Cases: Prosecutrix, or any person who heard her, may testify to the fact that she made prompt complaint of the assault. Baccio, 41 N. Y. 

265 (if prosecutrix does not testify on the trial, evidence by another that she made prompt complaint is inadmissible). But such testimony is limited to the bare fact that timely complaint was made; testimony of the contents of the complaint should not be admitted. Peo. v. Friedman, 124 

N. Y. S. 521. Moreover, delay in making the complaint may rob it of any evidentiary value. 

Peo. v. Sullivan, 104 N. Y. 481 (where proof of disclosure of crime eleven months after its commission was rejected). 

4. Declarations by Agent or Servant: Where an agent's acts are competent to bind his principal, his

declarations and admissions in regard to the subject matter of these acts will also bind the principal, if made at a time so as to constitute part of the res gestae. Luby, 17 N. Y. 131. Such cases being exceptions to the general rule that what one person says out of court is not admissible to charge or bind another, it must appear, not only that the relation of principal and agent existed, but, also, that the agent, at that time, was engaged in the principal's business and that his declarations related to and were connected with, such business then pending, so that they constituted a part of the res gestae. 

Queens & Nassau Del. Corp., 273 N. Y. S. 199;
