Golden, 270 N. Y. 544. (See "Admissions of Agent," supra.) a. Note: Some authorities incline to the view that in the rape and agency cases just considered there is, ordinarily, no question of res gestae. Thayer Cas. Ev. 2d. Ed. pp. 

641-651. In the agency case, it is said, the question is one of admissions rather than of res gestae. In the rape cases, it is urged the fact of the disclosure being made is circumstantial evidence corroborative of the victim's direct testimony and is admissible as such and not under any res gestae argument. McKelvey on Evid., 2nd Ed., sec. 

210. But see U. S. v. Gooding, 12 Wheat (US) 460; 

Baccio, 41 N. Y. 265; Mutual Life Ins. Co., 145 U. S. 285.

H. Declarations Concerning Matters of Public Interest: 

1. Generally: The dangers arising from the use of hearsay testimony are minimized in the case of matters of public or general interest. All persons living in the neighborhood are likely to be conversant with local matters and what is dropped in conversation respecting common rights and liabilities, may be presumed to be true, because conflicting interests would lead to contradiction from others, if the statements were false. Reg. v. Bedfordshire, 4 El. & Bl. 535. 

2. Rule Stated: Declarations of deceased residents concerning the general or common reputation in the community of matters of public or general interest are admissible. 

a. Requirements:

(1) The Declarant Must Be Dead. Partridge, 

2 N. Y. S. 529. 

(2) The Declarant Must Have Knowledge of What He Speaks: If the declaration refers to matters of public interest, actual knowledge is not necessary (Drury, 127 Mass. 57) but where matters of general interest are concerned, the declaration is admitted only when means of declarant's knowledge is shown. McKinnon, 21 N. Y. 206. (a) A matter is public if it is common to an entire people; it is general if it is common to a single community, or to a con siderable portion thereof. 1 Greenleaf 

Ev. (15th Ed.) sec. 128. 

(3) The Declaration Must be Ante Litem Motam: 

This requirement is met by proof that the statement was made before the commencement of the controversy, as distinguished from the action itself. Partridge, supra. 

(4) Must be Based on Reputation: The declara tion must be based on general reputation and not on individual opinion or observation. 

(5) Must Relate to Matter of Public or General Interest: The declaration must relate to matters of public or general interest. Most often the rule is applied where public or private boundaries are in dispute. Public boundaries, for example, are those between different political divisions of a county, or of the county itself. Drury, supra. Private boundaries are usually matters of general interest (McKinnon, supra), as are the locations of well known objects with reference to such boundaries. Thus, where the question was as to which of two towns should be charged with the support of a pauper, and it appeared that the house of the pauper's grandfather stood on the boundary line between the two towns, hearsay declarations

were held admissible to show exactly how it stood, the question being one of general interest, although the boundary itself was not involved. Hall v. Mayo, 97 Mass. 418. See also Brenstein v. North American Realty Co., 119 N. Y. S. 1.

I. Testimony on Former Trial: 

1. Rule Stated: C. P. A. sec. 348 provides, in effect, that testimony given by a witness at a former trial shall be admissible in any subsequent action or proceeding, if the witness has since died, become insane, left the jurisdiction, or has otherwise become incompetent to testify, provided the parties and issues involved in the two actions are substantially the same. The rule applies to both civil and criminal. cases. Peo. v. Elliott, 172 N. Y. 146. (Note: Sec. 8, subd. 2 (d) of C. Cr. Proc., as added in 1935, now provides: "Where the defendant has previously been tried upon an indictment or information embracing the same charge, the testimony of any witness who has testified upon such prior trial may be read in evidence upon any subsequent trial of the same indictment or information, upon its being satisfactorily shown to the court that the witness is dead or insane, or can not with due diligence be found in the state.") 

2. Grounds for Admission: 

a. Death: In the case of death, the right to use the former testimony is absolute. Wallack, 94 

N. Y. S. 574 (where rule was applied to expert witness). (1) Serious Sickness: Has been held not to be sufficient ground to accept former testimony. 

Fink, 280 N. Y. S. 269; Peo. v. Newman, 5 

Hill. 295. Cf. Morehouse, 41 Hun 146. b. Insanity: Is sufficient to warrant admission of the former testimony of the witness. C. P. A. 348. c. Absence from Jurisdiction: At common law, mere absence from the state was insufficient to warrant the admissibility of former testimony of

a witness. Crary, 12 Wend. (NY) 41. By virtue of C. P. A. 348, if the witness, being a non-resident has departed from the state, or being a resident has departed by reason of military or naval service under the State or United States, his former testimony is admissible, provided such non-residence, at the time of the second trial, is satisfactorily established. Longacre, 182 N. Y. S. 373; Peo. v 

Bruno, 220 N. Y. 702; Fink, supra. 

d. Disqualification of Witness Under C. P. A. 347: 

Under C. P. A. 347, where one party to a pending action dies, the surviving party will not be permitted to testify against the representative of the deceased, concerning a personal transaction with the deceased. Therefore the former testimony of the party who can no longer testify is admissible under C. P. A. 348, on a second trial, as secondary evidence of the facts stated. See Morehouse, 41 

Hun 146. 

3. Identity of Parties: The parties in the second action must be those in the first action or their "legal representatives." The latter term comprehends "persons standing in the relation of privies in blood, privies in estate, or privies in law." Shaw, 187 N. Y. 

186 (where it was held that a lessee is bound by testimony previously given in the same action against his lessor). a. Privies in estate include a remainderman or reversioner, in relation to the life tenant; and a grantee in relation to the grantor. Shook, 110 N. Y. S. 951. b. Privies in law include an executor or administrator. 

Wilbur, 6 Cow. 162. 

4. Identity of Issues: The issues in the two cases need not be identical; it is sufficient that the subject matter to which the evidence relates be so substantially the same that the party, or his privy, had a reasonably fair opportunity for cross-examination on the first trial as to the facts offered on the second. 

Cohen, 139 N. Y. S. 887. If a party against whom evidence is offered could not have cross-examined as to such evidence, when it was originally given,

such evidence is incompetent as against him on the second trial. This would be the case where new parties, not in privity with the former, are joined with them in the second action. Van Shaick, 266 

N. Y. 591. But, if the opportunity to cross-examine on the former trial existed, it is not necessary that the party availed himself of that right. Young, 177 

N. Y. 347; Bradley, 91 N. Y. 293 (default on trial). a. Case: P sues as administratrix of an intestate daughter to recover for the latter's wrongful death caused by the negligence of D railroad company. D offered in evidence the testimony of a deceased flagman, given on the trial of a former action brought by P as administratrix of a son who was killed in the same accident. Held, the former testimony is admissible. The issue was the same in both actions, to wit, the negligence of D's servant. Cohen, supra. b. Case: P sues D for damages for conversion. Previously, P had been the complainant against D in a felony prosecution for larceny arising out of the same transaction. On the criminal trial, X, now deceased, had testified as a witness. Is X's former testimony admissible under C.P.A. 

348? Held, Yes. Technically the parties are not the same in the two actions, but in substance they are. The issue is the same, viz., D's right to exercise dominion over P's alleged property. Profitos, 158 N. Y. S. 369. 

5. "Former Trial": The testimony must have been given on a former judicial trial. Kaufman, 248 A. D. 

628. A trial before a tribunal, commissioner, or referee, exercising judicial functions is a trial within the meaning of the statute. Cox, 7 Johns. 298 (bankruptcy commissioner); Jackson, 2 Johns. 17 (land title commissioner). But a hearing before a legislative committee is not such a trial. Peo. v. 

Sharp, 107 N. Y. 427. Lack of jurisdiction of the court, or declaration of a mistrial on the former trial, interrupting the witness, renders the witness' testimony inadmissible on a second trial. Deering, 85 N. Y. S. 275; Taft, 178 N. Y. 127. But the fact that the case was never completed, or ended in a jury deadlock, does not render the testimony inadmissible upon a second trial. Taft, supra; Lawson, 61 How. Pr. 424.

6. How Former Testimony Proved: The oral testi mony of any one who heard the testimony on the former trial is sufficient to prove it. Peo. v. Robins, 242 A. D. 516. A juror, witness, attorney, stenographer or even the presiding judge may be a competent witness therefor. McRorie, 203 N. Y. 426. a. The witness may refresh his memory by notes which he made. Van Buren, 14 Barb. 118. Minutes of testimony made by the judge may be used, provided the judge verifies them in court. Peo. v. Corey, 157 N. Y. 332. b. The usual method of proof, however, is to produce the stenographer's minutes made on the former trial. Such minutes must be verified by the stenographer, on his oath as a witness, in the absence of statutory provision to the contrary. Peo. v. Randazzio, 194 N. Y. 147. But if the stenographer has since died, or become incompetent, his notes may be read in evidence by anyone whose competency to read them accurately is established to the satisfaction of the court. C.P.A. 348. 

7. Testimony Admitted Subject to Objection: The former testimony when offered on the second trial may be subjected to any legal objection that may be addressed to it as of the time it is being offered in evidence, except the objections that the witness is not testifying in person, or that since the giving of the former testimony, the witness became incompetent under C. P. A. 347. Murphy, 167 N. Y. S. 

270; C. P. A. 348.

J. Miscellaneous Exceptions to Hearsay Evidence Rule: 

1. Standard Statistical Tables: Standard mortality tables and other standard scientific tables, standard business tables, and the state and federal weather reports are admissible in evidence, notwithstanding they are largely hearsay. Hartley, 22 N. Y. 178.

Standard books on art or science are not admissible. 

Foggett, 48 N. Y. S. 741; Pahl, 81 N. Y. S. 46. But see Winthrop Chem. Co., 268 N. Y. S. 647. 

2. In Arbitration Cases: Where parties stipulate to submit their controversy to arbitration, the arbitrators are not required to adhere to the strict rules of evidence, and admission of hearsay evidence will not be grounds for vacating the award. Wheat Export 

Co., 173 N. Y. S. 679.

a. But if an arbitrator, although in good faith, conducts an independent ex parte investigation after the hearing has been closed, the award will be set aside for misconduct. Berizzi Co., 239 N. Y. 315; 

Halstead, 82 N. Y. 27. 

3. Under Workmen's Compensation Act: The inquiry made into claimant's case need not closely follow the rules of evidence, and an award will not be set aside because founded, in part, on hearsay evidence. 

Belcher, 224 N. Y. 326; W. C. A. sec. 118. But an award based wholly on hearsay will not be sustained. 

Carroll, 218 N. Y. 435. 

4. Hearings on "Small Claims" in N. Y. C. Municipal

Court: Such hearings are conducted informally with a view to substantial justice. The court, or referee, is not bound by statutory rules of practice, procedure, pleading or evidence, except statutory provisions relating to privileged communications and to transactions with a deceased or incompetent. Mun. Ct. 

Code, 179-187.

CHAPTER VI. 

. 

I. The Parol Evidence Rule: 

A. Rule Stated: Parol evidence is inadmissible to vary, alter, contradict, add to, or, subtract from the terms of a written instrument in an action founded on such instrument between the parties or privies thereto. 

Thomas, 127 N. Y. 133. The rule is more a rule of substantive law than one of evidence since it operates to exclude proof of the alleged oral transaction no matter how cogent, material or relevant such proof may be. 

See Higgs, 263 N. Y. 473. Parol evidence of the contents of a writing collateral to the issue (i.e., the writing not being in issue) is admissible, but if the writing is otherwise material, the best evidence rule may be invoked. Note, that a unilateral, self-made and selfserving writing is parol.

B. Reason for Rule: Primarily, the rule was evolved to protect against fraud, perjury, death or lapse of memory of a witness (Thomas, supra), and is based on the theory that all prior negotiations between the parties to a written instrument are superseded by and merged in the instrument itself. Therefore, to permit conversations and oral dickering incident to the drawing up of a written instrument would defeat the very object aimed at in reducing the agreement to written form. 

King, 210 N. Y. 467.

C. Illustrations of Rule: Under the general rule above, parol is inadmissible to show that a blank indorsement on a note was meant to be without recourse (Martin, 104 U. S. 30); or to show that a tenant was to live rent free, or pay a lesser rental, in a certain contingency, where the lease recites the stipulated rental (Kaven, 84 N. Y. S. 470); or to show a parol modification of a written clause, in a conditional bill of sale of an automobile, against taking the automobile out of the State without the conditional vendor's consent (Jenkins, 215 

N. Y. S. 694); or to show that defendant, maker of a 90

note, orally agreed with the payee, that the former was to pay only a certain sum weekly on the note as long as he purchased beer from the latter. Munch Brewery, 112 N. Y. S. 1042.

D. Strangers to Instrument Not Affected: The parol evi dence rule does not affect strangers to the instrument, as to whom it is permissible to prove the exact transaction by parol even though the instrument is expressly contradicted thereby. Barro, 145 N. Y. S. 849; Traders 

Bank, 238 N. Y. 535. 

1. Case: P, realty broker, sues D for commissions for obtaining a purchaser for D's property. D had sent P a letter offering to sell for $175,000, “details to be settled later". P returned this letter with the written offer endorsed thereon of X and Y, customers, to buy the property for $155,000. D wrote his consent on the same letter and returned it to P. On the trial 

P offered this letter in evidence. May D show by parol that his indorsement of acceptance was not intended as a complete agreement and that X and Y finally backed out when it came to settle the necessary details later? Held, yes. Since the contract does not conclude P who was not a party to it, neither does it conclude D who was. Folinsbee, 157 N. Y. 

197. See also Lee, 161 N. Y. S. 509. 

2. Case: P, a diamond dealer, delivered to M, a diamond ring on a written memo signed by M which read: "These goods are sent for your inspection and remain our property and are to be returned to us on demand. Sale takes effect only from date of our approval of your selection." M, without P's consent pledged the ring with D loan company as security for a personal loan to M. D acted in good faith without notice of P's rights. D now refuses to surrender the ring to 

P unless P repays the amount of M's loan with interest. P brings replevin. May D show, by parol, the conceded fact that M was P's agent for the purpose of selling the ring? (Under the Factors Act, Pers. Prop. L. sec. 43, if M were such agent, although he had no authority to pledge, his act is so far validated by the statute that his principal may reclaim only upon redeeming from the pledge.) Held, Yes.

The parol evidence is admissible since D, not being a party to the written memo, is not bound by it. Nelkin, 265 N. Y. 

393. Cf. Green, 254 N. Y. 437 (where opposite result was reached through D's failure to claim protection outside the memo itself, i.e., under the Factor's Act). See also Sweet 

Co., 254 A. D. 242. 

E. Exception to Rule: 

1. To Show Validity or Invalidity: Parol evidence is

admissible to show that no binding obligation ever existed and that the agreement sued on is invalid. 

Conversely, where a written contract is attacked for invalidity, parol evidence is competent to prove its validity. Keuka College, 167 N. Y. 96. 

a. Failure of Consideration: Parol evidence is ad missible to show that an instrument is invalid for want of consideration, or that the consideration expressed is not the true one. Baird, 145

N. Y. 659. (This rule would not apply to a holder in due course of a negotiable instrument.) Also, where no consideration is expressed in a written contract, not required to be in writing by the Statute of Frauds, parol evidence is admissible to show the actual consideration. Taylor, 104 N. Y.

S. 557. Thus, where a written agreement is made. to reduce rent for the remainder of the term under a lease, stating no consideration therefor, it is proper to show by parol that the consideration for the agreement was a reciprocal obligation on the part of the lessee to make alterations in the building and additions thereto. Natelson, 99 

N. Y. S. 327. See also Benedict, 191 N. Y. 377. 

b. Where Consideration is Executory on Both Sides: 

In such case, as distinguished from a case where the recital of the consideration is merely evidence of the fact (as in a deed, mortgage, receipt, note, or bill of sale), parol evidence is inadmissible to alter, vary, enlarge or lessen such consideration. 

Cocks, 49 N. Y. 107. Thus, where a chattel mortgage recited the sum in which the mortgagor was indebted to the mortgagee, parol evidence was held inadmissible to show that the mortgagee owed a lesser amount. Sturmdorf, 102 

N. Y. S. 1042 aff'd, 190 N. Y. 555. In such case, the part of the agreement expressing the consideration is just as conclusive upon the parties as any other term of the contract. Emmett, 151 N. Y. 

564. Parol evidence is admissible, however, to alter or vary a written recital of an executed consideration.

23 c. Duress: Parol evidence is always admissible to prove that a written agreement is invalid because of duress. Mills, 23 Wend. 314. 

d. Illegality: Evidence of prior conversations or negotiations are admissible to prove that a written agreement is illegal. Mudgett, 18 Hun. 302.

Otherwise, a party to an illegal contract could make it enforceable by simply using such written words as would conceal or omit the illegal object intended to be accomplished. St. Hubert Guild, 118 N. Y. S. 582. Thus, a written contract, valid on its face, could be shown by parol to be void for usury. Again, an action for rent due, under a written lease could be defeated by parol proof that it was orally agreed that the premises were to be used for an illegal purpose. Plath, 45 N. Y. 

S. 95. e. Mistake: Parol evidence is admissible to show that, through mistake, the parties did not reach any agreement, or that their actual agreement was not truly disclosed by the written instrument. 

Nichols, 18 N. Y. S. 623, aff'd 137 N. Y. 471. Thus, it may be shown, in an action to reform a deed, that a certain tract of land intended to be included in the grant was left out by mistake. 

Beatty, 137 N. Y. S. 456. 

f. Material Alterations: Parol evidence is admissible to prove that a written instrument has been so materially altered as to fail to express the agreement of the parties. Booth, 56 N. Y. 22. g. Incapacity: In an action to enforce an agreement, it may be shown by parole evidence that a party was legally incapable of entering into such agreement. Linderman, 131 N. Y. 434. 

2. To Complete Writings; (Supplying Missing Terms): 

a. Rule Stated: Parol evidence is admissible to complete an incomplete written contract, as where it does not cover the entire contract orally agreed upon. Di Menna, 221 N. Y. 391. Three requirements must be fulfilled:

94 (1) The writing must not appear, upon inspection, to be a complete contract embracing all the particulars necessary to make a perfect agreement and designed to express the whole arrangement between the parties. (In such case, it is conclusively presumed to embrace the entire contract.) (2) The parol terms must be consistent with and not contradictory of the written instrument. (3) The written contract must not be one required to be in writing by the Statute of Frauds. (a) Illustrations: A written contract to furnish a door with fancy embossed glass may be supplemented by parol proof that such glass was to be oval in shape. Vogel, 51 N. Y. S. 173. Similarly, a letter offering employment at a stated salary for a stated time, but silent as to the nature of the employee's duties, which letter recited that it constituted a contract on the part of the employer and was accepted as such by the employee may be supplemented by parol proof as to the nature of the duties. Smith, 115 N. Y. S. 

204. But in Loomis, 203 N. Y. 359, it was held that, in a written contract to transport goods, a blank space left for the route could not be supplied by parol proof that before the contract the shipper instructed the carrier to ship by a particular route, since the unfilled blank was incidental merely and not essential to a perfect or complete agreement for the transportation of the merchandise. Cf. Goddard, 52 Barb. 87. See also Jacob Bros., 173 N. Y. S. 136, where parol was held admissible to supply the interest blank in a conditional sale contract. 

b. Rule Specifically Applied: 

(1) Warranties: Where a chattel is hired or sold, without an express warranty of fitness for use, extrinsic facts may be proved to create an implied warranty of such fitness. Hoisting 

Engine Sales Co., 237 N. Y. 30. Pers. Prop. 

L., sec. 96. An express, oral warranty may be proved only where it relates to a future condition, capacity or quality of the article hired

or sold, but not where it relates to a present condition. Cohan, 213 N. Y. S. 681. (a) Thus in Eighmie, 98 N. Y. 288; it was held improper to admit parol testimony of an express, oral warranty that oil wells sold under a deed manifestly complete, were producing a stated amount of oil daily at the time of the transfer; the court intimating that had the warranty been that the wells would continue for a fixed period to yield a certain output, parol proof would have been admissible. See also Chapin, 78 N. Y. 

74. It is doubtful whether the courts would still recognize this distinction, in view of the present tendency to exclude parol evidence of any oral express warranties, that tend to vary or add to the terms of a written contract complete on its face. Eastmann, 162 N. Y. S. 587; Colt, 144 

N. Y. S. 557, Bareham, 240 N. Y. S. 123. See also Wm. H. Waters Inc., 269 N. Y. S. 420, holding that parol warranties as to quality are so inseparably connected with the subject matter of a written sales contract that they cannot be proven by parol under the exception to the parol evidence rule relating to independent collateral oral agreements. Also Ernst Bros., 270 

N. Y. 165. 

(2) Receipts: Are informal, non-contractual writings, constituting mere, unilateral admissions on the part of the maker, and may be varied, contradicted, or explained by parol evidence as to amount, (Reikes, 99 N. Y. S. 

318); balance due, (Jones, 18 Hun. 452); payment (Farren, 26 N. Y. S. 619, aff'd 148 N. Y. 

741), or the circumstances thereof. Seeley, 220 N. Y. 416; Klein, 255 A. D. 335. 

(3) Memoranda: A memorandum of sale, un signed or signed only by the seller, is not a contractual writing within the parol evidence rule and therefore may be varied or contradicted by parol. Dominion Trading Co., 168

N. Y. S. 693. The same rule applies to a memorandum of an order for goods. (Hohenstein, 64 N. Y. S. 1137), and of a bill rendered. Crosby, 128 N. Y. S. 64.

3. To Prove Conditional Delivery: Parol evidence is admissible to show that a writing, other than a deed or mortgage of real property never became operative as a contract because it was delivered upon a condition precedent which was not performed. Smith, 200 

N. Y. 299 (where parol was held admissible to show that a note given in payment of a life insurance premium was delivered upon the condition that it was not to be effective unless the insurer first made á loan to the insured upon the security of the policy). Similarly, in Niblock, 200 N. Y. 390, parol was held admissible to show that a note was not to be paid until the maker and former co-partner of the payee had paid their partnership debts. But where there is a binding contract upon the delivery of the written instrument or contract, it cannot be defeated by parol proof of delivery upon a condition subsequent. Jamestown Business College, 172 N. Y. 

291 (holding that a note delivered in payment of tuition cannot be made void by parol proof that it was agreed that the note was to be ineffective if the maker subsequently decided not to become a student). Cf. Snowden, 101 N. Y. 322; Saltzman, 239 

N. Y. 332. (Of course, in the case of a completed, negotiable, promissory note in the hands of a holder in due course, parol evidence would not be admissible to show any kind of conditional delivery as a defense. N. I. L. sec. 35.) a. Deeds: In New York, parol evidence is inadmissible to show that a deed was delivered to the grantee on an oral condition, whether precedent or subsequent. Lafayette St. Church Soc., 202 

N. Y. 379; Moore, 160 N. Y. 703. 

4. To Show an Independent, Collateral, Oral Agreement: a. Generally: Parol evidence of an independent, collateral, oral agreement, made before, or concurrently with a written contract, and relating to the same subject, is admissible provided it does not vary or contradict the written contract and pro-

vided the collateral agreement is one which the parties would not ordinarily be expected to embody in the written contract. Mitchell, 247 N. Y. 377. b. Test of Collateralness: In determining whether a particular parol agreement is collateral to the written contract the two should be compared and the following test applied: Would ordinarily prudent business men, situated as were the ones to the written contract, naturally and normally include within the written contract the alleged parol agreement? If they would, the oral agreement is non-collateral and may not be proved. If they would not, it is collateral, and if also independent, parol is admissible. The question is for the court. Mitchell, 247 N. Y. 377. c. Test of Independency: Does the alleged, oral, collateral agreement rest upon a separate consideration? If yes, it is an independent collateral agreement and may be proved by parol; if no, parol is inadmissible. (1) Case: D sells P a business under a written contract of sale. May P prove by parol an alleged, oral agreement made by D at the time of the sale not to engage in the same business in the same territory? 

Held, No. Such an oral agreement, even if made, cannot be proved because (1) it is non-collateral (it relates to the same subject matter as the written contract and ordinarily prudent business men would have incorporated it into the written contract) and (2) it is not independent (since it is not founded on a separate consideration). Costello, 12 N. Y. S. 236, aff'd 128 N. Y. 650. (2) Case: P agreed in writing to buy from D a certain house and lot. In the written contract D undertook to fix foundation cracks, install screens and make certain other repairs at his own expense. P now sues D for damages for breach of an oral agreement, alleged by P to have been made by D contemporaneously with the written contract, to make the premises water-tight. Is parol evidence admissible to prove such an agreement? Held, No. The oral agreement, even if made, is not collateral since ordinarily, parties would have embodied it in the

written agreement. Besides it is not independent. 

Ball, 267 N. Y. 470. (3) Case: D orally agreed to construct a storehouse for 

P of sufficient capacity to hold at least three carloads of roofing slate, on condition that P would lease same for a period of two years under a written lease. The building was constructed and P entered into the written lease with D. Upon taking possession and unloading one carload of slate into the warehouse, the floor collapsed. P now sues D for damages. Is parol evidence as to the oral agreement admissible? Held, Yes. The oral agreement is collateral, and, besides, is independent of the lease since it rests on a separate consideration, (i.e., P's agreement to take the lease). Brown, 170 N. Y. S. 445. 

5. To Show Subsequent Parol Modifications: 

a. As to Simple Written Contract: The parol evi dence rule applies only to extrinsic matters preceding or accompanying the execution of the contract. A written contract, not under seal, may be varied or modified by a subsequent parol agreement based on a new consideration, (which may be the mutual promises of the parties) provided it is not one which the Statute of Frauds requires to be in writing. Coff-Garrod Co., 185 N. Y. S. 

553. Thus, a written hiring at will may be superseded by a subsequent oral agreement of hiring for a year. Horne, 165 N. Y. S. 185. Note, too, that even though the written contract provides. that no modification or variation shall be binding unless in writing, that provision, by consent of the parties, may be orally waived. Gen. Elec. Co., 178 N. Y. 369. 

b. As to Contracts Under Seal: An executed parol modification of a sealed contract (i.e., executed by one or both parties) is valid even though founded on no new consideration and may be shown by parol. McKenzie, 120 N. Y. 260. Also, a contract under seal may be modified, varied or cancelled (after Sept. 1, 1936) by an unsealed, executory agreement in writing signed by the party against whom the modification or discharge is sought to be enforced. C.P.A. 342 as amended

in 1936. A wholly executory, oral modification or discharge is still invalid. (1) Case: P borrowed money from D giving stock as security. The contract, executed under seal, merely recited that P sold these shares to D for a certain sum and that D agreed to resell, six months later, and not thereafter, at the same price. P failed to repurchase within the designated time. P now seeks to redeem the stock on the theory that its transfer was a mortgage and not a strict sale. Held, parol is admissible, since the contract on its face disclosed a security for a loan and extrinsic evidence is received not to vary the writing, but merely to explain it so that equity can enforce it. Lipe, 243 A. D. 433. (2) Case: P agreed in writing, under seal, to sell to Ds certain realty subject to a bank mortgage of $11,000, as to which P agreed to obtain an extension of payment from the bank for at least an additional two years, otherwise, the contract of sale to become void. 

Before the law day, P found that the bank would extend only for $10,000. P thereupon orally agreed with Ds to take their second mortgage for the $1,000 difference, and to pay the bank the $1,000 it was calling in, out of his own funds. P then agreed with the bank to pay it the $1,000 and to secure the extension. On the law day Ds refused to perform on the ground that it might impair their credit to place a second mortgage on the property, and stood upon the written agreement. P brings specific performance. Is parol evidence of the modification admissible? Held, Yes. The contract under seal had been orally modified and as thus modified has been fully performed by P in reliance thereon. 

Harris, supra. 

c. As to Contracts Required to be in Writing by 

Statute of Frauds: These may not be modified by parol evidence of a subsequent oral agreement (Maddaloni Olive Oil Co., 180 N. Y. S. 274) unless one of the parties has relied on such parol modification to his disadvantage (Imperator 

Realty Co., 228 N. Y. 447), or unless the subsequent oral agreement need not be in writing and is agreed by the parties to be a complete substitute for the first agreement. Lieberman, 236 

N. Y. 139.

F. Construction of Writings: 

1. Ambiguities: Oral evidence to explain an ambiguity in a writing is not regarded as an exception to the parol evidence rule, because it does not vary or contradict the writing, but is admitted merely to enable the court to understand it. Thomas, 127 N. Y. 

133. Such evidence must be relevant, material, and not too remote. U. S. Printing etc. Co., 233 N. Y. 143. a. Latent Ambiguity: One that arises out of some collateral matter when a writing, unambiguous on its face, is attempted to be applied to the subject matter thereof. Bacon, Max. 25. Where the words used apply equally well to two different things or subject matters, then parol evidence is admissible to show which was the thing or subject matter intended. Petrie, 158 N. Y. 458. (1) Case: A will gave to testator's three sons, "John, one house, George, one house, William, one house."

Held, parol evidence was admissible to show that testator owned three houses not otherwise disposed of by the will. Re Tirner, 206 N. Y. 93. (2) Case: A fire insurance policy on hay and grain did not clearly specify in which of two barns the insured property was located. Held, the latent ambiguity may be removed by parol evidence that a particular barn was pointed out to the agent writing the policy as the one containing the hay and grain. Bowman, 59 

N. Y. 521. See also Burr, 16 N. Y. 267. b. Patent Ambiguity: One that appears on the face of the writing. While the old rule that parol evidence is inadmissible to explain such ambiguity is still recognized to some extent (Strong, 50 

N. Y. S. 257), the present rule seems to be that a patent ambiguity may be explained by evidence of the circumstances surrounding the execution of the writing, so as to enable the court to determine what the parties meant, and to apply the writing to its subject matter. Decker, 42 N. Y. S. 

243, aff'd 154 N. Y. 764. But, "if after resort to all the evidence admissible, the court cannot ascertain the meaning and intention of the parties, then it is a case of incurable and hopeless uncer-

tainty," and the instrument will be held void.

White's Bk, 73 N. Y. 335. Thus, an assignment of property to A "or" B as security for an indebtedness the assignor then owed may be rendered certain by evidence that the assignor was largely indebted to both A and B. Decker, supra. 

But a contract "to dig a well," without specifying the kind of a well, cannot be cured by evidence of prior negotiations indicating what the parties intended by use of the quoted words. Strong, supra. 

c. Blanks in Writing: These constitute the most general form of patent ambiguity. The rule is that if the intention of the parties cannot be ascertained by reference to the circumstances surrounding the execution of the writing, the omission cannot be supplied by parol, and the writing is void for uncertainty. McCown, 187

N. Y. S. 856. This excluding rule does not apply where the blank occurs in a part of the writing which is not vital to its operation, as for instance, where the amount intended to be secured by mortgage is left blank in a defeasance clause. 

Burnett, 135 N. Y. 543. Cf. Loomis, 203 N. Y. 359. 

2. Meaning of Words: 

a. Technical Words, Trade Expressions, Symbols, etc.: Having a purely local, or technical, meaning, and unintelligible to persons unacquainted with the business, may be explained by parol, provided such explanation is consistent with the terms of the contract. Atkinson, 217 N. Y. 230.

The rule has been applied to show the meaning of "whaling voyage," as used in a marine insurance policy (Child, 5 N. Y. 26); of “port risk," as used in the same connection (Nelson, 71 N. Y. 

453); of "Amacid Blue Black K N," as used in a contract (American Aniline Products, 179 N. Y. S. 

895), and of "for the season," as used in a contract of employment. Waechterhauser, 10 N. Y. S. 535. 

b. Common or Popular Words: Whose meaning is

well-defined are not explainable by parol evidence. 

This rule has been applied to "incompatible" in a contract of employment (Gray, 147 N. Y. 177); "guarantee" (Phelps, 25 N. Y. S. 654); "returned for repairs," as indorsed on a bill of lading of a machine (Stimpson, 164 N. Y. S. 68); “pro rata" as used in an insurance policy. (Home Ins. Co., 180 N. Y. 389), and "approved houses", as used in a sales agency contract. Kaulbach, 166 N. Y. 

S. 1062. 

3. Custom or Usage: Parol evidence is admissible as to the uniform, continuous and well-settled usage and custom pertaining to matters embraced in a contract, unless such usage contravenes a rule of law or alters or contradicts the terms of the contract. Atkinson, supra. Thus the term "3% cash" in a contract for the sale of fur coats may be explained by parol to show that it means, in the fur trade, cash on delivery (Simmons Co., 150 N. Y. S. 547); similarly, the phrase "1800 tons of No. 1 heavy books and magazines, guaranteed free from ground wood" as used in a contract for the sale of paper stock, may be explained by parol evidence of a usage that the paper was not to contain over 3% ground wood. Gumbinsky Bros., 197 N. Y. S. 530, aff'd 235 N. Y. 619. a. A word or phrase need not be unusual or indefinite in order to admit parol evidence of usage; it is sufficient that the term has a secondary meaning in commerce, or in a particular trade or business to which the contract relates, and that the term was so used. Gumbinsky Bros., supra. 

Cf. Green, 254 N. Y. 437. 

4. Construction of Will: 

a. Generally: Where the language of a will is so obscure as not to disclose testator's real intent, parol evidence is admissible to show facts and circumstances surrounding the execution of the will, and thus to aid in determining testator's intention. Herzog, 177 N. Y. 86. But where the language of the will is clear and unequivocal,

parol proof of extrinsic facts is inadmissible. 

Dwight, 245 N. Y. 71. Nor is parol evidence admissible where it would materially change the dispositive provisions of the will. March, 186 

N. Y. 99. The court's duty is to construe the will, not to make a new one. Herzog, 177 N. Y. 86.

Thus, where testator leaves blank the name of the legatee, or devisee, or the amount of the bequest, parol evidence, outside the will, is inadmissible. to fill in the blank. The ambiguity is patent and there is nothing to interpret. Leggett, 185 N. Y. 70. 

b. Declarations of Testator: Are inadmissible to interpret his will, except to identify the legatee or the legacy, when either of two persons or two things may be involved. Lummis, 166 N. Y. 936. (1) Case: Testatrix bequeathed a certain sum to "my niece, Lena Baumann of Richmond Hill." Two people claimed the legacy, one, a grandniece whose name was Lena Baumann, and who resided at Richmond Hill, and the other, a niece whose maiden name had been Magdalena Baumann. Held, parol was admissible to show that testatrix was accustomed to refer to the niece as "Lena Baumann" of Richmond Hill. Baumann, 213 N. Y. 328. 

(2) Case: Testator devised property to the "Skin and 

Cancer Hospital" of New York City. The "New

York Skin and Cancer Hospital" and the "New York Cancer Hospital", both in the same city lay claim to the devise. Held, parol is inadmissible to show that testator meant the "New York Cancer

Hospital," since the other devisee was described with sufficient accuracy. Union Trust Co., 77 N. Y. 

S. 528, aff'd 175 N. Y. 505.

II. Best Evidence Rule: 

A. Rule Stated: A writing is the best evidence of its own contents, and must be introduced unless it has been lost or destroyed, or its absence has been otherwise satisfactorily accounted for. Butler, 171 N. Y. 208. The rule does not apply to a writing collateral to the issue. 

Reynolds, 1 N. Y. 283. Thus where A deposited the proceeds of a draft with B, it was held that A could testify orally as to the amount of the draft, in an action

to recover the same, and that the production of the draft itself was unnecessary, since the amount deposited and not the draft was the issue in dispute. Bowen, 11 

Hun 226.

B. History of Rule: Originally, the rule related to all classes of evidence, oral and written, and required the best evidence obtainable. McKinnon, 21 N. Y. 218.

Later it was narrowed so as to embrace writings only. 

Carroll, 186 N. Y. S. 737. Today, as to secondary evidence, there is no distinction as to degree, so far as admissibility is concerned. However, where it is a question of probative weight, parol evidence may be of primary or secondary grade. Seidenspinner, 175 N. Y. 95.

C. Rule Applied: 

1. Duplicate Writings: Where parties execute a con tract in duplicate, each being an exact copy of the other, except, perhaps, where signatures are interchanged, both copies are originals and primary evidence of the terms of the contract, and either is admissible in evidence without accounting for the non-production of the other. Hubbard, 24 Harb. 404. a. Carbon Copies: If the entire writing is typewritten at the time when the carbon copy is made, leaving no blanks to be filled in later, the carbon. copy is a duplicate original. Arenson, 162 N. Y. 

S. 142 (applied to stenographer's transcript of minutes). But where several copies are typewritten at the same time by use of carbon paper, and only one copy is signed, the weight of authority is that the signed copy becomes the original. 22 

C. J. p. 1024. 

b. Letter Press Copies: Are not duplicate originals. 

Menasha Ware Co., 107 N. Y. 299; Fitch, 160 

N. Y. S. 1066. 

c. Photostatic Copies: Being only reproductions of the originals, are secondary evidence, and to be admissible even as such, must first be shown to be true copies. Miller, 202 N. Y. S. 295.

2. Telegrams: Ordinarily, the best evidence of the contents of a telegram is the message as delivered to the addressee. But if it is the addressee who employs the telegraph company as his agent, then the telegram delivered by the addressor to the company is the best evidence of the message. Montgomery, 219 Fed. 162. 

3. Public Records: Under the Civil Practice Act, certi fied transcripts of public records or documents may be introduced in evidence whenever necessary, with the same force and effect as if the originals were produced. See same title Berk. Practice. 

4. Voluminous Facts: Contained in books and writ ings put in evidence, an examination of which could not be made in court, may be testified to by a competent person, such as an accountant, or auditor, who has examined them. Greenfield, 47 N. Y. 430; Peo. v. 

Miles, 108 N. Y. S. 510, aff'd 193 N. Y. 541. 

5. Admissions as to Contents: In New York, admis sions of a party as to the contents of written instrument are admissible only where parol evidence would be admissible to establish the same facts, i.e., where there is no better evidence which can be produced. Sherman v. Peo. 13 Hun 575.

D. Secondary Evidence: 

1. Generally: A non-duplicate copy of a material document, and the personal testimony of a witness as to its contents are both secondary evidence, and may be given only if a sufficient legal excuse is shown. for the non-production of the original. Crane, 177 

N. Y. 106. As far as the admissibility of the evidence is concerned there is no legal difference between proving a written agreement by parol evidence and proving it by a copy, not a duplicate original, since both are secondary evidence, and there is no such thing as "best secondary evidence." 

Rosenbaum, 162 N. Y. S. 227. 

2. Loss or Destruction of Original: 

a. Generally: Where a party is able to satisfy the court that he cannot produce the original docu-

ment, after diligent effort, because it has been lost or destroyed, parol evidence of its contents is admissible. Ford, 19 Wend. 334. Such loss or destruction should be established, if possible, by the testimony of the person who last possessed it. Kearney, 92 N. Y. 617. b. Due Diligence: No arbitrary rule may be laid down as to what constitutes due diligence. The question is a preliminary one of fact for the court to decide. Kearney, supra. If the party acts in good faith and exercises the same degree of diligence that an ordinary person, desirous of finding and producing the original, would have employed, then secondary evidence will ordinarily be admitted. Blair, 141 N. Y. 53. Thus, where a party shows that he has been in the habit of destroying bank vouchers as soon as he found his bank statement to be correct, and that he always did this, and that he had no particular object in destroying a specific voucher, this constitutes a satisfactory explanation for the destruction of the original voucher and parol evidence of its contents will be accepted. Steele, 70 N. Y. 

280. If the document is important, strict proof of diligent search may be required; otherwise, slight proof will suffice. Peo. v. Dolan, 186 N. Y. 4. 

Loss may be presumed from lapse of time. Daniels, 8 N. Y. S. 128, aff'd 130 N. Y. 696. c. Intentional Destruction: Where a party intentionally destroys an original document, so as to fraudulently create an excuse for its non-production on the trial, secondary evidence on his part is inadmissible. West, 67 N. Y. S. 104. However, if there was no fraudulent intent or ulterior motive, secondary evidence is admissible. Steele, supra. If destruction is by the adverse party, motive or intent is immaterial, and secondary evidence is admissible. Scott, 5 Sandf. 572. 

3. Refusal of Adverse Party to Produce Original: 

a. Generally:

Where the original writing is in the

possession or under the control of the adverse party, who fails or refuses to produce it after reasonable notice to do so, secondary evidence on the part of his adversary is admissible. 

Saunders, 167 N. Y. 261. It is immaterial that production of the writing could have been enforced by subpoena duces tecum. Guggenheim, 123 N. Y. S. 950. (Note that the service of a subpoena duces tecum will not substitute for the service of a notice to produce.) b. Notice to Produce: Either party may serve upon his opponent, at any reasonable time before trial, a written notice demanding production of a specified paper or document in the latter's possession, and stating that, upon failure to produce the original, secondary evidence of its contents will be offered. If the adverse party fails to comply with such notice, he loses his right to object to secondary evidence as not being the "best evidence." If it is patent from the pleadings that a certain paper will be material on the trial of the issues, or if the document is on hand at the trial, notice to produce, previously given, is not necessary to support a demand at the trial for production of the original. Gordon, 188 N. Y. S. 135. 

c. Effect of Failure to Produce: The only effect of non-production after notice is to permit secondary evidence. No inference may be drawn from failure to produce (Bissell, 145 N. Y. S. 591, aff'd 214 

N. Y. 672); except, possibly, the presumption of a weak cause from wilfully withholding evidence. 

Tanger, 104 N. Y. S. 681. However, it has been held that where secondary evidence has been given, as the result of a party's failure to produce the original, it cannot be rebutted by that party introducing the original writing. Mather, 118 

N. Y. 629. See same title Berk. Practice. 

4. Document Beyond Court's Jurisdiction: In New 

York, the fact that an original document is beyond the court's jurisdiction does not, of itself, warrant

admission of secondary evidence. The deposition of the person having possession of the document, out of the state, must be obtained, or some satisfactory explanation must be given why this cannot be done. 

Kearney, 92 N. Y. 617.

CHAPTER VII. 

OPINION EVIDENCE. 

I. General Rule: The opinion or conclusion to be drawn from facts is within the province of the jury, or of the court if there is no jury; hence, the opinion of an ordinary witness is, generally, inadmissible. Peo. v. Sharp, 107 N. Y. 427; 

Conn. Ins. Co., 111 U. S. 612.

A. Illustrations: Thus, a witness may not testify: whether certain transactions or negotiations amounted to a contract (Hillock, 97 N. Y. S. 823); whether X was acting in a careful (or careless) manner (McCarrogher, 120 

N. Y. 526); whether X's negligence caused an accident (Crofut, 36 N. Y. 201); whether a fire was of incendiary origin (Peo. v. Grutz, 212 N. Y. 272); or whether a person needed medical attention (Valentine, 106 A. D. 

487) whether a delivered deed was to take effect (Braman, 26 N. Y. 483) or that a lien was created. Boyd, 176 N. Y. 613. 

B. Exceptions to Rule: 

1. Opinions of Ordinary Lay Witnesses: 

a. Generally: Opinion evidence of an ordinary lay witness is competent when the facts cannot be stated in such a manner as to enable the jury to form an accurate judgment therefrom and no better evidence than such opinion is obtainable. 

Peo. v. Creasy, 236 N. Y. 205. From the nature of the subject to be investigated, there must be no other means of stating the result of the witness' observation. Collins, 109 N. Y. 423. Thus, no mere description of color of hair or number of wrinkles would convey a very accurate impression as to the age of a person. DeWitt, 17 N. Y. 

340. The witness may state the instantaneous conclusion formed by his mind as to the appearance, condition, or mental or physical state of persons, animals, and things presented to his perceptive faculties simultaneously. Although the 109

witness, in a sense, is stating his opinion or conclusion, he is also testifying to a "collective fact," which is admissible. An ordinary lay witness speaking from ordinary, everyday knowledge may express his opinion on the following subjects: b. Solids and Liquids: Their size, quantity, color, light, darkness, weight, kind, taste or smell, such characterizations being true sense impressions, (Miller, 104 A. D. 33); that liquor he drank was intoxicating liquor (Peo. v. Marx, 128 A. D. 828), but the basis of observation must be taste, and not sight (Matter of Search, 204 A. D. 185); or that a red stain was blood and not paint (Peo. v. 

Burgess, 153 N. Y. 561), but not whether it was human or animal blood. Lindsay, 63 N. Y. 49. 

c. Physical Condition of Body: That a person was dead at a certain time (Hermann, 75 Misc. 599), or was conscious or unconscious (Watson, 158 

A. D. 59), that a woman appeared to be pregnant (Sturges, 18 Wkly. Dig. 266), that a person appeared to be in good or bad health (Highbee, 53 

N. Y. 603, but see Valentine, 106 A. D. 487, holding that a witness cannot testify that her son needed medical attention), or appeared to be in agony, or looked emaciated and feeble. Webb, 51 

A. D. 194. Also, a lay witness may describe the changed physical demeanor of a person following an accident. Hamel, 59 A. D. 135. d. Emotional Characteristics: As that a man was grasping another in a "friendly" way (Blake, 73 

N. Y. 586), but not that two persons seemed affectionately attached to each other. Pearce, 207 

N. Y. 506. e. Intoxication: That a certain person was drunk or sober at any given time. Peo. v. Packenhorn, 115 N. Y. 200; Molissani, 152 Misc. 270. f. Sanity: An ordinary lay observer is not qualified to state whether a man is sane or insane, (Peo. v. 

Koerner, 154 N. Y. 355) except that a subscribing

OPINION EVIDENCE 

111 witness to a will may testify as to testator's testamentary capacity, as evidenced by his conduct at time of execution of will. Coleman, 111 N. Y. 

220. See C.P.A. 354. 

(1) Rationality of Acts: After describing a per son's acts, the lay witness may characterize them (but not the person) as being rational or irrational. Peo. v. Pekarz, 185 N. Y. 470. 

The witness may give no direct opinion as to the latter's sanity. Hewlett, 55 N. Y. 634. The federal rule is otherwise. Turner, 213 U. S. 

257. (Note that an alienist who has examined a patient's sanity may state his opinion as to such sanity without first stating the symptoms upon which he bases his opinion. Peo. v. Faber, 199 N. Y. 256.) 

g. Resemblance or Identity: Of a person, or article of personal property. King, 72 N. Y. 607. Direct and positive evidence of identification is not usually available and is therefore not required. 

Peo. v. Strollo, 191 N. Y. 42. But a witness cannot testify that he "thought" or was "impressed" as to the subject's identity or resemblance. Peo. v. Williams, 29 Hun 520. A lay witness may testify that a bust of a person known to him is a correct likeness (Schwartz, 21 N. Y. S. 1053); or that certain footprints resembled each other (Hotchkiss, 5 Hun 90); but the identity of fingerprints is for an expert. Peo. v. Roach, 215 N. Y. 592. (1) Identification by Voice: Is permissible if the speaker can be identified. Wilbur, 35 Barb. 

303. If the witness knows the speaker, he may identify him by his voice, even though he speaks over the telephone. Murphy, 142

N. Y. 215. The same rule applies even if he does not know the speaker over the telephone, but subsequently meets him and recognizes his voice. Peo. v. Dunbar, 215 N. Y. 

416. The fact that a person answered the

telephone and stated he was the person asked for is not sufficient to identify him; the witness must state he recognized the voice, or the speaker's identity must be established with reasonable certainty by other evidence (Bonner, 169 N. Y. S. 43; Mankes, 163 A. D. 

789); as for example, that the speaker on several occasions, on the telephone, discussed intimate details of his stock account with the witness. Woodruff, 112 Misc. 489 (where the witness also recognized the voice at the trial). h. Age: A lay witness may testify as to the apparent age of another person (Hartshorn, 55 

A. D. 471) and to his own age, although the latter is founded on hearsay. Koester, 194 N. Y. 92. i. Solvency: An ordinary witness may state what a person's financial reputation is or was at a given time (Iselin v. Peck, 2 Rob. 629), but he may not give his opinion as to that person's solvency or insolvency at such time. York, 31 Hun 446. 

j. Ownership of Property: Is frequently a question of law, and a witness' opinion thereon is inadmissible. Hess, 75 A. D. 619; Pichler, 171 N. Y. 577, (e.g., that a certain person had no title to certain. piece of realty). But where such ownership is so plain and uncontroverted as to be patently a matter of fact, and title is not a direct issue of the case, a witness may state his opinion as to such ownership. Bunke, 188 N. Y. 600. k. Payments on Indebtedness: A witness may not testify that one person is or is not indebted to another, since that would be a conclusion of law (Pope, 58 Hun 294) but he may be asked if any payments have been made on a given indebtedness, since that is primarily a question of fact. 

Ramsay, 12 N. Y. S. 726. 

1. Intent, Motive or Belief: A witness may not state what he thinks was the intent, motive or belief which actuated a given person (Bogart, 200 N. Y. 

379); but he may describe what his own inten-

OPINION EVIDENCE 

113 tions were. Noonan, 206 N. Y. 105. The witness cannot speculate as to what he would have done had he known certain facts. Marine Trust Co., 240 A. D. 176. m. Speed: An ordinary lay witness may testify to the speed of a train, auto or other moving object, when it passed him or when he saw it at a given place or time. Flanagan, 70 A. D. 505, aff'd 173 

N. Y. 631; Waldele, 4 A. D. 549. The witness need not give the exact rate of speed, but may characterize it as fast (Ehrman, 23 A. D. 21) or very rapid. Hickey, 8 A. D. 123. (1) The witness must first be qualified by showing that he has had some experience in observing the rate of speed of moving objects. 

At least, he must be able to give some reasonable basis for his opinion (Salter, 59 N. Y. 

631), as by having frequently ridden on trains, etc., and occasionally timed their speed. Kitay, 23 A. D. 228. The witness' opinion of the speed must be based on sight and not hearing. Parsons, 133 A. D. 461. 

n. Value: Of real or personal property may be testi fied to by a lay witness if he has seen the property in question and is familiar with the value of similar property (Clark, 9 N. Y. 183) e.g., the value of household furniture (Myerson, 27 N. Y. S. 

112), or wearing apparel. Merrill, 30 N. Y. 594.

But his opinion must be confined to value at a given time; he may not state what such value would have been if a certain contingency had, or had not, happened. Roberts, 128 N. Y. 455. (1) Of Services: A witness may testify to the value of his own services. Helmuth, 17 Misc. 

623. He may also give his opinion as to the value of particular services of another, if he knows their nature and character, and has observed the rendition of similar services, (Manhattan Delivery Co., 98 N. Y. S. 844) but the witness need not have seen the actual

services, if he has heard the testimony as to their nature and character. McCollum, 62 

N. Y. 316. o. Damages: A witness will not be allowed to state his opinion as to plaintiff's or defendant's damages; he must confine his opinion to the value of the constituent elements of damage. Reed, 101 

N. Y. 270; Avery, 121 N. Y. 31. p. Causation: Where that is the issue before the jury, a lay witness may not, ordinarily, be asked as to what caused a certain effect, as, for instance, what caused a boiler explosion (O'Doherty, 113 

A. D. 636) or what caused a pot to slip from a hook (Wells, 186 N. Y. 319) or whether a blow caused deafness. Stevens, 25 Hun 54. q. Handwriting: An ordinary witness is competent to express an opinion as to the genuineness or identity of the handwriting of another person, provided he is shown to be familiar with such person's handwriting (Peo. v. Corey, 148 N. Y. 

476; Burbank, 104 A. D. 312, aff'd 185 N. Y. 559) usually by proof that: (1) he has seen him write at least once (Hammond, 54 N. Y. 398), or, (2) has seen writings acknowledged by the party to be in his handwriting (Johnson, 19 Johns. 

134), or, (3) has received letters from the party in response to communications from the witness. Hynes, 82 N. Y. 41. r. Negative Inferences: A witness who was in a position to hear a particular sound, if made, may testify to the improbability of the sound having been made, because of his having failed to hear it. Steven, 39 N. Y. S. 944. But a witness cannot testify that a third person could not have overheard a conversation from a particular position without first stating the particulars as to distance, acoustics, etc. See also Curtis, 117 A. D. 

349; McLaughlin, 141 N. Y. 76.

OPINION EVIDENCE 

115 

II. Expert Testimony: 

A. Generally: The necessity for expert testimony is that the nature of the subject under inquiry requires technical, scientific or special knowledge. Goldstein, 160 Misc. 

364. But, ultimately, the question of weight, to be given the expert witness' testimony, like that of any other witness, is for the jury. Com. Cas. Ins. Co., 269 N. Y. 

451. The expert witness can testify both to facts and his opinions or conclusions based thereon; but if the jury is competent to draw the conclusion, not even the expert can testify as to anything but facts. Dougherty, 163 N. Y. 527 (experts held competent to testify as to the manner of construction of a derrick, but since no scientific knowledge is necessary to draw a conclusion as to the safety of the construction, that conclusion is for jury). Where a witness is offered as an expert, two questions arise: (1) Is the subject one on which expert testimony is admissible?; (2) Is the witness qualified as an expert? These questions are for the court. Peo. v. Polstein, 184 A. D. 260. 

1. Illustrations: Expert testimony has been admitted to explain bullet markings (Peo. v. Soper, 243 N. Y. 

320, but even a lay witness may testify that a pistol which he examined had not been fired recently because the barrel was cold, Peo. v. Driscoll, 107 N. Y. 

414); to explain X-ray plates properly in evidence (Marion, 216 N. Y. 178); to describe proper ceiling repairs (Slater, 207 A D. 413); to state whether the grade of a sidewalk on which a person slipped was excessive (Glynne, 198 N. Y. S. 751); whether a vessel was skilfully handled (Carpenter, 71 N. Y. 

574); whether a bank vault was properly placed (Wolfe, 139 A. D. 848); whether symptoms were feigned by a patient (Peo. v. Koerner, 154 N. Y. 

355); whether shutting off steam causes smoke to rise from a locomotive (Harrison, 195 N. Y. 862); to explain technical terms in an insurance policy. 

Nelson, 71 N. Y. 453. On the other hand, expert testimony has been held inadmissible to prove whether a certain number of men are sufficient to

erect a telephone pole safely (Hall, 168 A. D. 396); whether a sidewalk was broken because stones were piled on it (Parish, 160 N. Y. 302); whether a bullet wound was self-inflicted (Peo. v. Creasy, 236 N. Y. 

205); whether the spreading of railroad tracks caused a derailment (Schultz, 181 N. Y. 333); whether it was safe to start a fire under conditions present (Ferguson, 97 N. Y. 507); whether it is good practice to maintain a worn steel plate as a saddle in the entranceway to a public building (Jean, 255 A. D. 279); whether goods on deck of boat were adequately covered from rain. Schwinger, 105 N. Y. 648. See also Goodman, 222 N. Y. 121, holding it improper to ask a fireman how long before he arrived a fire had been burning.

B. Qualifying the Witness: An expert witness may be qualified as such by proof of actual experience, study or observation in the field upon which he is offered as an expert. McCormack, 61 Misc. 601. Generally, the witness must be a member of the profession to which the subject matter of his testimony relates. Thus, a physician who is not an osteopath, may not give an opinion as to osteopathic treatment. Granger, 187 Mo. 197. Similarly, a professional nurse is incompetent to testify, as an expert on the value of the services of a non-professional nurse. Scheu, 119 A. D. 825. However, it has been held that a physician, who is not a veterinary, may testify as to the nature of an animal's disease. Goodrich, 3 Park Cr. 

622, aff'd 19 N. Y. 574. The expert should be qualified before he starts to testify, and this should not be left to cross-examination (Dolan, 105 A. D. 366); but it has been held that proper qualification may be brought out during cross-examination. Kitay, 23 A. D. 228. 

1. Manner of Examination: The expert may be ex amined in either of two ways: a. If he is familiar with the facts from personal observation and so testifies, he may be asked directly for his opinion. He need not state the facts upon which the opinion is based, although such facts may always be brought out either on

OPINION EVIDENCE 

117 direct or cross-examination. 

Christastie, 195 

N. Y. S. 156. On the other hand, the expert cannot be precluded from stating the reasons for his opinion. Johnson Service Co., 142 A. D. 677. b. If he is not familiar with the facts, they must be stated to him hypothetically, and upon the assumption of the facts as so stated, he must base his opinion. Christastie, supra. (1) Hypothetical Questions: In wording the hypothetical question, counsel may assume facts according to his theory of the case (Cowley, 83 N. Y. 464), and the jury must determine whether the facts have been proved which are thus assumed. Reynolds, 64 N. Y. 589. The hypothetical question need not include all the facts in evidence. Peo. v. Truck, 170 N. Y. 

203. If the question assumes material facts not supported by the evidence introduced, it should be excluded. Peo. v. Tuczkewitz, 149 

N. Y. 240; Peo. v. Patrick, 182 N. Y. 131, 172.

The proper way to put the hypothetical question is to state the salient facts of the case, fairly and accurately, and then ask the expert for his opinion. Peo. v. McElvaine, 121 N. Y. 

250. An expert may not be asked to express an opinion on facts as "he has heard them given in evidence" or "as shown" or "proved by the evidence" since that would leave it to the expert to decide for himself what facts he considered to be proved. Marx, 211 N. Y. 33. a. Case: On a murder trial, an alienist called by the state and properly qualified, testified, over D's objection, that based upon a history of D's actions and conduct as related to him by D's mother and sister, he was of the opinion that D was sane. Held, error. Experts called to testify concerning the sanity of D in a criminal case are limited to opinions based upon proper hypothetical questions and opinions based upon personal observation and examination. But opinions based in whole or part upon statements, other than those of the subject, as related to the expert, are

inadmissible, because the statements of the third parties cannot be subjected to oath like those of witnesses before the jury. Peo. v. Keough, 276 

N. Y. 141.

C. Medical Experts: In order for a physician to qualify as an expert, it must appear that he has had such training. or experience that his opinion will be of some value to the jury. However, he need not be a specialist (Pierson, 47 Barb. 243), or engaged in active practice (Roberts, 58 N. Y. 613) or even have a diploma. Peo. v. Rice, 159 

N. Y. 400. 

1. On What Subjects Admissible: A medical expert may testify as to the causes, present condition, including symptoms and manifestations, and probable effects of a certain physical or mental condition. 

Stouter, 127 N. Y. 661. He may give his opinion as to the probable consequences and duration of a present condition (Griswold, 115 N. Y. 61) but no opinion as to the possible outbreak of new and different diseases even though having their cause in the original injury, (Cross, 200 N. Y. 393) because his opinion must not relate to future probabilities which are too conjectural or speculative. Strohm, 96 N. Y. 305.

D. Handwriting: As discussed, supra, an ordinary witness may testify as to the genuineness and identity of a handwriting, but cannot make comparisons between genuine and disputed handwriting. An expert, however, may so testify, not on his knowledge of the person's handwriting, but on comparing the disputed writing with one proved to the satisfaction of the judge, or conceded to be, a genuine specimen. C. P. A. 332. If the genuineness of the standard of comparison is not conceded, common law evidence must be introduced to show it is genuine (i.e., by persons familiar with the handwriting). Peo. v. Molineux, 168 N. Y. 459. Testimony of experts cannot be introduced to show by comparison with other admitted or proved writings that the "standard of comparison" is genuine. Turnure, 195 

A. D. 200, aff'd 233 N. Y. 649. Where the original is not in evidence, a photostatic copy of a writing cannot be

OPINION EVIDENCE 

119 used as a "standard of comparison." Hynes, 82 N. Y. 41. 

1. Cross-Examination: The usual procedure is to sub mit to the expert other specimens of handwriting, not all genuine, ask him to compare them with the "standard of comparison" in evidence, and to pick out the genuine specimens. Hoag, 174 N. Y. 36. 

2. Weight of Testimony: A handwriting expert's opin ion should be received and weighed with caution. 

Ferguson, 97 N. Y. 507. It may even be entitled to less weight than the testimony of a lay witness who saw the writing executed. Card, 68 A. D. 327. It has also been held that testimony that a signature is genuine is entitled to greater weight than testimony that it is spurious. Sarvent, 26 Hun 550, aff'd 91 

N. Y. 662. 

3. Proof as to Identity of Marks: An expert acquainted with standard typewriters may state his opinion as to whether two typewritten papers were written on the same machine. Peo. v. Storrs, 207 N. Y. 147. But a handwriting expert may not testify that an "X" signature was or was not made by a given person. (In re Corcoran, 129 N. Y. S. 165) or that a certain person drew ink lines (not writing) through written matter for the purpose of striking out the written matter. Comparison of finger prints is a proper subject of expert testimony. Peo. v. Roach, 215 N. Y. 592.

CHAPTER VIII. 

; PRIVILEGED COM- MUNICATIONS; TRANSACTIONS WITH DECEDENTS. 

I. General Competency: 

A. Necessity of Oath: 

1. Generally: At common law, a person who did not believe in a God who would punish him if he swore falsely was incompetent as a witness. 1 Stark. Ev. 

22. At one time, in England, only Christians could be witnesses. 7 Coke 17 b. The first notable statutory exception was in favor of Quakers, who were allowed to affirm in civil actions, where others were required to swear. In the United States generally, and in New York, particularly, a person is not rendered incompetent as a witness on account of his religion (N. Y. Const., Art. 1, sec. 3) but if his religious principles do not countenance an oath, he may affirm (C.P.A. sec. 362); or, if he is not a 

Christian, he "may be sworn according to the peculiar ceremonies, if any, of his religion." C.P.A. sec. 364. 

2. Cross-Examination: A 

A witness may be crossexamined as to his competency. Woodworth, 48 N. Y. 

S. 80. It is the rule, in other jurisdictions, that crossexamination as to the facts of his religious belief, is proper for the purpose of affecting his credibility. 

Free, 59 N. H. 219; Blair, 26 Pa. 274. But in Brink, 176 N. Y. 150, it was held there can be no direct inquiry into the religion of the witness for such a purpose, although he may be interrogated, on crossexamination, as to whether he thinks it wrong to give false testimony. Cf. Stanbro, 28 Barb. (N. Y.) 265. 

3. Effect of Unsworn Testimony: In a civil case, un sworn testimony is inadmissible (Ranofsky, 203 N. Y.

S. 160); but if it gets to the jury without objection from the adverse party, the defect is waived. Peo. ex rel Neibuhr, 184 N. Y. 304. But see Scherback, 283 N. Y. S. 804, holding that if a child's unsworn 120

testimony is admitted, even though without objection, it is reversible error, unless there be other proof in support of the determination. In a criminal case, unsworn testimony is also inadmissible, except that a child, who is, or appears to be, under twelve years of age, and who does not understand the nature of an oath, may, in the discretion of the court, testify without an oath, if he is otherwise sufficiently intelligent. But his uncorroborated testimony will not support a conviction. Peo. v. Johnson, 185 N. Y. 

219; C. Cr. Proc., sec. 392. If the child, although under twelve, has the capacity to comprehend the obligation of an oath, and is sworn, corroboration of the child's testimony is, apparently, not necessary to support a conviction. Peo. v. Taraylea, 271 N. Y. S. 1. 

B. Mental Incapacity: As a general rule, an insane person, or one non compos mentis, is incompetent as a witness. 

Hammond, 230 N. Y. S. 621. But if such a person possesses sufficient intelligence to be truthful and to correctly narrate facts and occurrences, he may be permitted to testify. Barker, 200 N. Y. 280; Peo. v. N. Y. 

Hospital, 31 Abb. N. C. 229. Under C.P.A. 365, the court, in its discretion, may make a preliminary inquiry into the mental condition of a witness. Ellarson, 190 

N. Y. S. 6. 

1. Deaf Mutes: May testify in writing, by signs through an interpreter, or by any other means approved by the court. Peo. v. McGee, 1 Denio 19. 

2. Intoxication: To such a degree as to render a witness incapable of narrating facts and events in a reliable way, and of realizing the sanctity of an oath, disqualifies him as a witness. Whether a witness is intoxicated to such a degree may be determined by the court from personal inspection. Hartford, 16 

Johns. 143.

C. Infancy: There is no arbitrary age at which an infant is deemed to be competent as a witness. Such competency depends upon the "capacity and intelligence of the child, his appreciation of the difference between truth and

falsehood," and upon his understanding of the nature of an oath to tell the truth. Wheeler, 159 U. S. 523 (five year old infant held competent); Agnew, 5 N. Y. S. 

756, aff'd 117 N. Y. 651, (six and a half year old child held competent). In criminal cases, a child under twelve is presumed incompetent to testify (Peo. v. Klein, 266 

N. Y. 188); competency not being presumed until the age of twelve. C. Cr. Proc. sec. 392. Thus, in a criminal case, the court cannot refuse to give a preliminary examination to a child ten years old in order to determine his competency. Peo. v. Taraylea, supra. In civil cases, a child over fourteen is presumed competent. Peo. v. 

Linzey, 29 N. Y. S. 560. But where he is under that age, the court must determine his competency by a preliminary examination, and to permit the child to testify without such preliminary examination is ground for reversal. Olshansky, 172 N. Y. S. 856. In a civil case, if a child is held competent to testify, he must be sworn, irrespective of his age, and failure to do so constitutes reversible error. Ranofsky, 203 N. Y. S. 160; Grace, 162 

N. Y. S. 1121.

D. Persons Convicted of Crime: The common law rule was that persons convicted of infamous crimes were incompetent as witnesses. Peo. v. Whipple, 9 Cow. 707. This rule has been abolished in New York (Pen. Law, sec. 

2444; C.P.A. 350); and the present rule is that such a person is a competent witness, but that the conviction. may be proved to affect the weight of his testimony. 

Peo. v. McGloin, 91 N. Y. 241; Shea, 193 N. Y. S. 693, aff'd 235 N. Y. 529. Under the statute it is never permissible to inquire into anything less than an actual conviction. McWharf, 225 N. Y. S. 761. The federal rule is substantially the same. Rosen, 245 U. S. 467.

E. Interested Parties: At common law, a party to an action, or one in interest, was incompetent to testify. 

This disability has been removed (C.P.A. 346) with one exception relating to personal transactions with. decedents or incompetents (C.P.A. 347). Matter of Fitzpatrick, 252 N. Y. 121. Interest in the action may of course be shown to affect weight of the testimony.

F. Husband and Wife: At common law, a husband or a wife was incompetent to testify either for or against the other because of identity of interest. Wilke v. Peo., 53 

N. Y. 525. This disqualification has been largely removed, (Penal Law, sec. 2445; C.P.A. 346), but still exists, to some extent, in actions for criminal conversation and in actions founded on allegations of adultery. 

C.P.A. 346, 349. See also Botwinick, 198 N. Y. S. 151. 

G. Judges: A judge is incompetent as a witness in an action over which he is the sole presiding judge. Morse, 11 

Barb. 510. Where the court is composed of more than one judge, a judge may descend from the bench and testify, if his services are not needed as a member of the court; but he should not thereafter return to the bench. Peo. v. Dohring, 59 N. Y. 374. 

H. Attorneys: May testify in behalf of their clients like any other witness (Greenfield, 101 N. Y. S. 567); but the practice is not favored. Kissinger, 200 N. Y. S. 599.

I. Jurors: 

1. Generally: A juror may testify as a witness. Peo. v. 

Dohring, supra. But he may not testify to what took place in the jury room for the purpose of impeaching the verdict. Peo. v. Sprague, 217 N. Y. 373.

However, where no attack on the verdict is made, a juror may testify as to proceedings in the jury room, as in contempt proceedings against another juror (Peo. ex rel Nunns, 176 N. Y. S. 858); or on a motion for a new trial, to show that the verdict was erroneously reported to the court. Dalrymple, 63 

N. Y. 361. 

2. Grand Jurors: Production of the minutes of a grand jury, may be ordered, or any of the members may be before it, whenever deemed necessary by the trial court. Peo. v. Naughton, 38 How. Pr. 430; Peo. v. 

Goodheim, 176 N. Y. S. 468 (on a trial of a witness for perjury); Peo. ex rel Hirschberg, 251 N. Y. 156 (proceedings to remove a district attorney). But a grand juror may not testify how he or his co-jurors voted or as to opinions expressed by them. C. Cr. 

Proc., sec. 265.

II. Privileged Communications: 

A. Generally: On the ground of public policy, certain privileged or confidential communications, when made under certain circumstances, are inadmissible in evidence. The privilege, in New York, is given by statute and if the communication is not protected by statute it is not privileged. Peo. ex rel Mooney, 269 N. Y. 291 (holding that a newspaper reporter's sources of information are not privileged against disclosure). However, communications in the nature of state secrets are privileged on principles of public policy. In addition, "public policy demands, and it is a sound doctrine, that those standing in confidential relation with a judge of a court of record, as a clerk and stenographer, with regard to statements in proceedings pending before the court, should not be compelled or allowed to betray the trust and confidence which such relationship implies." 

Matter of Cohen, 174 N. Y. S. 427. On the same principle, "communications to the district attorney in connection with possible prosecutions of crime should not be disclosed, with the attendant deterrent effect upon people coming forward to furnish information, or with a corresponding deterrent effect upon freedom of utterance by those forcibly brought forward to be interrogated by the district attorney in advance of the tendering, if it becomes necessary, of them to the grand jury or elsewhere." Lewis, 131 N. Y. 177; Peo. ex rel Lemon, 245 N. Y. 24. But in Peo. v. Walsh, 262 N. Y. 140, a witness in a criminal prosecution testified that he had given a written statement to the district attorney. It was held that the latter could be compelled to produce same and if from a preliminary inspection the court is convinced that it shows contradictory matter, it may be used on cross-examination to impeach the credibility of the witness, provided, the statement contains no matter prejudicial to public interest. See also Peo. v. Giallarenzi, 268 N. Y. S. 488. It is doubtful whether discovery and inspection may be had in criminal proceedings. Cf. C. 

Cr. Proc. sec. 392.

B. Clergyman and Penitent: At common law, confessions

made to priests, or spiritual advisers, were not deemed privileged. In New York, C.P.A. 351 provides that a clergyman, or other minister of any religion, shall not be allowed to disclose a confession made to him in his professional character, in the course of discipline enjoined by the rules or practice of his church. Peo. v. 

Gates, 13 Wend. 311. Only the penitent can waive the privilege. C.P.A. 354. 

C. Attorney and Client: 

1. Generally: C.P.A. 353 provides that an attorney or counselor shall not be allowed to disclose a communication made to him by his client, or his advice given thereon, in the course of his professional employment. The same rule applies to the attorney's clerk, stenographer or other person employed by him. Re Putnam's Will, 257 N. Y. 140. a. Object of Rule: The rule prevents both client and attorney from being compelled to testify as to privileged communications of either. Bolt, 198 

N. Y. S. 616. However, only the client may claim the benefit of the privilege, and if the client waives it, the attorney must testify. Peo. ex rel
