Vogelstein, 270 N. Y. S. 362. The object of the rule is, as it was at common law, to encourage prospective litigants to disclose freely the facts. in reference to which they seek advice, without fear that such facts will be published by their attorney. Hurlburt, 128 N. Y. 420; Doheny, 168 

N. Y. 213. 

2. Attorney and Client Relationship Necessary: 

a. Attorney Must Be Licensed: It must appear that the attorney was duly licensed to practice. Bacon, 80 N. Y. 394. The fact that the client believed he was consulting an attorney, when he was not, will not render his statements privileged. Wallace, 143 N. Y. S. 1148, aff'd 216 N. Y. 28. (1) To practice law one must conform to all the rules laid down for admission to the bar, take the oath prescribed, and become an officer of the court, with all the rights, duties and obligations incident to that position. Any person who falsely holds him-

self out to be an attorney, appears for a party in court, or draws documents by which legal rights are secured, is guilty of a misdemeanor. Penal Law 

270. See Peo. v. Bruno, 244 N. Y. 55; Peo. v. Alfani, 227 N. Y. 334, rev'g 174 N. Y. S. 527. A corporation may not practice law directly or indirectly (Matter of Co-Operative Law Co, 198 N. Y. 479); but an incorporated title insurance company may lawfully draw, for a fee, such papers as a simple chattel mortgage, or bill of sale (Peo. v. Title Guarantee etc. Co., 227 N. Y. 386, rev'g 168 N. Y. S. 278) or a contract of sale, deed and mortgage of real estate, as part of the examination or insuring of property covered by one of its policies. Peo. v. Title 

Guarantee etc. Co., 181 N. Y. S. 52, aff'd 230 N. Y. 578. b. Payment of Retainer: Is unnecessary to constitute the attorney-client relationship. Nor need a suit be pending. It is sufficient that professional aid be sought, and the communications relate thereto. Bacon, supra. Conversations with an attorney consulted as a friend, or as a business adviser, and not in his professional capacity are not privileged. Kitz, 61 N. Y. S. 64. If the attorney refuses the case, communications previously made are nevertheless privileged. But communications thereafter made, or after his employment has terminated, are not privileged. Lawless, 264 N. Y. S. 280 (holding that where two parties mutually interested seek advice from an attorney, the latter may later testify as to what was said by either in an action between the two, but not in an action against them by a stranger). See also Wallace, 216 N. Y. 28; Root, 84 N. Y. 72. c. Communication Must Be Professional and Confidential: To be privileged, the communication must be professional. Lifschitz, 127 N. Y. S. 1091.

To be professional, it must relate to a matter as to which the attorney is professionally consulted. 

Hampton, 46 Hun. 151; Avery, 102 N. Y. S. 12.

The communication must also be confidential. 

Bauman, 213 N. Y. 328. If made in the presence of a stranger (Doheny, 168 N. Y. 213) or with

the intention that it be repeated (Rosseau, 131 

N. Y. 177) it is not privileged. (1) Case: A retained X, an attorney to draw a deed from A to B and told X to deliver the deed to B on A's death, which occurred several months later. 

P, as administrator of A, on behalf of A's creditors, now seeks to set aside the deed as a fraud upon creditors. B calls X to testify to the fact that A delivered the deed to him with instructions to deliver to B. (In such case, delivery to B would relate back to the time of delivery to X. Munoz, 111 N. Y. 295.) 

May X, over P's objection of privileged communication, be permitted to testify to the above? Held, Yes. "But even if the relation of attorney and client had been shown to exist, the attorney was still a competent witness to prove the delivery of the deed to him for the purpose of delivering it to another. 

Such a communication from client to attorney is not within the prohibition of the section of the Code above cited and the attorney was always competent to prove it, as from its very nature, it was not made in professional confidence nor intended to be confidential, but, on the contrary, was intended to be imparted to another." Rosseau, 226 N. Y. S. 70. 

See also In re Decker's Est., 268 N. Y. S. 280. 

3. Communications Between Attorney and Witness of a Party: Are not privileged. Bergmann, 125 N. Y. S. 973. 

4. What Disclosures May Be Compelled From At torney: An attorney may be compelled to reveal the names and addresses of his clients (those who retain, pay and instruct the attorney even though not parties to the record) in the action in which he represents them (R. C. P. 9a; Peo. ex rel Vogelstein, supra); but not after their relationship has ended. (Re 

Trainor, 130 N. Y. S. 682); nor where the clients, whom he represented in other litigation, are not yet parties to the present action. Brooklyn Savings 

Bank, 260 N. Y. S. 508; Neugass, 249 N. Y. S. 631.

An attorney may also be compelled to reveal whether certain papers are in existence and where they are, so that the adverse party may sustain a notice to produce them. Dunn, 92 N. Y. S. 787. But he may not be asked to disclose whether a certain signature

is that of his client. Holtharisen, 18 N. Y. St. Rep. 

360, aff'd 120 N. Y. 622. 

5. Consultation for Illegal Purpose: Only those con fidences between attorney and client which are properly within the scope of a lawful employment are privileged, and therefore a client who consults an attorney for guidance in connection with a contemplated (as distinguished from an already committed) violation of law is not protected. Peo. ex rel 

Vogelstein, supra. Peo. v. Hunter, 249 N. Y. S. 66. 

6. Time During Which Privilege Exists: A communi cation which is privileged, when made, remains privileged even after the attorney-client relationship has been terminated by act of the parties or by death of the client. The right to waive the privilege does not survive the client's death. Matter of Cunnion, 201 N. Y. 123. 

7. Waiver of Privilege: Under C.P.A. 354, the client's waiver of a privileged communication must be express. He may waive the privilege in open court on the trial of the action or proceeding, either by expressly announcing his waiver, or by voluntarily testifying on direct examination to the privileged communication. Peo. v. Patrick, 182 N. Y. 131. But if privileged matter is brought out on cross-examination, no waiver results. Kaufman, 90 N. Y. S. 205, aff'd 183 N. Y. 562. In addition, the parties or their attorneys, prior to the trial, may stipulate in writing for such waiver; but a paper unilaterally executed by a party, prior to the trial, providing therefor, is insufficient to effect a waiver. C.P.A. 354. a. In Will Cases: By requesting an attorney to subscribe a will as a witness, a testator waives his privilege as to communications made or things done in the course of drafting the will. C.P.A. sec. 354; Matter of Cunnion, supra; Matter of 

Eno, 187 N.Y.S. 756. 

(1) Testimony of Non-Subscribing Attorney 

Who Drafted Will: C.P.A. 354, as amended, now provides that an attorney or his em-

ployee, may, in a will probate, or other proceeding involving the will, testify as to the preparation and execution of the will, or of any prior will, indenture or deed of trust, affecting the will in question, whether or not the attorney or employee is a subscribing witness thereon, provided that no disclosure of any confidential communication shall be made. tending to disgrace the memory of the deceased. Cf. Matter of McCulloch, 263 N. Y. 408. 

8. Determination of Question of Privilege: The object ing party has the burden of showing the communication to be privileged (Katz, 61 N. Y. S. 64); the question being for the court. Peo. v. Hess, 40 N. Y. 

S. 486. Notice that the objection is to the competency of the witness who seeks to testify to the privileged communication.

D. Husband and Wife: 

1. Generally: At common law, both in civil and criminal actions, a husband or a wife was generally held incompetent to testify either for or against the other. 

While this disqualification has been largely removed by statute, confidential communications between husband and wife still remain privileged. Stillman, 187

N. Y. S. 383 (holding that on an application by a wife for alimony pendente lite, in a divorce action, the husband cannot incorporate in his opposing affidavits, a letter alleged to have been written by the wife to him, tending to prove her guilty of the adultery). 

2. Statutory Provisions: In New York, by statute, a husband or wife is competent to testify either for or against the other in both civil and criminal actions. (C.P.A. 346; Penal Law, sec. 2445), subject to the following exceptions: 

a. In a Divorce Action: Neither spouse may testify against the other, except to prove the marriage, disprove adultery, or disprove the defenses of connivance, condonation, recrimination and the

statute of limitations. C.P.A. 349, 1153; Abbott, 228 N. Y. S. 611. (1) Case: P sues D, husband, for a divorce on the ground of adultery. D puts in an answer denying the material allegations of the complaint and there is a trial. Has P the burden of proof to show the truth of the allegations in her complaint that the adultery charged was committed without her "consent, command, connivance" etc.? Held, No. These are matters of affirmative defense. It is only in case of D's default that P must negative the existence' of these defenses. Olmstead, 206 N. Y. S. 289. 

b. In Action for Criminal Conversation: Plaintiff's wife is a competent witness for defendant, but not for her undivorced husband. C.P.A. 349. This is largely academic today. 

c. Penal Law, sec. 1091: Permits a wife to testify against her husband on a prosecution against him under Penal Law 1090 for forcing her to lead a life of prostitution. But this section must be read with Penal Law 2445 which prohibits disclosure of confidential communications. d. Bastardy Proceedings: In such proceedings, if the mother is married, both she and the husband may testify to non-access against the putative father. Inf. Crim. Ct. Act sec. 67 subd. 1. The constitutionality of this statute is doubtful. e. Confidential Communications: In any action, neither spouse may testify to a confidential communication, made during marriage, unless the other consents, except in a criminal prosecution for abandonment of an infant child. Penal Law, sec. 480. 

3. When Communications Privileged: To be privileged the communication must be confidential, because expressly made so, or because induced by the marital relation. Warner, 132 N. Y. 181. Thus where plaintiff sues defendant for libel in writing that plaintiff had illicit intercourse with X, plaintiff's husband is incompetent to testify as to whether he ever discussed X with plaintiff. Warner, supra. But ordinary conversations are usually not regarded as con-

fidential or induced by the marital relation. Pardee, 265 N. Y. S. 833. A communication made in the presence of a stranger (Peo. v. Lewis, 16 N. Y. S. 

881, aff'd 136 N. Y. 528) is not confidential, and any person overhearing the conversation may be compelled to testify. a. Illustrations: The following have been held not privileged 

(1) Communications in connection with ordinary business matters transacted between husband and wife. Grossman, 132 N. Y. S. 158. (2) Advice given by physician to his wife as a patient. 

Sheldon, 131 N. Y. S. 291. (3) Unfounded charges of adultery, abusive language and profanity used by a husband towards his wife. Millspaugh, 71 N. Y. S. 134. But testimony of a divorced husband that, prior to the divorce, his wife had confessed an act of adultery to him, has been held privileged. Yax, 213 N. Y. S. 4. 

4. Waiver of Privilege: The privilege as to confidential communications between husband and wife belongs to both, and consent of both is necessary to waive it. Peo. v. Wood, 126 N. Y. 249. Therefore, if either spouse testifies to the privileged communication with the consent of the other, the privilege is waived. 

But even though no objection is raised by the other spouse to improper testimony as to adultery, in a divorce action, the court must exclude the testimony. 

Bolognino, 241 N. Y. S. 445, holding the provisions of C.P.A. 349 cannot thus be waived, for reasons of public policy. Cf. Gunsberg, 195 N. Y. S. 29. The provisions of C.P.A. 354, with respect to waiver, do not apply to communications between husband. and wife. a. Possible Collusive Waiver: A spouse may waive a written privileged communication by making it public, or giving it to another, the communication thus losing its confidential aspects and becoming admissible as to him or her. Peo. v. Hayes, 140 

N. Y. 484. It would seem that where a written confidential communication is voluntarily put into the possession of a stranger, by the addressee, it should

continue to remain privileged, at least as to the addressor, since otherwise by collusion, the privilege could be defeated as to the addressor. 

5. Effect of Death or Divorce: Death or divorce does not terminate the privilege. But in an action for criminal conversation, the statutory prohibition against plaintiff's wife testifying for him does not extend to a divorced wife. Wottrich, 71 N. Y. 601. 

E. Physician and Patient: 

1. Generally: At common law, communications made by a patient to a physician were not privileged. Re 

Cushman's Will, 289 N. Y. S. 328. In New York under C.P.A. 352, a physician, surgeon or registered nurse is not allowed to disclose any information acquired in attending a patient in a professional capacity, which information was necessary to enable him or her to act in that capacity, except where the patient is a child under the age of sixteen and the information acquired indicates that the patient has been the victim of a crime. The privilege covers communications of both patient and physician. Galligano, 280 N. Y. S. 419 (holding that in an action to annul a marriage because of concealment of tubercular condition, plaintiff is entitled to a physical examination of defendant before trial unless defendant stipulates to waive the privilege as to all physicians who had treated her). 

2. Essentials of Statutory Rule: To bring the evidence of a physician within the statute, three elements must coincide: first, the relationship must be that of physician and patient; second, the information must be acquired while attending the patient; third, the information acquired must be to enable the physician to act in that capacity. Griffiths, 171 N. Y. 106. 

a. Duly Licensed Physician or Nurse: To come within the rule of exclusion, the physician or surgeon must be duly licensed, and the nurse must be a professional or registered nurse. C.P.A. 

352. Therefore, a confidential communication to a druggist (Weil, 21 N. Y. St. Rep. 427); or a

dentist (Howe, 132 N. Y. S. 837) is not privileged.

So, a communication made to a person who assumes to be a licensed physician but who is not, is not privileged. Weil, supra. Likewise, a non-professional or unregistered nurse may testify to confidential communications. Hobbs, 171 N. Y. S. 

190. The testimony of an interne, and the record made by him upon reception of the patient, has been held privileged where it appeared that the interne was the agent of the attending physician. 

Rarogiewicz, 242 N. Y. 590. b. Professional Attendance: In order for the rule to apply, the physician must attend the patient in a professional capacity. Meyer, 178 N. Y. 63.

Payment of a fee is not necessary (Bauch, 180 N. Y. S. 188); nor is it necessary that the patient himself should have actually employed the physician. Meyer, supra. A physician who had never treated an insured but conducted an autopsy can testify as to an aggravated heart condition since after death the corpse cannot be regarded as a "patient." Felska, 259 N. Y. S. 35. Again, where a physician in the employ of the defendant who ran over the patient, visited the latter not to treat him, but merely to ascertain the extent of his injuries, statements made to him by the patient are not privileged. Griffiths, 171 N. Y. 106. c. Necessity of Information Acquired: Generally, the physician may testify to any extraneous matter, communicated to him by the patient, which was not necessary for the purpose of treatment. Hoyt, 112 N. Y. 493. For instance, the statement of a prospective mother as to the putative father of her child (Peo. v. Abrahams, 88 N. Y. S. 924), or the statement of the victim of an accident as to the cause thereof, or as to his conduct at the time, if unnecessary to his treatment is admissible. Griffiths, 171 N. Y. 106. 

Green, 171 N. Y. 201 (where plaintiff's leg was amputated by trolley car, held, his answer to sur-

geon's question as to how accident happened unnecessary to treatment and hence not privileged). But if it is necessary to ask the patient how the accident happened, in order to diagnose and treat his injuries, his responses are privileged. 

Nelson, 156 N. Y. 219. 

3. What Information Privileged: A physician may testify as to matters open to observation of anyone without expert or professional knowledge. Polachek, 263 N. Y. S. 230. He may testify that he attended a certain person professionally (Klein, 221 N. Y. 

449) and that the latter was sick (Keck, 264 N. Y. S. 

892, aff'd 264 N. Y. 422); but if the physician could not plainly observe that the patient was sick other than by expert determination after diagnosis he may not so testify. Lande, 271 N. Y. S. 551, aff'd 265 N.

Y. 655. The physician may testify that he performed an operation upon the patient (Sparen, 173 N. Y. S. 

673) and may give the places and dates of his visits. 

Cirrincioni, 228 N. Y. S. 354. (Hospital records are admissible to show dates of entry and discharge of the patient and that treatment was given. Palmer, 270 N. Y. S. 10.) But the physician may not be asked what he treated the patient for, or the nature of his ailment; (Poses, 281 N. Y. S. 126) or the cause of his death. Polachek, suprà. However, the physician can state that he recommended the taking of 

X-ray pictures. Entian, 279 N. Y. S. 580. 

4. Actions to Which Statute Applicable: C.P.A. 352 

applies to all actions and proceedings, civil and criminal. Renihan, 103 N. Y. 573. 

a. In Criminal Cases: C.P.A. 352 applies as long as the patient upon whom a crime has been committed is alive. Peo. v. Murphy, 101 N. Y. 126 (where upon discovery that the crime of abortion had been committed upon an adult female, the district attorney sent a doctor to examine and treat her, Held, the female being alive, the doctor is incompetent to testify as to what he found).

But in Peo. v. Lay, 254 A. D. 372, the testimony

of a physician who extracted a bullet from an adult female shot by defendant, was held admissible against defendant over the latter's objection of privileged communication, even though the female was alive at the trial, as long as the disclosure did not subject her to prosecution, damage her reputation or wound her feelings. (Note that 

Penal Law, Sec. 915 requires every physician attending a bullet wound to report same to police.)

If the patient dies defendant cannot invoke the protection of the statute for himself. Peo. v. 

Brecht, 192 N. Y. 581. (1) But "where the patient is a child under the age of sixteen and the information so acquired indicates that the patient has been the victim or subject of a crime, *** the physician or nurse may be required to testify fully in relation thereto." C.P.A. 352. 

5. Waiver of Privilege: a. Generally: The privilege created by C.P.A. 352 is not strictly personal to the patient, but may be asserted by his personal representative or by his assignee. It may also be asserted by a third person, or even by the court, on its own motion, if necessary, to prevent disparaging the reputation of a living person, or disgracing the memory of a deceased one. Edington, 67 N. Y. 185. But the privilege does not belong to the physician; he may be compelled to testify, if the patient or his representative does not object. Treiber, 134 N. Y. 

S. 267. b. Who May Waive: On a trial or examination, the patient or his personal representative may waive the privilege, but the latter cannot waive the disclosure of information which constituted a confidential communication between physician and the deceased patient (Re Cushman's Will, 289 N. Y.

S. 328) or the disclosure of such facts as would tend to disgrace the memory of the patient. 

C.P.A. 354. On a will contest, the executor named

in the will may waive, and so may the surviving husband or wife, or any heir at law or next of kin of the deceased, or any other party in interest. 

C.P.A. 354; Re Murphy 33 N. Y. S. 198; Re Hopkins, 77 N. Y. S. 178. (1) The term "personal representative" applies only to the executor or administrator of a deceased (Polachek, 263 N. Y. S. 230) and would not include a beneficiary under his life insurance policy. Beil, 80 N. Y. S. 751 (widow); Polachek, supra (infant son). 

c. How Waiver Effected: The privilege may be expressly waived in open court. A paper executed by a party, prior to the trial, providing for such waiver, as for instance, an express waiver in an insurance policy, is insufficient for the purpose. 

C.P.A. 354; Holden, 165 N. Y. 13. Attorneys for the respective parties may waive the privilege before trial by a stipulation in writing. Clifford, 188 N. Y. 349. If the patient takes the stand and testifies to the privileged matter, as by describing the injuries, an express waiver is effected (Hether, 233 N. Y. 370); or if the patient places his physician on the stand to so testify (Steinberg, 263 

N. Y. 45); or any other witness. Apter, 266 N. Y. 

333. If the patient disclosed to a third person the communication made to his doctor, it is no longer privileged. Deutschmann, 87 A. D. 503. Describing his injuries in his pleadings may be regarded as a waiver. Capron, 193 N. Y. 11 (where the court also relied on the additional factor that the patient took the stand and described his injuries). If the patient calls to the stand one of two physicians who attended him at about the same time, the second physician is free to testify. 

Morris, 148 N. Y. 88. If privileged testimony is brought out on cross-examination, it is not a sufficient waiver. Murphy, 157 N. Y. S. 962. Also, if the patient is present and does not object to the testimony, which would otherwise be protected by privilege, the privilege is waived (Peo.

v. Bloom, 193 N. Y. 1) but there is no such waiver where the patient is not present. Weil, 136 N. Y. S. 190. d. Duration of Waiver: A waiver once legally effected is general and permanent, and is not limited to a particular purpose, person, or trial. 

Morris, 148 N. Y. 88. Peo. v. Bloom, supra.

III. Transactions With Decedents or Incompetents: 

A. Generally: While the disqualification of a party, or person in interest, to testify in his own behalf has been removed (C.P.A. 346) one notable disqualification still remains in the provisions of C.P.A. 347.

B. Rule Stated: C.P.A., sec. 347 (as amended in 1935) now provides: "Upon the trial of an action or the hearing upon the merits of a special proceeding, a party or a person interested in the event, or a person from, through or under whom such a party or interested person derives his interest or title by assignment, or otherwise, shall not be examined, as a witness in his own behalf or interest against the executor, administrator or survivor of a deceased person or the committee of a lunatic, or a person deriving his title or interest from, through or under a deceased person or lunatic, by assignment or otherwise, concerning a personal transaction or communication between the witness and the deceased person or lunatic, except where the executor, administrator, survivor, committee or person so deriving title or interest is examined in his own behalf, or the testimony of the lunatic or deceased person is given in evidence, concerning the same transaction or communication. A person shall not be deemed interested, for the purposes of this section, by reason of being a stockholder or officer of any banking corporation which is a party to the action or proceeding, or interested in the event thereof. No party or person interested in the event, who is otherwise competent to testify, shall be disqualified from testifying by the possible imposition of costs against him or the award of costs to him. A party or person interested in the event or a person from, through or under whom such a party or interested person derives

his interest or title by assignment or otherwise, shall not be qualified for the purpose of this section, to testify in his own behalf or interest, or in behalf of the party succeeding to his title or interest, to personal transactions or communications with the donee of a power of appointment in an action or proceeding for the probate of a will, which exercises or attempts to exercise a power of appointment granted by the will of a donor of such power, or in an action or proceeding involving the construction of the will of the donee after its admission to probate."

C. Reason for Rule: Since a deceased person, or incompetent, is unable to testify in his own behalf, C.P.A. 

347 attempts to equalize the position of the parties by prohibiting an adverse party from testifying against the deceased's or the incompetent's representative with reference to personal transactions or communications. 

Griswold, 205 N. Y. 384. The party raising the objection has the burden of establishing the incompetency under the statute. Franklin, 219 N. Y. 409; Harrington, 231 N. Y. 278. Proof of a personal transaction with an agent of the deceased is competent even though the agent is also dead. Burke, 166 N. Y. S. 199.

D. Actions or Proceedings Contemplated by Statute: C.P.A. 

347 has been held applicable to every civil action and hearing on the merits of every special proceeding, both in courts of record and those not of record. Abbot, 204

N. Y. 223. It applies also to proceedings in the Surrogates courts, (Re Eysaman, 113 N. Y. 67); for instance, a proceeding to revoke letters of administration (Re 

Kelly, 238 N. Y. 71) but it would not apply to a proceeding where the decedent's estate has no interest, such as a transfer tax proceeding. Re Gould, 46 N. Y. S. 506.

E. Persons Disqualified Under Statute: 

1. Parties: C.P.A. 347 expressly applies to parties to the record. Bartlett, 278 N. Y. S. 578. A defendant who has withdrawn his answer or who has not interposed one is not a party. Boston, 170 N. Y.

554. The same is true of an executor or administrator suing or being sued in a representative capacity. McLaughlin, 141 N. Y. 76. 

2. Persons "Interested in Event": Are also disqualified.

The disqualifying interest is one in the result of the action and not necessarily in the precise question to be decided. Franklin, 219 N. Y. 409. The true test of interest is that the witness "will either gain or lose by the direct legal operation and effect of the judgment or that the record will be legal evidence for or against him in some other action." Wallace, 113 

N. Y. 238; Laka, 261 N. Y. 126. The disqualifying interest must also exist at the time the testimony is offered, and not at the time of the transaction in question. Re Demmerle, 225 N. Y. S. 190, 200. 

a. Persons Liable for Costs: Prior to the 1935 

amendment, which expressly covers the situation and provides to the contrary, a person who might become liable for costs upon failure of a suit against the representatives was held disqualified. 

Croker, 245 N. Y. 7. b. Creditors of Estate: Are interested persons as to the determination of their claims. Re Luppold's 

Estate, 262 N. Y. S. 522. But where the claim has been paid and satisfied, the claimant is competent to testify to establish the justice of the claim. Matter of Demmerle, supra; Matter of 

Lese, 163 N. Y. S. 1014. 

c. Executors, Administrators and Trustees: An executor is a competent witness on the probate of a will; his interest in his commissions not being sufficient to disqualify him as an "interested person" under C.P.A. 347. Re Wilson, 103 N. Y. 

374; Golland, 147 N. Y. S. 263. It has been held this is the rule even where the executor is given a legacy to compensate him for his services to the estate. Pruyn, 57 Barb. 176. But where the executor or administrator has a personal claim against the estate, otherwise, he is disqualified as an interested witness. Re Atkinson, 182 N. Y. S.

780; Re Berardini's Will, 264 N. Y. S. 479; Re 

Smith, 153 N. Y. 124. An executor is not "interested" in a controversy between beneficiaries in the estate. Farar, 83 N. Y. S. 218. d. Heirs at Law and Next of Kin: Who will inherit, if a will is declared void, are interested persons in will contests (Re Torkington, 29 N. Y. S. 443); but if the possibility of inheritance is uncertain, remote or contingent, there is no disqualification.

Thus a contestant, who was the statutory next of kin, and who would take the property if the testatrix was held to have died intestate, was held not disqualified, where there were three later wills in existence; his interest, therefore, not being a present, certain and vested interest. Re McCulloch's Will, 263 N. Y. 408. (1) Case: Ejectment. P claims as sole son and heir at law of X, deceased; the issue being X's marriage to 

L, P's mother. Is L a competent witness to prove the marriage? Held, Yes. L is not a party to the action nor one interested in the event. Eisenlord, 126

N. Y. 552. Contra, if L intervened claiming dower. 

Fisk, 148 N. Y. S. 501; Matter of Bush, 49 N. Y. S. 803. e. Husband and Wife: A spouse of a party to an action, or the spouse of one interested in the result thereof, is generally not a "person interested in the event." Laka, 261 N. Y. 126; Re 

Brush's Estate, 233 N. Y. S. 355 (where wife kept her earnings separate, Held, her husband might properly testify in her suit against estate for board furnished decedent). But if either spouse has a direct, financial interest in the outcome of the other's action, he or she is disqualified as a witness. Scheu, 121 N. Y. S. 122 (where husband and wife kept all earnings in one fund,

Held, husband could not testify in action brought by wife against estate to collect for nursing services rendered to decedent). (1) Case: A and B are husband and wife respectively. 

They both borrowed money from X giving back A's note. X died and P, administratrix, now sues A on

his note. Defense, payment. Is B a competent witness for A to prove payment? Held, yes. There is a possibility that if P gets judgment against A, the latter may proceed against B for B's share of the money loaned; but this possibility, because of the relationship of the parties, is so remote as not to disqualify B. Also, the possibility of B being liable to P is likewise too remote, since the note in suit was executed by A only. Laka, supra. f. Legatees and Devisees: Are persons "interested in the event" on a will contest, even though the legacy or devise is conditional. Hollender, 167 

N. Y. S. 824, aff'd 227 N. Y. 614. But if a person is named a legatee in a will, executed two wills prior to the one being probated, he may testify on the contest of the will offered for probate, as the intervening wills make his interest remote and contingent. Re McCulloch's Will, supra. g. Mortgagor: Is an interested person in any action which, if successful, would relieve him from liability on his bond or mortgage. Gardner, 52 

N. Y. S. 461. 

h. Parent and Child: Where the outcome of an action would relieve a parent from his duty of supporting a child in the future, the parent is an interested person. Re Russell, 110 N. Y. S. 706. (1) Case: D, putative father of P, for a valuable consideration, contracted with P's mother to settle a sum of money for P on the latter's tenth birthday. 

P now sues D to enforce the contract. Is the mother a competent witness for P to prove the contract?

Held, No. P's interest is derived from the mother. 

Rosseau, 180 N. Y. 116. (2) Case: P has a contract with A whereby she is to board A's illegitimate child, C. A dies. P sues his estate for a board bill. M, mother of C, was allowed to testify for P to prove the contract. Held, no error. M, while interested in the question is not interested in the event. Connelly, 117 N. Y. 91. i. Stockholders: Of a corporation, which is a party to an action, are interested persons (Merchants 

Nat. Bank, 249 N. Y. S. 6) unless the corporation is insolvent, and the stockholder cannot benefit

from the action. Talbot, 188 N. Y. 421. An officer or director who is not a stockholder is not within the purview of the statute (Griggs, 167 

N. Y. S. 355) and the same is true of an officer of a municipal or religious corporation. Matter of Kladreve, 234 N. Y. S. 551; Wash. Heights 

Church, 246 N. Y. S. 450 (pastor). Officers and stockholders of banking corporations are expressly excepted. C.P.A. 347. j. Vendor and Vendee: A vendor of personal property is not a person, from, through or under whom a vendee derives his interest or title, by assignment or otherwise, within the meaning of the statute, since the vendor no longer has any interest in the property, nor any moral, legal or direct interest in maintaining the validity of the sale. Abbot, 204 N. Y. 223. See also Bishop, 201 

N. Y. S. 256. k. Release of Interest: If an otherwise incompetent witness parts with his interest, he becomes competent to testify (Re Kindberg, 207 N. Y. 220) as in case of release of a legacy (Re Kindberg, supra), or a transfer by a former stockholder of his stock (Kulman, 181 N. Y. S. 171), or where a claim has been paid. Re Hanrette, 252 N. Y. S. 

424. But where A, an interested person, releases his entire interest to B, his co-plaintiff, A is nevertheless precluded from testifying, since in such case, A continues to be "a person from, through or under whom" B derived his title. 

O'Brien, 140 N. Y. 281. This case should be distinguished from the case of a general release by a legatee, where the legatee, is not disqualified after such release, because the residuary legatee, in no sense derives his title from, through or under him. Re Kindberg, supra. 

3. "Own Behalf or Interest": Testimony of an interested person against his own interest is admissible, as where a legatee testifies in behalf of contestants. 

Re Meltzer's Will, 279 N. Y. S. 564, aff'd 270 N. Y.

522. To admit his testimony, his interest need not be prejudiced; it is sufficient that it be not benefited. 

Harrington, 231 N. Y. 646; In re Lambert, 283 N. Y.

S. 64 (where the administrator sought an examination before trial of the adverse party plaintiff, who urged she was incompetent to testify under C.P.A. 

347, Held, the objection is untenable; the administrator may choose to call her as a witness and waive the privilege of silence). 

4. Persons Protected by Statute: C.P.A. 347 expressly indicates who may claim protection. a. "Executor" or "Administrator": An executor or administrator, when a party to an action or proceeding, is protected by the statute. (This is true even of death actions brought by him on behalf of next of kin under D. E. L. 130. Minns, 193 

N. Y. S. 714.) But where the action is for or against him individually, the statute does not apply. Atkinson, 182 N. Y. S. 780. b. "Survivor": The term includes heirs and next of kin (Roberts, 90 N. Y. S. 526) and surviving partners (Levy, 222 N. Y. 14), but not a surviving co-maker of a note (Sprague, 28 Hun 49) or a surviving tenant in common. Church, 3 Hun 254. c. "Successors": C.P.A. 347 expressly protects "a person deriving his title or interest from, through or under a deceased person or lunatic, by assignment or otherwise." This embraces grantees (Withaus, 105 N. Y. 332); assignees (Levy, supra); legatees (Re Smith, 95 N. Y. 516); heirs at law and next of kin (Hutton, 175 N. Y. 375); and cestui que trustent. Equitable Trust Co., 193

N. Y. S. 152. But the beneficiary of a life insurance policy, designated by the insured, does not claim "from, through or under" the insured, since 

C.P.A. 347 does not "contemplate a case where a party claims property from a third person which never belonged to the deceased and which in fact did not come into existence until his death."

Ward, 225 N. Y. 314. A purchaser of the equity of redemption derives his title from the mortgagor (Beattie, 198 N. Y. S. 71, aff'd 236 N. Y. 

574) but a creditor does not derive his interest from his debtor. Crane, 35 N. Y. S. 970, aff'd 153

N. Y. 656. A person who claims to derive his interest or title from an adjudicated lunatic, and thus come under the protection of C.P.A. 347, must first show that his assignor was duly adjudged incompetent under the provisions of the 

Civil Practice Act. Clark, 171 N. Y. S. 205. 

(1) Case: P, and M (D's husband) were business associates. M told D that the business needed $1000., and that if D would execute an undated note in blank amount, payee unnamed, P had represented that he could raise a $1000. loan, with D's note as collateral, from a third party. D executed the note relying on M's assurance that the note would not be filled in for an amount exceeding $1000. Nevertheless, without D's knowledge, M filled in the amount of $17,500. inserted P's name as payee, and delivered the note to P. Subsequently M died. P now sues 

D on the note. On the trial D tried to prove the above by her conversations with M. Her testimony was excluded under C.P.A. 347 on the ground that

P derived his title from, through or under M, the deceased, and that therefore, D, an interested party, was incompetent as a witness. Judgment for P and 

D appeals. Held, error. D was a competent witness to prove the transaction with M because P did not derive his title or interest in the note through M. 

P was merely an agent possessing no property in the subject matter. The note never belonged to him; hence, C.P.A. 347 does not apply. Jones, 277 N. Y. 437. 

5. "Personal Transaction or Communication": Form erly, a witness was disqualified to testify only as to personal transactions or conversations with a deceased. Holcomb, 95 N. Y. 316. But the rule has been gradually extended to include conversations or transactions of deceased which were personally overheard or witnessed by the witness. Griswold, 205 

N. Y. 384. a. Illustrations: Thus, an interested witness is incompetent

to testify that he saw the deceased write, where the genuineness of a writing is in dispute. Weber, 112 N. Y. S. 

20. Such a witness is also incompetent to testify as to the nature or value of personal services rendered to a decedent directly (Re Kahn's Estate, 251 N. Y. S. 23) or to testify that delivery of goods were made to the decedent; or that certain payments were made. Matter of 

Clodgo, 227 N. Y. S. 690. Similarly, as against a deceased payee, the maker of a note, when sued thereon, may not testify as to consideration, or that the note was for accommodation. Cody, 90 N. Y. S. 873. The interested witness cannot testify that he and decedent were intoxicated (Trombly, 256 N. Y. S. 225), nor can he testify as to a conversation between A and B, in the presence of the decedent, to show the latter's consent by silence. Lally, 247 N. Y. 58. But proof of a personal transaction with an agent of deceased is competent, even though the agent is also dead. Burke, 166 N. Y. S. 199. Genealogical facts, such as age or relationship, are not "personal transactions or communications" within the statute. Greco, 154 N. Y. S. 306. 

6. Waiver of Protection: 

a. By Testifying: A personal representative, or sur vivor, by testifying in his own behalf (or calling a witness on the stand to so testify) as to the personal transaction or conversation between the adverse party and the decedent, waives the protection of the statute. C.P.A. 347; Re McArdle's 

Estate, 250 N. Y. S. 276. But where such testimony is brought out on cross-examination, there is no waiver. Corning, 100 N. Y. 547. Where former sworn testimony of a lunatic or deceased person is read in evidence by the personal representative, a waiver also results (C.P.A. 347; Lyon, 141 N. Y. 225), but if the adverse party introduces it, it does not "open the door," because it is competent evidence only as an admission against interest. Lyon, supra. 

b. By Protected Party Examining Interested Person: 

Merely calling the adverse interested party or witness and examining him on direct, or crossexamination (Re Van Muffling's Estate, 277 N. Y. 

S. 584) as to a personal transaction with the de-

ceased does not ordinarily result in a general waiver permitting the adverse party to introduce testimony of any and all communications or transactions with decedent. Rogers, 153 N. Y. 

343; Matter of Volkenburgh, 233 N. Y. S. 457. If the protected party interrogates the witness as to a particular part of a personal transaction, the witness may state the whole transaction or conversation "and thereby explain or qualify the testimony brought out by the other party." Leask, 205 N. Y. 191. But the witness cannot go on to testify as to other transactions. Merchants Nat. 

Bank, 249 N. Y. S. 6. 

c. By Failure to Object: Evidence incompetent under C.P.A. 347 will be considered, nevertheless, if not timely objected to before admission. Matter of Smith, 242 N. Y. S. 464. But if an administrator is pressing his own claim against the estate, and no objection is made to incompetent testimony, there is no waiver, and the evidence is improperly received. Re Kahn's Estate, 251 N. Y. S. 23.

The objection should be against the competency of the witness. Hoag, 174 N. Y. 36. A general objection is insufficient (Hull, 97 N. Y. S. 570); it must be in such form as to apprise the court that it is made under section 347. Richie, 143 

N. Y. S. 19. See Russell, 93 N. Y. S. 950.

CHAPTER IX . 

I. Examination of Witnesses: 

A. Administrative Matters: 

1. Generally: Certain administrative matters in con nection with the examination of witnesses are within the discretion of the court. Such discretion will not be reviewed by an appellate court unless flagrantly abused, as, for example, where the trial judge extensively cross-examines a defendant before the jury at great length. David, 188 N. Y. S. 72. 

2. Exclusion of Witnesses: When a witness takes the stand, the judge may, either on request, or on his own motion, exclude from the courtroom all other witnesses, except parties, in order to prevent them from being coached by listening to the testimony of the witness. Peo. v. Green, 1 Denio 614. In New York, by statute, this procedure is made specially applicable to examinations before committing magistrates. C. 

Cr. Proc. 202-203. If a witness who has been ordered to do so, refuses to leave the court-room, he may be punished for contempt, but his testimony cannot be refused by the court. Friedman, 14 N. Y. S. 142. 

3. Exclusion of Jurors: In civil actions, jurors may be excluded during the argument of a motion for a nonsuit, dismissal of the complaint or direction of a verdict or the argument of a question concerning admission of evidence. R. C. P. 164. 

4. Use of Interpreters: If the court deems it necessary and advisable, an interpreter may be employed where a witness speaks only a foreign language (Peo. v. 

Constantino, 153 N. Y. 24) or a deaf and dumb one.

Cowley, 83 N. Y. 464. If there is no official interpreter, a qualified person must be sworn to interpret well and truly. Peo. v. Fisher, 169 N. Y. S. 

729. Peo. v. Chin Sing, 242 N. Y. 419. 

5. Order of Proof: The order in which proof shall be received in both civil and criminal cases is in the 147

discretion of the court. Barson, 79 N. Y. S. 194;

C. Cr. Proc. 388. Even after the jurors have retired, they may be recalled and further evidence taken. 

Peo. v. Ferrone, 204 N. Y. 551. The discretion should not be abused. Meyer, 54 N. Y. 392. 

B. Leading Questions: If counsel so frames a question as to suggest the answer to a witness, it may be objected to as "leading." Peo. v. Mather, 4 Wend. 229 ("State whether or not you addressed defendant as one of those concerned in the transaction," Held, improper as leading.) The same is true when the question assumes the truth of facts in controversy. But if the leading question is designed merely to expedite the witness' testimony, as to introductory matter, it is not objectionable. 

Peo. v. Mather, supra. 

1. Exceptions to Rule: In the case of a hostile or biased witness (Becker, 104 N. Y. 394), or an illiterate or weak-minded witness (Nicholetti, 151 N. Y. S. 520), leading questions are permissible. The same is true on cross-examination where it is attempted to contradict the witness' direct testimony, but not where it is attempted to bring out new matter. Peo. v. 

Genet, 19 Hun 91.

C. Memoranda to Refresh Memory: 

1. Writing not in Evidence: To refresh his memory, a witness may use any memorandum, written or printed, whether made by himself or not, but he must then testify to facts of his own knowledge, the memorandum itself not being evidence. Howard, 77 

N. Y. 592. “A witness cannot be allowed to refresh his recollection with reference to a matter about which he never had any knowledge." Kaplan, 223 

Mass. 152; Kirschner, 90 N. Y. S. 351. The cases are vague as to the time when the memorandum must have been made. It has been held that the record of former testimony of a witness is competent to refresh his memory although not made at or near the time of the events recorded. Hart, 91 

N. Y. S. 922. Huff, 6 N. Y. 337 (newspaper report held proper to refresh recollection). Cf. Bigelow,

91 N. Y. 145. It would seem that since the memorandum is not itself evidence, but merely an aid to permit the witness to testify from a refreshed recollection, if it serves the latter purpose, it should be immaterial that it was not made contemporaneously with the event recorded. a. Although the memorandum reviving the present recollection cannot be put in evidence, nevertheless, on cross-examination, the adversary has the right to inspect it, and if insufficient on the face thereof (because of brevity or lapse of time) to adequately refresh the witness' recollection, the adversary may offer it in evidence as bearing upon the witness' credibility. Tibbets, 66 Barb. 

201; Miller, 183 N. Y. S. 97.

D. Memorandum Used as Auxiliary Evidence: Where a witness has so far forgotten the facts that he cannot recall them, and he testifies that he once knew them, and made a memorandum at or near the time when they occurred, which he intended to make correctly, which he believes to be correct, such memorandum may be received as evidence of the facts therein contained, although the witness has no present recollection of them. 

Howard, 77 N. Y. 592; McCarthy, 183 N. Y. 190. Later cases have held that if the witness did not personally make the memorandum, it is admissible, nevertheless, if he testifies that he saw it soon after it was made and then recognized it as containing a true statement of the facts within his own knowledge. Clark, 164

N. Y. 498. Such a memorandum is admitted not as primary evidence (i.e., as a substitute for the witness' oral testimony) but only as auxiliary evidence. 

1. Time of Making Record: In order for the memo randum to be admissible in evidence, it need not appear that it was made exactly at the time of the occurrence, but it should appear from all the facts, that it was made while the occurrence was still fresh in the witness' memory. Halsey, 15 N. Y. 485; 

Howard, supra. 

2. Knowledge of Witness: Before the memorandum is

properly admissible, the witness must testify that although he has no present recollection of the facts, he remembers that he made, or saw the memorandum about the time of the occurrence and recognized it then as correct, and that at that time he had personal knowledge of the facts stated therein. If the witness does not remember having seen the memorandum at any time previous, but from his handwriting or business habits, can presently swear that the memorandum is genuine, and that the statements therein contained are correct, the memorandum is admissible. Peck, 94 N. Y. 569; Hodas, 196 N. Y. 

S. 801; Vicksburg, 119 U. S. 99; Clark, supra. a. Note: "If the witness can say, 'I distinctly remember that when I made or saw this memorandum, about the time of the events, I was then conscious of its correctness', his verification is satisfactory. But if he relies, not on a present recollection of his past state of mind, but on other indications, such as a habit, a course of business, a check mark on the margin, or merely the genuineness of his handwriting, then the certainty is of a lower quality, though still the witness' certainty rests on his usual habit or course of business in making memoranda or records, it is sufficient." Wigmore on Ev. p. 747. 

3. Production of Original Memorandum: The original memorandum must be produced. Peck, supra. 

4. New York Rule: "It is, however, an indispensable preliminary to the introduction of such a memorandum in evidence, that it should appear that the witness is unable with the aid of the memorandum to speak from memory as to the facts" (i.e., from a "refreshed recollection"). Russell, 17 N. Y. 134. 

To same effect, see Mathison, 203 N. Y. 79. But see 

Peo. v. Weinberger, 239 N. Y. 307. a. Case: Ds are being prosecuted for presenting an alleged obscene play. The People called witnesses who saw the play and who were permitted to describe from memory the actions and gestures of the actors on the stage and the substance of the words they heard, and, so far as they remembered, the exact words. Ds offered in evidence a transcript of the "actors' script" containing the dialogue and "business" of the entire play. This was excluded on the objection that the recollection of the witnesses as to the words and action of the play should first have been

exhausted before a transcript of the actors' script could be admitted. Was the script properly admissible? Held, yes. "The rule that the present recollection of a witness must be exhausted before a record of his past recollection may be admitted in evidence, though applied in New York and the Federal courts, has not been universally accepted or approved. There are times when the record of a past recollection, if it exists, is more trustworthy and desirable than a present recollection of greater or less vividness, and that is clearly the case here. * * * A rule of evidence should not be permitted to become a mere fetish. The evidence offered would unquestionably be competent if a few preliminary questions had been asked of the witness as to his present recollection; the asking of these questions would have been useless and could have elicited no answers that would have helped the jury. The transcript represents a record of the witness' past memory of the play more reliable, as evidence, than testimony based upon his present memory could possibly be, and it should, therefore, have been received." Peo. v. Weinberger, supra. 

E. Cross-Examination: 

1. Generally: The right of cross-examination is an inalienable one. While the opportunity need not be availed of, if it is denied, reversible error is committed. Thus, where the opportunity is denied to a party, either through the death or illness of the witness after direct examination, or through any other cause, that party is entitled to have a juror withdrawn, and a mistrial declared, or else, to have the testimony of the incapacitated witness, given on direct examination, stricken out. Peo. v. Cole, 43 

N. Y. 508. 

2. Scope: Generally speaking, a witness may be crossexamined only as to matters to which he testified on direct examination, or as to matters which would tend to impeach his credibility. Houghton, 1 Wall. (U. S.) 702. If the cross-examiner elicits new matter, he makes the witness his own. Kay, 163 N. Y. 

447. Notice that the court, in its discretion, may permit a variance in the order of proof so as to allow a party to make an adverse witness his own on crossexamination. Peo. v. Pender, 210 N. Y. 191. 

3. Collateral Matters: As a necessary precaution against

entangling the court and jury in collateral issues without end, a general rule is observed that the cross-examiner is bound by the answer of the witness as to collateral matters and may not thereafter call other witnesses to refute or disprove such answer; unless, within the court's discretion, such crossexamination as to collateral matters is necessary in the furtherance of justice. Mowbray, 71 N. Y. S. 

365; Hong, 174 N. Y. 36. a. Past Moral Conduct: If the cross-examiner attempts to impeach the credibility of the witness by interrogating him as to his past or present moral conduct, that is generally regarded as a collateral matter and the witness' answer is binding on the cross-examiner. Potter, 197 N. Y. 

288; Peo. v. Webster, 137 N. Y. 73; Langley, 99 N. Y. 61. In any event, the scope and extent of such collateral cross-examination is within the court's discretion. Peo. v. Slover, 232 N. Y. 264. 

b. Previous Criminal Convictions: However, where the cross-examiner, in impeaching the credibility of the witness, asks him, if he was ever convicted of a crime, and the witness answers in the negative, the cross-examiner may nevertheless prove the contrary by the judgment or record of conviction. Penal Law 2444; C.P.A. 350.

F. Privilege of Witness Against Self-Incrimination: 

1. Defendant: A defendant in a criminal case need not take the stand in his own behalf (N. Y. Const. Art. 1, sec. 6) and his failure, or refusal to do so, creates no inference of guilt. C. Cr. Proc., sec. 393. Nor may the prosecution comment upon the fact in summing up. Peo. v. Minkowitz, 220 N. Y. 399; Peo. v. 

Michor, 235 N. Y. S. 387. However, if the defendant takes the stand, he waives his privilege and becomes an ordinary witness who may be cross-examined and have his credibility attacked like any other witness. 

Peo. v. Hinksman, 192 N. Y. 421. But his general character may not be attacked unless he first opens the door by putting in evidence of good character.

Peo. v. Hinksman, supra. Cf. Peo. v. Johnston, 238

N. Y. 332 (where the court said, "It is true that his waiver probably extends only to matters that are relevant to the charge on trial; that is, not to collateral matters, that is, facts affecting merely credibility, but unless the privilege is claimed, a witness who may be the defendant at the same time, may, like any other witness, be compelled to testify to specific acts for the purpose of affecting his credibility as a witness.") 

2. Ordinary Witness: In any civil or criminal proceeding, a witness may decline to answer a question which would tend to expose him to criminal punishment (fine, penalty, imprisonment, forfeiture or confiscation of land). Disbarment is not a penalty or forfeiture. Matter of Rouss, 221 N. Y. 81. But the fact that his answer may cause him pecuniary loss or render him liable in a civil action, does not warrant the witness in claiming the privilege. C.P.A. 355. 

Nor does the fact that the answer may expose him to public disgrace or ridicule, justify a refusal to answer, unless the question is on a collateral matter. 

Meyer, 159 N. Y. S. 405. The privilege is personal and may be claimed only by the witness. Peo. v. Bodino, 1 Denio 281. Counsel for a witness who is not a party (the rule is contra where the witness is a party) may not claim the constitutional privilege for the witness. Peo. v. Brown, 72 N. Y. 571. Ordinarily, the witness must decide for himself whether or not to answer the question, but the court, in its discretion, may inform the witness, in a proper case, that he may claim his privilege. Peo. v. Priori, 164 N. Y. 459. 

3. Where Privilege Terminated: Manifestly, if the witness cannot be subjected to criminal punishment, as a result of his testimony, he cannot assert his privilege. Thus, where the witness was previously tried and convicted or acquitted, his incriminating replies would not be privileged, since by virtue of the "double jeopardy" clause of the Constitution, he

could not be punished therefor. A similar situation results where prosecution for the offense, in respect to which the witness would be protected from disclosure, has been barred by a statute of limitations, (Meyer, 159 N. Y. S. 405) or where there is a statute granting immunity to witnesses from criminal prosecution for any matter to which their testimony may relate. Peo. v. Sharp, 107 N. Y. 427. Where the protection against prosecution is granted by statute, in order that the privilege against self-incrimination be terminated, it is essential that the statute be as broad as the privilege. Arndstein, 254 U. S. 71. For example, in New York, Penal Law 996, as amended in 1904, provides that a witness in a gambling prosecution "shall not be prosecuted or subjected to any penalty or forfeiture for or on account of any transaction, matter or thing concerning which he may so testify or produce evidence, documentary or otherwise, and no testimony so given or produced shall be received against him upon any criminal investigation. or proceeding." This section has been held to grant the witness an immunity as broad as his constitutional privilege. Peo. ex rel Lewisohn, 89 N. Y. S. 

364, aff'd 179 N. Y. 594; Peo. v. Wilmot, 217 N. Y. S. 

477. (Before the amendment in 1904, the section. extended immunity only to the extent that the evidence of a witness might not be used against him on a criminal prosecution. The section was held not coextensive with the constitutional privilege of the witness, since it did not prevent the use of evidence against him obtained through his testimony. Peo. ex rel Lewisohn, 176 N. Y. 253). Compare Penal Law, sec. 381 (witness in bribery prosecution); Penal

Law, sec. 584 (witness in prosecution for conspiracy to bribe). Doyle, 257 N. Y. 244. See also C.P.A. 789. 

4. Waiving the Privilege: The privilege against selfincrimination is waived, in any case, by the person entitled thereto offering himself as a witness and testifying upon the privileged matter, and where so waived, the witness becomes subject to cross-exam-

ination like any other witness. But the waiver is limited to the one proceeding in which it is thus made. Peo. v. Cassidy, 213 N. Y. 388. a. Case: D is being prosecuted for a violation of a statute making it a crime for an operator of a motor vehicle to leave a scene of an accident, caused by his culpability, without stopping and giving his name, residence and operator's license number to the injured party or to a police officer. D demurred to the indictment on the ground that the statute is unconstitutional since, in effect, it compels him to be a witness against himself in a criminal case. Is the statute constitutional? Held, yes. "The primary object of the statute, in my judgment, is not to convict any person of crime, but to subject him to civil liability. I appreciate that when examined as a witness in a civil suit, the defendant might claim his privilege. 

I also appreciate that the right to justify a disobedience of this statute by proof that the circumstances render him liable to criminal prosecution would be of little advantage to the defendant. He would acquit himself of one crime only by convicting himself of another. Nevertheless when we bear in mind, not only the great danger occasioned by the use of motor vehicles, but also the fact that the great speed at which they can be run enables the person causing injury to readily escape undetected, leaving parties injured in person or property unable to tell from whom they shall seek redress, I think it involves no violation of public policy, or of the principles of personal liberty, to enact that as a condition of operating such a machine, the operator must waive his constitutional privilege and tell who he is to the party who has been injured or to the police authorities, if, indeed, requiring him to give such information is an impairment of his constitutional privilege, which we do not decide." Peo. v. 

Rosenheimer, 209 N. Y. 115. To same effect see State v. 

Sterin, 78 N. H. 220.

II. Impeachment of Witnesses: 

A. Adverse Party's Witnesses: 

1. Bad Reputation for Veracity: After a witness has testified, the adverse party may call impeaching witnesses who reside, move, circulate or do business within the "natural radius of repute" to testify to the general bad reputation of the first witness for truth and veracity. Carlson, 147 N. Y. 652. The impeaching witness must be able to state that he

knows the reputation for veracity of the first witness, in the community in which the latter resides, and that he knows it to be bad, but he may not testify to specific instances of want of veracity which he personally observed, or to specific instances of immoral conduct or as to the general bad reputation of the first witness for traits other than veracity. 

Bakeman, 18 Wend. 146. Jackson, 13 Johns. 504; 

Kenyon v. Peo., 26 N. Y. 203. Where the credibility of a witness has been impeached by evidence of his general bad reputation for veracity, his credibility may be rehabilitated by evidence of good reputation. 

Stape v. Peo., 85 N. Y. 390. Peo. v. Van Gaasbeck, 189 N. Y. 408. 

2. By Cross-Examination: More latitude is allowed where the opponent's witness is being impeached by cross-examination instead of by impeaching witnesses. In such case, he may be asked about any specific instances of immoral or criminal conduct on his part which would tend to prove him unworthy of belief (Shepard, 36 N. Y. 17), but he may not be interrogated as to conduct which would not necessarily imply moral turpitude. Peo. v. Montlake, 172 

N. Y. S. 102. For instance, he may be asked, whether he uses drugs, (Peo. v. Webster, 68 Hun 11, aff'd 

139 N. Y. 73), whether he is engaged in a certain nefarious business (Peo. v. Giblin, 115 N. Y. 196); whether he is a disbarred attorney (Peo. v. Dorthy, 156 N. Y. 237); or whether he or she is addicted to promiscuous fornication, or to the company of disreputable persons, (Peo. v. Webster, 139 N. Y. 73); but not whether he has defaulted on a certain debt. 

Peo. v. Montlake, supra. a. Conviction of Crime: A witness in a civil, as well as a criminal, case (Cooke, 207 A. D. 592) may be impeached by asking him if he has ever been convicted of a crime or has served a prison sentence (Peo. v. Cascone, 185 N. Y. S. 317), but not whether he has ever been arrested or indicted, since that does not necessarily imply guilt. Peo.

v. Latshaw, 199 N. Y. S. 592. The conviction may be shown even though sentence thereon was suspended or defendant was pardoned. Webb, 179 N. Y. S. 957. (1) Case: D was indicted on a charge of attempting to bribe a witness. Upon the trial, he was asked whether he had ever been convicted of a crime and answered in the negative. The People, over D's objection and exception, were permitted to show, on cross-examination, that many years earlier, D had been convicted for abortion but the judgment was subsequently reversed on appeal. Was the judgment of conviction properly admitted to impeach D's credibility? 

Held, No. There was no conviction against D. "The word conviction means a legal and not an illegal conviction." Peo. v. Van Zile, 159 A. D. 61. 

b. Conviction Must Involve Moral Turpitude: A 

conviction for violation of a local ordinance, not involving moral turpitude, such as a traffic law violation, cannot be shown either to impeach the witness' credibility or to show bad character. 

Peo. v. Joyce, 233 N. Y. 61; Penal Law, 2, 2444; 

C.P.A. 355; De Stasio, 279 N. Y 501. 

c. How Conviction Proved: The witness' denial of conviction may be broken down either by his admission on cross-examination or by offering in evidence the judgment of conviction. Peo. v. 

Marendi, 213 N. Y. 600. The reception of any other or different proof of conviction is impliedly forbidden. Accordingly, it has been held error to permit evidence of the defendant's admission of the fact of conviction to another witness, Peo. v. 

Cardillo, 207 N. Y. 70; Peo. v. Stein, 96 Misc. 

507. If the witness on cross-examination admits a previous conviction, he may be rehabilitated by proof of present good reputation. Derrick, 217 

N. Y. 525. 

3. By Showing Bias or Interest: A witness may be impeached by showing him to be biased or prejudiced in favor of the party in whose behalf he is testifying. Peo. v. Grutz, 212 N. Y. 72; Meltzer, 91 N. Y. 365. This may be shown by the admissions

of the witness, brought out on cross-examination (Potter, 197 N. Y. 288) or by the testimony of others as to the acts or declarations of the witness tending to show hostility to one party and friendliness to the other. Potter, supra. a. Nature of Bias or Interest: Just why a witness. should be biased in favor of one party depends upon the peculiar circumstances of each case. In one case, the witness may be biased because he is an expert and is being paid for his testimony (Zimmer, 55 N. Y. S. 314); or he may be related to one party by blood (Kavanagh, 70 N. Y. 117); or in business (Anderson, 40 N. Y. S. 671) or in a criminal prosecution, he may have been promised immunity for turning State's evidence (Peo. v. Becker, 210 N. Y. 274) or for any other reason. 

Peo. v. Milks, 74 N. Y. S. 1042. 

b. Extent of Examination to Show Bias or Interest: 

The extent to which a party will be permitted to examine a witness for the purpose of proving him to be hostile or biased, is largely within the discretion of the court. Peo. v. Lustig, 206 N. Y. 

162. Also, it is the rule in New York, decisions elsewhere notwithstanding, that the hostility or bias of a witness may be established directly by the independent evidence of other witnesses, and no foundation need be laid therefor by first examining the witness himself. Peo. v. Brooks, 131 N. Y. 321. Such a foundation is necessary, however, where it is sought to impeach the testimony of a witness, other than a party, by showing that he made previous, contradictory statements. McCullock, 133 N. Y. 114. 

4. By Showing Previous, Contradictory Statements: 

For the purpose of impeaching an opponent's witness, a party may always show that on previous occasions the witness made contradictory statements, whether oral or in writing. Larkin, 205 N. Y. 267; 

Hanlon, 80 N. Y. S. 692. a. Laying Foundation: "But it is requisite that the

party offering the impeaching evidence should first call the attention of the witness to the circumstances under which the statements were made, that he may have an opportunity of correcting the evidence given on the trial or of explaining the apparent inconsistency between his evidence and his former statements, * * * and when his evidence is sought to be impeached by written statements, alleged to have been made by him, the writing should be first produced so that he may have an opportunity for inspection and examination." Gaffney v. Peo., 50 N. Y. 416; See also McCullock, supra. Peo. v. Weldon, 11 N. Y. 

569. Where, however, the witness is a party, his testimony may be impeached by proof of prior, contradictory statements, even though no foundation be laid therefor beforehand. Mindlin, 189 

N. Y. S. 265. (1) If the witness denies having made the alleged inconsistent statements, or if he denies recollection of them, that is sufficient to introduce the impeaching evidence. Stope v. Peo., 85 

N. Y. 390. (a) Case: P was injured while a passenger in D's bus. O, the operator of the bus was called as a witness for D. O testified as to his careful operation of the vehicle, and in the course of his cross-examination, he was asked whether after the accident he had any conversation with H, P's daughter. O responded that he didn't remember speaking to H. Thereupon, P called H in rebuttal, and H, over D's objection and exception, was permitted to testify that she said to 

O: "that is an awful thing to do, to drive like that" and that O replied: "I am sorry, lady. I admit it is my fault but there is nothing I can do about it now." On sur-rebuttal, O denied this. Judgment for P and D appeals claiming error in the admission of the above. Held, no error. While O's statement was not binding as an admission against the employer, D, because not made within the scope of O's authority, nevertheless, it was admissible for the limited purpose, upon the laying of a proper foundation,

of impeaching his credibility-by showing his prior inconsistent statement. It is always permissible to attack the credibility of a witness, and this includes an agent or employee, by showing that at some other time he has said things which are inconsistent with or contrary to the testimony which he has given on the trial. In the case of verbal statements, the attention of the witness should be called to what it is claimed he had previously said, specifying the time when, the place where, and the person with whom, the alleged conversation was had, and he should be asked if he had so stated. If he does not admit it, proof may then be given. Here no proper foundation was laid. O should have been asked not only whether he had a conversation with H at a certain time and place but even after he denied having had any conversation with her, he should have been asked specifically whether he said to her at that time and place that the accident was all his fault. But D's general objection to H's testimony was no good, since, had D specifically called the attention of the court to the fact that no proper foundation had been laid therefor, the defect could have been remedied. Moreover, the error could hardly be prejudicial to D since on sur-rebuttal O denied making the statement. Also, the court should have instructed the jury on D's request as to the effect of the impeaching evidence i.e. that it was only for the purpose of affecting O's credibility and was not affirmative proof of negligence binding on D. But D made no such request. Affirmed. Wolfe, 13 N. Y. S. (2) 741.

Note: The statement as to culpability is received not as expressing the opinion or conclusion of the employee (in which case it would be clearly inadmissible) but as a statement inconsistent with the entire line of testimony given by him on direct examination, to the effect that he had acted prudently. (2) Also, where impeaching evidence has been properly given showing prior inconsistent statements by the witness, the latter may not attempt to cover up by proving prior consistent statements. Peo. v. Rector, 19 Wend. (N. Y.) 569. It has been held, however, that where the witness' testimony is attacked

as a recent fabrication, the witness may prove prior consistent statements made at a time when he apparently had no incentive to misrepresent. Peo. v. Katz, 209 N. Y. 311.

B. Impeaching Own Witness: 

1. Generally: As a general proposition, a party may not impeach his own witness. Sometimes, as in criminal prosecutions, the rule works a genuine hardship on the prosecutor who has to make out a case from the mouths of recalcitrant and prejudiced witnesses, who are often friends of the accused; but the fundamental basis of the rule is nevertheless sound. 

It is premised on the theory that when a party presents a witness to the court, he holds him out as worthy of belief, and he should not thereafter be at liberty to prove the contrary. Peo. v. Sheehan, 49 

Barb. 219; Greenleaf on Ev. 16th Ed. Sec. 442. In 

Hanrahan, 238 N. Y. 194, the Court of Appeals, speaking of the rule said: "While it may be urged with some degree of force that a party does not by calling a witness upon one point, vouch for him on every point, without knowing what he may say when the adverse party calls him, it logically follows that one should not be permitted to say in one breath, 'the witness speaks truthfully for me and, by reason. of his bad character, is not to be believed when he testifies against me"." a. "Own Witness" Explained: Ordinarily if A calls

X as a witness first, in an action against B, and X gives material testimony on behalf of A, X becomes A's witness, although thereafter B calls him on another branch of the case and X testifies adversely to A. Hanrahan, supra. b. If however, A after calling X, and making a few preliminary inquiries, decides not to use him as a witness, and if thereafter X is called by B and gives material testimony in favor of B, X becomes B's witness, although first called by A. Fall 

Brook Coal Co., 158 N. Y. 150. See also Bennett, 270 N. Y. 456.

c. If X is a compulsory witness, (i.e., the law requires A to call him) as, for example, where A is the proponent of a will signed by X, as a subscribing witness, X is not A's "own witness" and A may impeach him as if he were an adverse witness. Matter of Cottrell, 95 N. Y. 329. 

2. Rule Stated: The New York rule is that a party may not impeach the credibility of his own witness, either by general evidence showing the witness to be unworthy of belief, or by proof of self-contradictory statements, or by showing the witness to be biased or hostile. Peo. v. Minsky, 227 N. Y. 94; Sanchez v. Peo., 22 N. Y. 147. By recent amendment, however, a prior contradictory statement may be shown, provided such statement "was made in any writing by him subscribed, or, was made under oath.” C.P.A. 343a. a. Case: P, a customer in D's retail grocery store was injured when a heavy box struck her foot, and sues D for negligence. D contended that the accident was due to the negligence of K, an independent, contractor truckman. K was made a co-defendant by P. K, called by D, testified that he had dropped a box in making a delivery to D's store on the occasion in question, but that he could not say whether such box struck P's foot. D's counsel then asked K if he, K, had not written a letter (showing same to K) to the commissioner of motor vehicles stating that the box had fallen out of his hands and struck one of the customer's feet. This was excluded by the trial court on the ground that counsel was impeaching his own witness. 

Judgment for P and D appeals. Held, error. The letter should have been received. Under C. P. A. 343a, it is permissible to impeach one's own witness by a statement inconsistent with his testimony, provided the statement was made in writing and subscribed by the witness. Reversed for new trial. Bachand, 279 N. Y. 177. See also 

Lewis, 15 N. Y. S. (2d) 579. 

3. Rule Applied: Under the rule a party may not ask his own witness whether he has ever been convicted of a crime (Peo. v. Countryman, 195 N. Y. S. 728) or whether he has been guilty of immoral conduct. 

Peo. v. Minsky, supra. Neither may a party question the credibility of his own witness by proving by other witnesses that he made self-contradictory statements on previous occasions, except in a case

covered by the new amendments, supra. Peo. v. 

De Martini, 213 N. Y. 203. This is the rule even though the witness has proven obviously hostile. 

Becker, 104 N. Y. 394; Mason Co., 212 N. Y. S. 353; 

Bernstein, 131 N. Y. S. 129, aff'd 205 N. Y. 603. On the same principle, if one's own witness proves adverse, impeaching evidence is nevertheless inadmissible to show the witness to be biased or interested. In re Mellins Estate, 9 N. Y. S. 929; Fulton 

Bank, 2 Wend. 483. 

4. Apparent and Real Exceptions to Rule: a. If A calls X and makes him his witness and X testifies adversely, as to certain material facts, A may thereafter call other witnesses to prove the facts testified to by X to be the contrary. 

This is not impeaching X's credibility. Coulter, 56 N. Y. 585; Quick, 205 N. Y. 330. b. If X's testimony is fundamentally improbable, or if it is contradicted by other evidence in the record, A, who called him as a witness is not bound by it, although he does not call other witnesses to contradict it. In such case, the jury may believe only that part of X's testimony which sounds true and reject the rest. Henry, 175 

N. Y. S. 738; Carlisle, 215 N. Y. 400. c. Using the same parties as in the illustrations. above, if X's testimony should prove adverse, although A had no reason to expect X to be hostile, A may interrogate X as to previous contradictory statements made by X, not for the purpose of impeaching him, but to refresh his memory, and correct his adverse testimony. Bullard, 53 N. Y. 230; Peo. v. Sherman, 133 N. Y. 

349; Miller, 183 N. Y. S. 623. d. Moreover, if X should happen to be the adverse party himself, A may impeach X, by showing previous inconsistent statements, in the nature of admissions, although he cannot impeach X's general reputation for veracity. Koester, 194 

N. Y. 92; Cross, 108 N. Y. 628.

CHAPTER X. 

. 

I. Public Records: 

A. Definition: All writings, or records, made by public officers, setting forth facts which such officers are required to record in the performance of their duties, are public or official records. Greenleaf on Ev. (16th Ed.) sec. 470.

B. Generally: Public records and documents, if competent otherwise, are admissible as proof of the facts appearing therein, although unsupported by the evidence of the official who made them. Despite the absence of an opportunity to cross-examine, trustworthiness is established by the fact that the entries made in public records are made by officials who are sworn to faithfully discharge their official duties, and, further, it would be a great hardship to require the official to personally attend in court, every time it is sought to prove an entry made in the records of his office. Gaines, 12 How. (U.S.) 472. 

Moreover, since there is danger of loss or destruction, if the original public record is taken from the custody of the official who is entrusted with its care, not to mention the inconvenience of bringing it to court, a public record is usually proved by means of a certified, exemplified or sworn copy. Bailey, 91 N. Y. S. 852; 

C. P. A. 382. 

1. Certified Copy: Is a true copy together with a certificate, under the hand and official seal of the official authorized to certify, or who has legal custody of the record, stating that he compared the copy with the original on file and found it to be a correct transcript of the whole of such original. C. P. A. 329-330. 

2. Exemplified Copy: Is substantially the same as a certified copy, except that the former is made in the name of "the People of the State of New York" and is witnessed by a judge and signed by the clerk of the court wherein the original record is on file. 164

3. Sworn Copy: Is one to which a witness has attached an affidavit that he has compared it, word for word, with the original record and found them to be identical. Kellogg, 6 Barb. 116.

C. Judicial Records: 

1. Domestic Records: In New York, if the judicial record sought to be proved is that of a court of record within the state, a certified copy is sufficient. C. P. A. 

382. If the record sought to be proved is in the same action, the court will take judicial notice of it; or, if in another action in the same court, the court may take judicial notice of it. Ritchey Corp., 191 N. Y. S. 

870; Re Ordway, 196 N. Y. 95. a. Justice of the Peace: The minutes kept by a justice of the peace in a case before him are regarded as a judicial record and are admissible. Pollock, 4 E. D. Smith, 473.

When offered as proof in another court, however, it must be authenticated by the oath of the justice, if he is alive; or by proof of his handwriting, if he is dead. Baldwin, 13 

Johns, 430; C. P. A. 387. 

2. Foreign Records: 

a.
