Of Sister State: "The records and judicial pro ceedings of the courts of any State or Territory or of any country subject to the jurisdiction of the United States shall be proved or admitted in. any other Court, within the United States, by the attestation of the clerk, and the seal of the court annexed, if there be a seal, together with a certificate of the judge, chief justice, or presiding magistrate, that the said attestation is in due form." U. S. Rev. Stat. sec. 905. Where the records of judicial proceedings are so authenticated they must be given full faith and credit in every court in the United States. U. S. Const. 

Art. 4 sec. 1. A judgment of the court of another state, not authenticated according to this statutory provision, may not be proved in New York. 

Bittleston Law Agency, 215 N. Y. S. 796. Under

Rev. St. Sec. 905, the copy of the foreign record must be exemplified and must be certified by a judge of the court to the effect that the clerk's

signature and the seal of the court are genuine. and that the clerk's attestation is in due form; the clerk then certifies to the effect that the signature of the judge is genuine. (1) There must be strict compliance with the statute. 

The attestation must be made by the clerk of the court and if made by a deputy clerk, it is invalid, even though the judge certifies that the certification is made by the proper officer. Morris, 24 N. Y. 394.

Moverover, the certificate of the judge must be that of the presiding judge, where the court consists of more than one judge. Rich, 114 N. Y. S. 672. 

b. Of Foreign Countries: A record of a foreign court of a foreign country is usually proved by a certified copy of the record, certified by the clerk of the foreign court, whose certification in turn is authenticated by the certificate of the chief judge or presiding magistrate, which latter signature, is again authenticated by the certificate of the secretary of state, or other officer having charge of the great seal of the government, which last certificate must also specify the general nature of the court's jurisdiction. The foreign record may also be proved by a copy authenticated in the manner prescribed by the laws of the foreign country and bearing a certification that the authentication is proper under such laws. C. P. A. 

395. A sworn copy of the record is also admissible upon proof of certain requirements mentioned in C. P. A. 396. c. Records of Federal Courts: "A copy of the record, or any other proceeding, of a court of the United States, is evidence when certified by the clerk or officer in whose custody it is required by law to be." C. P. A. 399. (1) Thus, in a summary proceeding brought to remove the tenant for illegal use of premises, a certified copy of a judgment of the U. S. Dist. Court, decreeing that the premises were used for sale of liquor, is admissible against the tenant. B'way Cent. Soc., 218

A. D. 594.

D. Non-Judicial Records: 

1. Legislative Acts: 

a. Domestic, Public and Private Statutes: Although the state courts will take judicial notice of a domestic, public statute, any legislative act of this state may be read in evidence from a newspaper, designated as prescribed by law to publish the same, until six months after the close of the session at which it was passed; or, at any time, from the official volumes or from a copy certified by the secretary of state. C. P. A. 380. 

(1) Local Ordinances: May be proved by a cer tified copy or read in evidence from the official printed volume. C. P. A. 388. Sec. 32 of the City Home Rule Law, as amended in 1939, requires that judicial notice be taken of all local laws of a city. b. Foreign Statutes: Since the statutory law of a sister state, or foreign nation, is not judicially known or presumed to be the same as that of New York, such law must be pleaded and proved. 

C. P. A. 391 provides that a printed copy of a statute, or other written law of another state, or nation, contained in a book or publication purporting, or proved, to have been published by the authority thereof, is presumptive evidence of such statute or law. Russian Re-Ins. Co., 207 N. Y. S. 

504 (where it was held foreign statute may not be proved by parol). 

(1) Foreign Common Law: The unwritten or common law of a foreign state, territory or country may be proved as a fact by oral evidence or by the official case reports. The court, or referee, must determine such law and include it in the findings of fact, or charge to the jury. C. P. A. 391. See Italiano, 284 N. Y. S. 177 (where question of foreign law was held one of fact and not of law, to be determined by trial judge). 

2. Official Registers: 

a. Generally: In order that a record kept in an official register, be competent evidence of its

contents, it must appear that it was required by law to be so kept, and that it was not kept for a particular purpose merely. C. P. A. 367; Richards, 165 N. Y. S. 780. Thus, in an annulment action on the ground of non-age, a school record of plaintiff's age, required by law to be kept of all children attending New York City public schools, was held admissible as sound evidence of the plaintiff's age. Price, 185 N. Y. S. 

570. But a police blotter report of an accident, in which one of the parties was reported as having been negligent, is inadmissible in an action to recover damages brought by the other party. 

Kerr, 57 N. Y. S. 794; Johnson, 253 N. Y. 124.

Again, a factory inspector's report showing a dangerous condition to exist in a certain factory, is inadmissible in a private, negligence action brought by a person injured in such factory, since the law does not require the keeping of such records. Harholm, 144 N. Y. S. 836. 

b. Birth Certificates: In New York, a certified copy of a birth certificate is evidence of the fact of birth of the child named therein, where the record of such birth is required by law to be kept. 

Pirrung, 93 N. Y. S. 575. But it is not evidence of any other fact contained therein. Hammerstein, 134 N. Y. S. 473 (where in a divorce action, birth certificate of defendant's child, which named defendant and co-respondent as the child's parents, was held inadmissible). See also Bilkovic, 141 

N. Y. S. 279. c. Death Certificates: Are not regarded in New

York as public records, so as to make them evidence of the facts recorded in private actions between parties, although in most cities, they are required by law to be kept by the boards of health as vital statistics. The rule may be stated to be that a certified copy of a death certificate is evidence of the fact of death (Re Hall, 154 N. Y. S. 

317) but not of the causes of death, since such

latter evidence would be hearsay and would abridge the privilege of a patient arising from the relationship with his physician. Beglin, 173 N. Y. 

374; Painton, 135 N. Y. S. 418. (1) But where the beneficiary under a life insurance policy attaches to his proof of loss, a death certificate showing the insured's death, the cause of death stated therein, is competent against the beneficiary as an admission in a subsequent action on the policy. Klein, 221 N. Y. 449; Rudolph, 251 N. Y. 208. 

d. Marriage Certificates: A certified copy of a mar riage certificate is presumptive evidence of the marriage. C. P. A. 372. 

e. Census Returns: C. P. A. sec. 401 provides that "a certificate of the director, or other officer in charge, of the census of the United States, attested by the secretary of the interior", giving the results of said census, is prima facie evidence of such facts. But the census return can not be used to show individual facts, such as the age of persons listed therein. Maher, 96 N. Y. S. 496. 

f. Notarial Certificate of Protest: C. P. A. 368, subd. 1 provides: "the certificate of a notary public of the state, under his hand and seal of office, of the presentment by him for acceptance or payment, or of the protest, for non-acceptance or non-payment, of a promissory note or bill of exchange, or of the service of notice thereof, on a party to the note or bill; specifying the mode of giving the notice, the reputed place of residence of the party to whom it was given, and the post-office nearest thereto; is presumptive evidence of the facts certified, unless the party against whom it is offered has served upon the adverse party, with his pleading, or within ten days after joinder of an issue of fact, an original affidavit to the effect that he has not received notice of non-acceptance, or of non-payment of the note or bill. A verified answer is not sufficient as an affidavit within the meaning of this section."

(1) If the testimony of the notary public cannot be procured by reason of his death, insanity, or absence, his original protest, duly proved to be genuine, or a personal memorandum made or signed by him at the foot of the protest, or in his official register, is presumptive evidence "that a notice of non-acceptance or non-payment was sent or delivered at the time, and in the manner stated in the note or memorandum." C. P. A. 368, subd. 2. See also C. P. A. 

369 for proof of presentment and protest of foreign bills. g. Acknowledged, Proved or Certified Instrument: 

C. P. A. 386 provides: "any instrument, except a promissory note, a bill of exchange, or a last will, may be acknowledged or proved, and certified, in the manner prescribed by law for taking and certifying the acknowledgment of proof of a conveyance of real property; and thereupon, it is evidence as if it was a conveyance of real property."

II. Private Records: 

A. Generally: Any writing, not a public record or official document, is a private record. The admissibility of such document is governed by the general rules of evidence. 

If otherwise competent, it may be introduced in evidence after due authentication.

B. Attested and Unattested Writings: 

1. Attested Writings: An attested document is one subscribed by a witness to the execution, at the request, or with the consent, of the parties executing it. At common law, the execution of an attested writing had to be proved by the testimony of the subscribing witness, or his failure to testify had to be properly explained. 22 C. J. p. 936, note 60. In

New York, however, even though a writing is attested, its execution may be proved like an unattested instrument, unless, for the validity of such instrument, subscribing witnesses are necessary, as in the case of a will. C. P. A. 331. 

2. Unattested Writings: The execution of unattested writings may be shown by the testimony of any wit-

ness. Usually, it is proved by establishing the handwriting of the maker. U. S. v. Moreno, 1 Wall 400.

But it may be proved by a witness who saw him sign, or by any other competent, circumstantial evidence.

C. Instruments Executed Under Power: Where a person executes an instrument as the representative of another, proof of his authority may be required. Bowen, 20 

Johns. 338. Where an officer signs a corporate instrument, and the corporate seal is affixed thereto, that is usually sufficient proof of his authority. Quackenboss, 78 N. Y. S. 1019. If the seal is missing, authority to sign must be proved. Smith, 114 N. Y. S. 867.

D. Corporation Records: The acts and doings of a private corporation may be proved from its books which "are received in evidence, generally, to prove corporate acts of a corporation such as, its incorporation, its list of stockholders, its by-laws, the formal proceedings of its board of directors and its financial condition when its solvency comes in question." Rudd, 126 N. Y. 113. (The corporate minute books, if available, are the best evidence of a corporate act. Manhattan Co., 1 N. Y. S. (2) 215). In the absence of a permissive statute, however, corporate books are inadmissible for the purpose of proving a claim, in favor of the corporation or its members, against a stranger. Thayer, 121 N. Y. S. 

1064. But, such books are admissible in actions between the corporation and its members (Wesp, 120 N. Y. S. 

976) and even as against strangers to prove the things quoted above from the Rudd case. 

1. Method of Proof: The ordinary method of proving a corporate record is by producing the record and authenticating it by the sworn testimony of one who is acquainted with the entry, and the facts pertinent thereto, but not necessarily the entrant himself. 

Davis, 164 N. Y. S. 65. a. Entries in books of a foreign corporation may be proved by sworn copies. C. P. A. sec. 374. See Sigua Iron Co., 171 N. Y. 488.

E. Diagrams and Maps: In connection with the testimony

of a witness, diagrams, and maps are often admitted. 

Clegg, 159 N. Y. 550 (where diagram describing the scene of an accident was held admissible in negligence action). Personal testimony that the map or diagram is correct is required to establish admissibility. 

F. Standard Statistical Tables: Standard mortality tables, certain scientific tables, etc., are admissible as evidence of the data they contain. Hartley, 222 N. Y. 178. But standard books or treatises on art or science, etc., are not admissible. Pahl, 81 N. Y. S. 46. 

G. Photographs: Are admissible in evidence whenever they are competent to portray the physical characteristics of some person or thing. Peo. v. Webster, 139 

N. Y. 73. But such photographs must be authenticated by a witness familiar with the subject portrayed who is required to testify that the picture is a correct representation of the person, place or thing described as of a given time. Alberti, 118 N. Y. 77; Cowley v. Peo., 83 N. Y. 464. See Cahill, 233 N. Y. 255.

SUPPLEMENT BERKMAN'S EVIDENCE 1940 EDITION TRANSACTIONS WITH DECEDENTS: 

C.P.A. 347, amended, as of September, 1940, so as to provide: Nothing contained in this section, however, shall render a person incompetent to testify as to the facts of an accident or the results therefrom where the proceeding, hearing defense or cause of action involves a claim of negligence or contributory negligence in an action wherein one or more parties is the representative of a deceased or incompetent person based upon, or by reason of, the operation or ownership of a motor vehicle being operated upon the highways of the state, but this provision shall not be construed as permitting testimony as to conversations with the deceased. (see p. 137, text)

BIRTH OR DEATH CERTIFICATES: 

Public Health Law, Sec. 391, as amended in 1940, now provides that a copy of a birth or death record certified by the State Commissioner of Health shall be prima facie evidence in all courts and places as to the facts stated therein. (see pp. 168-9, text) cf
